∎

The next proposition is straightforward to show.

Proposition 2.7.3
.

Let
(A,i_{A}^{X},f_{A}^{Y}),(B,i_{B}^{X},f_{B}^{X})\in\mathcal{F}(X,Y)
A
X
B
Y
.
i_{A}^{X}
i_{B}^{X}
f_{A}^{Y}\ \
\ f_{B}^{Y}

Their left
f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{l}f_{B}^{Y}
and right
intersection

f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{r}f_{B}^{Y}
are the partial functions

f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{l}f_{B}^{Y}:=\bigg(A\cap B,\ i_{A\cap B}^{X},\ \big(f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{l}f_{B}^{Y}\big)_{A\cap B}^{Y}\bigg),\ \ \ \mbox{where}

\big(f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{l}f_{B}^{Y}\big)_{A\cap B}^{Y}(a,b):=f_{A}^{Y}(a);\ \ \ \ (a,b)\in A\cap B,\ \ \ \ \mbox{and}

f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{r}f_{B}^{Y}:=\bigg(A\cap B,\ i_{A\cap B}^{X},\ \big(f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{r}f_{B}^{Y}\big)_{A\cap B}^{Y}\bigg),\ \ \ \mbox{where}

\big(f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{r}f_{B}^{Y}\big)_{A\cap B}^{Y}(a,b):=f_{B}^{Y}(b);\ \ \ \ (a,b)\in A\cap B.

Their union
f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}
is the partial function

f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}:=\bigg(A\cup B,\ i_{A\cup B}^{X},\ \big(f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}\big)_{A\cup B}^{Y}\bigg),\ \ \ \mbox{where}

\big(f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}\big)_{A\cup B}^{Y}(z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f_{A}^{Y}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in A$}\\
f_{B}^{Y}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in B$.}\end{array}\right.

(i)

f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{l}f_{B}^{Y}\leq f_{A}^{Y}
and
f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{r}f_{B}^{Y}\leq f_{B}^{Y}
.

(ii)

If
f_{A}^{Y}(a)=_{Y}f_{B}^{Y}(b)
, for every
(a,b)\in A\cap B
, then
f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{l}f_{B}^{Y}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,Y)}f_{A}^{Y}\cap_{r}f_{B}^{Y}
.

(iii)

f_{A}^{Y}\leq f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}
and
f_{B}^{Y}\leq f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}
.

(iv)

f_{A}^{Y}\cup f_{B}^{Y}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,Y)}f_{B}^{Y}\cup f_{A}^{Y}
.

Definition 2.7.4
.

Let the operation of multiplication on
\mathbb{2}
, defined by
0\cdot 1:=1\cdot 0:=0\cdot 0:=0
and
1\cdot 1:=1
.
If
(A,i_{A}^{X},f_{A}^{\mathbb{2}}),(B,i_{B}^{X},g_{B}^{\mathbb{2}})\in\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
, let

f_{A}\cdot g_{B}:=\big(A\cap B,i_{A\cap B}^{X},(f_{A}\cdot g_{B})_{A\cap B}^{\mathbb{2}}\big),

where
(f_{A}\cdot g_{B})_{A\cap B}^{\mathbb{2}}\colon A\cap B\to\mathbb{2}
is defined, for every
(a,b)\in A\cap B
,
by

(f_{A}\cdot g_{B})_{A\cap B}^{\mathbb{2}}(a,b):=f_{A}^{\mathbb{2}}(a)\cdot g_{B}^{\mathbb{2}}(b).

By the equality of the product on
A\cap B
, it is immediate to show that the operation

(f_{A}\cdot g_{B})_{A\cap B}^{\mathbb{2}}
is a function. More generally, operations on
Y
induce
operations on
\mathcal{F}(X,Y)
. The above example with
Y:=\mathbb{2}
is useful to the next section.

2.8
Complemented subsets

An inequality on a set
X
induces a positively defined notion of disjointness of subsets of
X
.

Definition 2.8.1
.

Let
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
be a set, and
(A,i_{A}^{X}),(B,i_{B}^{X})\subseteq X
. We say that

A
and
B
are disjoint with respect to
\neq_{X}
, in symbols

A\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{{\neq_{X}}}B
, if

A\underset{{{{\neq_{X}}}}}{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}B:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{a\in A}\forall_{b\in B}\big(i_{A}^{X}(a)\neq_{X}i_{B}^{X}(b)\big).

If
\neq_{X}
is clear from the context, we only write
A\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}B
.

Clearly, if
A\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}B
, then
A\cap B
is not inhabited.
The positive disjointness of subsets of
X
induces the notion of a complemented subset of
X
, and
the negative notion of the complement of a set is avoided. We use bold letters to denote a complemented subset of a set.

Definition 2.8.2
.

A complemented subset of a set
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
is a pair

\bm{A}:=(A^{1},A^{0})
,
where
(A^{1},i_{A^{1}}^{X})
and
(A^{0},i_{A^{0}}^{X})
are subsets of
X
such that
A^{1}\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}A^{0}
. We
call
A^{1}
the
1
-component of
\bm{A}
and
A^{0}
the

0
-component of
\bm{A}
. If

\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}):=A^{1}\cup A^{0}
is the domain of
\bm{A}
,
the
indicator function, or
characteristic function, of

\bm{A}
is the operation
\chi_{\bm{A}}:\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{2}
defined by

\chi_{\bm{A}}(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{, $x\in A^{1}$}\\
0&\mbox{, $x\in A^{0}$.}\end{array}\right.

Let
x\in\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}
and
x\notin\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}
. If
\bm{A},\bm{B}
are complemented subsets of
X
, let

\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow A^{1}\subseteq B^{1}\ \&\ B^{0}\subseteq A^{0}.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
be their totality,
equipped with the equality

\bm{A}=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)}\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{B}\ \&\ \bm{B}\subseteq\bm{A}
.
Let
\textnormal{{PrfEql}}_{0}(\bm{A},\bm{B}):=\textnormal{{PrfEql}}_{0}(A^{1},B^{1})\times\textnormal{{PrfEql}}_{0}(A^{0},B^{0})
. A map
\bm{f}\colon\bm{A}\to\bm{B}
from
\bm{A}

to
\bm{B}
is a pair
(f^{1},f^{0})
, where
f^{1}\colon A^{1}\to B^{1}
and

f^{0}\colon A^{0}\to B^{0}
.

Clearly,
\bm{A}=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)}\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow A^{1}=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}B^{1}\ \&\ A^{0}=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}B^{0}
,
and
\textnormal{{PrfEql}}_{0}(\bm{A},\bm{B})
is a subsingleton, as the product of subsingletons.
Since the membership condition for
\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)
requires quantification over
\mathbb{V}_{0}
, the
totality
\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)
is a class. The operation

\chi_{\bm{A}}
is a function, actually,
\chi_{\bm{A}}
is a partial function in
\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
.
Let
z,w\in A^{1}\cup A^{0}
such that
z=_{A^{1}\cup A^{0}}w
i.e.,

\left.\begin{array}[]{lll}i_{A^{1}}^{X}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in A^{1}$}\\
\\
i_{A^{0}}^{X}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in A^{0}$}\end{array}\right\}:=i_{A^{1}\cup A^{0}}^{X}(z)=_{X}i_{A^{1}\cup A^{0}}^{X}(w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}i_{A^{1}}^{X}(w)&\mbox{, $w\in A^{1}$}\\
\\
i_{A^{0}}^{X}(w)&\mbox{, $w\in A^{0}$.}\end{array}\right.

Let
z\in A^{1}
. If
w\in A^{0}
, then
i_{A^{1}}^{X}(z):=i_{A^{1}\cup A^{0}}^{X}(z)=_{X}i_{A^{1}\cup A^{0}}^{X}(w):=i_{A^{0}}^{X}(w)

i.e.,
(z,w)\in A^{1}\cap A^{0}
, which contradicts the hypothesis
A^{1}\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}A^{0}
. Hence
w\in A^{1}
, and

\chi_{\bm{A}}(z)=\chi_{\bm{A}}(w)
. If
z\in A^{0}
, we proceed similarly.

Definition 2.8.3
.

If
(X,=_{X})
is a set, let the inequality on
X
defined by

x\neq_{X}^{{\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}}x{{}^{\prime}}:\Leftrightarrow\exists_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}\big(f(x)=_{{\mathbb{2}}}1\ \&\ f(x{{}^{\prime}})=_{{\mathbb{2}}}0\big)

If
f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
, the following extensional subsets of
X

\delta_{0}^{1}(f):=\{x\in X\mid f(x)=_{{\mathbb{2}}}1\},

\delta_{0}^{0}(f):=\{x\in X\mid f(x)=_{{\mathbb{2}}}0\},

are called detachable, or free subsets of
X
. Let also
their pair
\bm{\delta}(f):=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f),\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\big)
.

Clearly,

x\neq_{X}^{{\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}}x{{}^{\prime}}\Leftrightarrow\exists_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}\big(f(x)\neq_{\mathbb{2}}f(x{{}^{\prime}})\big)
,
and
\bm{\delta}(f)
is a complemented subset of
X
with respect to the inequality
\neq_{X}^{{\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}}
.
The characteristic function
\chi_{\bm{\delta}(f)}
of
\bm{\delta}(f)
is definitionally equal to
f
(recall that

f(x)=_{{\mathbb{2}}}1:\Leftrightarrow f(x):=1
), and
\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{0}(f)=X
.

Definition 2.8.4
.

If
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
and
\bm{C}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(Y)
,
let

\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}:=(A^{1}\cup B^{1},A^{0}\cap B^{0}),

\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}:=(A^{1}\cap B^{1},A^{0}\cup B^{0}),

-\bm{A}:=(A^{0},A^{1}),

\bm{A}-\bm{B}:=(A^{1}\cap B^{0},A^{0}\cup B^{1}),

\bm{A}\times\bm{C}:=\big(A^{1}\times C^{1},\ [A^{0}\times Y]\cup[X\times C^{0}]\big).

The following diagrams depict
\bm{A}\cup\bm{B},\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}
,
\bm{A}-\bm{B}
, and
\bm{A}\times\bm{C}
, respectively.

A^{1}
B^{1}
A^{0}
B^{0}

B^{1}
A^{0}
B^{0}

B^{1}
A^{0}

Remark 2.8.5
.

If
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)
and
\bm{C}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(Y)
, then

\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}
,
\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}
,
-\bm{A}
, and
\bm{A}-\bm{B}
are in
\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)

and
\bm{A}\times\bm{C}
is in
\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X\times Y)
.

Proof.

We show only the last membership. If
(a_{1},b_{1})\in A^{1}\times B^{1}
and
(a_{0},b_{0})\in A^{0}\times B^{0}
,
then
i_{A^{1}}^{X}(a_{1})\neq_{X}i_{A^{0}}^{X}(a_{0})
and
i_{B^{1}}^{Y}(b_{1})\neq_{Y}i_{B^{0}}^{Y}(b_{0})
. By definition

i_{A^{1}\times B^{1}}^{X\times Y}(a_{1},b_{1}):=\big(i_{A^{1}}^{X}(a_{1}),i_{B^{1}}^{Y}(b_{1})\big).

If
(a_{0},y)\in A^{0}\times Y
, then
(i_{A^{0}}^{X}\times\mathrm{id}_{Y})(a_{0},y):=(i_{A^{0}}^{X}(a_{0}),y)
, and if

(x,b_{0})\in X\times B^{0}
, then
(\mathrm{id}_{X}\times i_{B^{0}}^{Y})(x,b_{0}):=(x,i_{B^{0}}^{Y}(b_{0}))
. In both
cases we get the required inequality.
∎

Remark 2.8.6
.

Let
\bm{A},\bm{B}
and
\bm{C}
be in
\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)
. The following hold:

(i)

-(-\bm{A}):=\bm{A}
.

(ii)

-(\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}):=(-\bm{A})\cap(-\bm{B})
.

(iii)

-(\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}):=(-\bm{A})\cup(-\bm{B})
.

(iv)

\bm{A}\cup(\bm{B}\cap\bm{C})=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)}(\bm{A}\cup\bm{B})\cap(\bm{A}\cup\bm{C})
.

(v)

\bm{A}\cap(\bm{B}\cup\bm{C})=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)}(\bm{A}\cap\bm{B})\cup(\bm{A}\cap\bm{C})
.

(vi)

\bm{A}-\bm{B}:=\bm{A}\cap(-\bm{B})
.

(vii)

\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow(\bm{A}\cap\bm{B})=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)}\bm{A}
.

(viii)

\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow-\bm{B}\subseteq-\bm{A}
.

(ix)

If
\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{B}
and
\bm{B}\subseteq\bm{C}
, then
\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{C}
.

Proposition 2.8.7
.

Let
\bm{A}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X)
and
B,C\in\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(Y)
.

(i)

\bm{A}\times(\bm{B}\cup\bm{C})=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X\times Y)}(\bm{A}\times\bm{B})\cup(\bm{A}\times\bm{C})
.

(ii)

\bm{A}\times(\bm{B}\cap\bm{C})=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X\times Y)}(\bm{A}\times\bm{B})\cap(\bm{A}\times\bm{C})
.

Proof.

We prove only (i). We have that

\displaystyle\bm{A}\times(\bm{B}\cup\bm{C})

\displaystyle:=(A^{1},A^{0})\times(B^{1}\cup C^{1},B^{0}\cap C^{0})

\displaystyle:=\big(A^{1}\times(B^{1}\cup C^{1}),(A^{0}\times Y)\cup[X\times(B^{0}\cap C^{0})]\big)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(X\times Y)}\big((A^{1}\times B^{1})\cup(A^{1}\times C^{1}),[(A^{0}\times Y)\cup(X\times B^{0})]\ \cap

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \cap[(A^{0}\times Y)\cup(X\times C^{0})]\big)

\displaystyle:=(\bm{A}\times\bm{B})\times(\bm{A}\times\bm{C}).\qed

Proposition 2.8.8
.

Let the sets
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X}^{f})
and
(Y,=_{Y},\neq_{Y})
, where
f\colon X\to Y

(
see
Remark
2.3.3
)
.
Let also
\bm{A}:=(A^{1},A^{0})
and
\bm{B}:=(B^{1},B^{0})
in
\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(Y)
.

(i)

f^{-1}(\bm{A}):=\big(f^{-1}(A^{1}),f^{-1}(A^{0})\big)\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
.

(ii)

f^{-1}(\bm{A}\cup\bm{B})=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}f^{-1}(\bm{A})\cup f^{-1}(\bm{B})
.

(iii)

f^{-1}(\bm{A}\cap\bm{B})=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}f^{-1}(\bm{A})\cap f^{-1}(\bm{B})
.

(iv)

f^{-1}(-\bm{A})=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}-f^{-1}(\bm{A})
.

(v)

f^{-1}(\bm{A}-\bm{B})=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}f^{-1}(\bm{A})-f^{-1}(\bm{B})
.

Proof.

(i) By Definition
2.6.9
we have that

f^{-1}(A^{1}):=\{(x,a_{1})\in X\times A^{1}\mid f(x)=_{Y}i_{A^{1}}^{X}(a_{1})\},\ \ \ \ i_{{f^{-1}(A^{1})}}^{X}(x,a_{1}):=x,

f^{-1}(A^{0}):=\big(\{(x,a_{0})\in X\times A^{0}\mid f(x)=_{Y}i_{A^{0}}^{X}(a_{0})\},\ \ \ \ i_{{f^{-1}(A^{0})}}^{X}(x,a_{0}):=x.

Let
(x,a_{1})\in f^{-1}(A^{1})
and
(z,a_{0})\in f^{-1}(A^{0})
. By the extensionality of
\neq_{Y}
we have that

i_{{f^{-1}(A^{1})}}^{X}(x,a_{1})\neq_{X}^{f}i_{{f^{-1}(A^{0})}}^{X}(z,a_{0}):\Leftrightarrow x\neq_{X}^{f}z:\Leftrightarrow f(x)\neq_{Y}f(z)\Leftrightarrow i_{A^{1}}^{X}(a_{1})\neq_{Y}i_{A^{0}}^{X}(a_{0}),

and the last inequality holds by the hypothesis
\bm{A}\in\mathcal{P}^{{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}}(Y)
.
Next we show only (ii):

\displaystyle f^{-1}(\bm{A}\cup\bm{B})

\displaystyle:=f^{-1}\big(A^{1}\cup B^{1},A^{0}\cap B^{0}\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(f^{-1}(A^{1}\cup B^{1}),f^{-1}(A^{0}\cap B^{0})\big)

\displaystyle=\big(f^{-1}(A^{1})\cup f^{-1}(B^{1}),f^{-1}(A^{0})\cap f^{-1}(B^{0})\big)

\displaystyle:=f^{-1}(\bm{A})\cup f^{-1}(\bm{B}).\qed

Alternatively, one can define the following operations between complemented subsets.

Definition 2.8.9
.

If
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
and
\bm{C}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(Y)
, let

\bm{A}\vee\bm{B}:=\big([A^{1}\cap B^{1}]\cup[A^{1}\cap B^{0}]\cup[A^{0}\cap B^{1}],\ A^{0}\cap B^{0}\big),

\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}:=\big(A^{1}\cap B^{1},\ [A^{1}\cap B^{0}]\cup[A^{0}\cap B^{1}]\cup[A^{0}\cap B^{0}]\big),

\bm{A}\ominus\bm{B}:=\bm{A}\wedge(-\bm{B}),

\bm{A}\otimes\bm{C}:=\big(A^{1}\times C^{1},\ [A^{1}\times C^{0}]\cup[A^{0}\times C^{1}]\cup[A^{0}\times C^{0}]\big),

The following diagrams depict
\bm{A}\vee\bm{B},\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}
,
\bm{A}\ominus\bm{B}
, and
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{C}
,
respectively.

A^{1}
B^{1}
A^{0}
B^{0}

A^{1}
B^{1}
A^{0}
B^{0}

A^{1}
B^{1}
A^{0}
B^{0}

Y
X
A^{1}
A^{0}
C^{1}
C^{0}

With the previous definitions the corresponding characteristic functions are expressed through the characteristic
functions of

\bm{A}
and
\bm{B}
.

Remark 2.8.10
.

If
\bm{A},\bm{B}
are complemented subsets of
X
, then
\bm{A}\vee\bm{B},\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B},\bm{A}-\bm{B}

and
-\bm{A}
are complemented subsets of
X
with characteristic functions

\chi_{\bm{A}\vee\bm{B}}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}\chi_{\bm{A}}\vee\chi_{\bm{B}},\ \ \chi_{\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}\chi_{\bm{A}}\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}},\ \ \chi_{\bm{A}-\bm{B}}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}\chi_{\bm{A}}(1-\chi_{\bm{B}}),\ \ \

\chi_{\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}}(x,y)=_{\mathcal{F}(X\times X,\mathbb{2})}\chi_{\bm{A}}(x)\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}}(y),\ \ \ \chi_{-\bm{A}}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}1-\chi_{\bm{A}}.

Proof.

We show only the equality
\chi_{\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}}=_{\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}\chi_{\bm{A}}\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}}
. By Definition
2.7.4
the multiplication of the partial maps
\chi_{\bm{A}}\colon\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\to\mathbb{2}
and
\chi_{\bm{B}}\colon\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})\to\mathbb{2}
is
the partial function

\chi_{\bm{A}}\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}}:=\big(\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B}),i_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})}^{X},(\chi_{\bm{A}}\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}})_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})}^{\mathbb{2}}\big),

(\chi_{\bm{A}}\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}})_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})}^{\mathbb{2}}(u,w):=\chi_{\bm{A}}(u)\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}}(w),

for every
(u,w)\in\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})
. The partial function
\chi_{\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}}
is the triplet

\chi_{\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}}:=\big(\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}),i_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})}^{X},(\chi_{\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}})_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})}^{\mathbb{2}}\big).

Since
\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})
, and if
(f,g)\colon\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})
, it is straightforward to show that also the following outer diagram commutes

\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})
\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})
X
\mathbb{2}
f
g
(\chi_{\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}})_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})}^{\mathbb{2}}\ \
\ \ (\chi_{\bm{A}}\cdot\chi_{\bm{B}})_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})}^{\mathbb{2}}
\ i_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B})}^{X}
i_{\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A})\cap\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{B})}^{X}\

and hence the two partial functions are equal in
\mathcal{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
.
∎

2.9
Notes

Note 2.9.1
.

In
[
55
]
Greenleaf introduced predicates on objects through the totality
\Omega
of propositions
and then he defined
\mathcal{P}(X)
as
\mathbb{F}(X,\Omega)
. A similar treatment of the powerset
\mathcal{P}(X)
is found
in
[
115
]
. For us a predicate on a set
X
is a bounded formula
P(x)
with
x
as a free variable.
In order to define new objects from
X
through
P
we ask
P
to be extensional.

Note 2.9.2
.

In
[
27
]
, pp. 114-5, Cantor described a set as follows:

A manifold (a sum, a set) of elements belonging to some conceptual
sphere is called well-defined if, on the basis of its definition and in
accordance with the logical principle of the excluded third, it must
be regarded as internally determined, both whether any object of
that conceptual sphere belongs as an element to the mentioned set,
and also whether two objects belonging to the set, in spite of formal
differences in the mode of givenness, are equal to each other or not.

Bishop’s intuitive notion of set is similar to Cantor’s, except that he does not invoke
the principle of the excluded middle (
\mathrm{PEM}
). As it was pointed to me by W. Sieg, Dedekind’s
primitive notions in
[
44
]
were “systems” and “transformations of systems”.
Notice that here we study defined totalities that are not defined inductively.
The inductively defined sets are expected to be studied in a future work within an extension
\mathrm{BST}^{*}
of
\mathrm{BST}
.

Note 2.9.3
.

Although
{\mathbb{N}}
is the only primitive set considered in
\mathrm{BST}
, one
could, in principle, add more primitive sets. E.g., a primitive set of Booleans, of integers, and,
more interestingly, a primitive continuous interval, or a primitive real line (see
[
23
]
for an axiomatic
treatment of the set
{\mathbb{R}}
of reals within
\mathrm{BISH}
).

Note 2.9.4
.

In Martin-Löf type theory the definitional, or judgemental equality
a:=b
, where
a,b
are terms
of some type
A
, is never used in a formula. We permit the use of the
definitional equality
:=
for membership conditions only.
In the membership condition for the product we use the primitive notion of a pair.
The membership condition for an extensional subset
X_{P}
of
X
implies that an object
x
“has not unique typing”,
as it can be an element of more than one sets.

Note 2.9.5
.

The positively defined notion of discrete set used here comes from
[
76
]
, p. 9. There it is also mentioned that
a set without a specified inequality i.e., a pair
(X,=_{X})
, is discrete, if

\forall_{x,y\in X}\big(x=_{X}y\ \vee\ \neg(x=_{X}y)\big)
.
In
[
84
]
it is mentioned that the above discreteness of
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}})
implies
the non-constructive principle “weak
\mathrm{LPO}
”

\forall_{f\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}})}\bigg(\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(f(n)=_{{\mathbb{N}}}0\big)\ \vee\ \neg\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(f(n)=_{{\mathbb{N}}}0\big)\bigg).

Because of a result of Bauer and Swan in
[
4
]
, we cannot show in
\mathrm{BISH}
the existence of an
uncountable separable metric space, hence, using the discrete metric, the existence of an uncountable
discrete set. Note that in
[
9
]
, p. 66, a set
S
is called discrete, if the set

D:=\{(s,t)\in S\times S\mid s=_{S}t\}
is a free
, or a detachable
subset of
S\times S
.
In Definition
2.2.4
we use the symbol
D(S)
for
D
and we call it the diagonal of
S
. We employ here the diagonal of a set in the fundamental definition of a set-indexed family of sets (Definition
3.1.1
).

Note 2.9.6
.

In
[
9
]
and
[
19
]
, the negation
\neg\phi
of a formula
\phi
is not mentioned explicitly. E.g., the exact
writing of condition
(\mathrm{Ap}_{1})
in Definition
2.2.5
is “if
x=_{X}y
and
x\neq_{X}y
, then
0=_{{\mathbb{N}}}1
”.
Similarly, the condition of tightness in Definition
2.2.5
is written as follows:
“if
x\neq_{X}y
entails
0=1
, then
x=_{X}y
”. hence, if
\neq_{X}
is tight, the implication
x\neq_{X}y\Rightarrow 0=_{{\mathbb{N}}}1
is logically equivalent to the (positively defined, if
X
is a defined totality) equality
x=_{X}y
.
Within intuitionistic logic one defines
\neg\phi:=\phi\Rightarrow\bot
.

Note 2.9.7
.

The definitions of
(-2)
-sets and
(-1)
-sets are proof-irrelevant translations of the corresponding notions in
\mathrm{HoTT}
,
which were introduced by Voevodsky (see
[
126
]
). The definition of a
0
-
set
requires to determine a set

\textnormal{{PrfEql}}_{0}^{X}(x,y)
of witnesses of the equality
x=_{X}y
. This is done in a universal way in
\mathrm{MLTT}
, while in
\mathrm{BST}

in a “local” way, and by definition (see Definition
).

Note 2.9.8
.

In the literature of constructive mathematics (see e.g.,
[
7
]
, pp. 34–35)
the term
preset
is used for a totality.
Also, the term
operation
is used for a non-dependent assignment routine from a totality
X
to a totality
Y

(see
[
7
]
, p. 44), while we use it only for a non-dependent assignment routine from a set
X
to a set
Y
.

Note 2.9.9
.

The notion of uniqueness associated to the definition of a function is
local
, in the following sense:
if
f\colon X\to Y
, it is immediate to show that
\forall_{x\in X}\exists!_{y\in Y}\big(f(x)=_{Y}y\big)
.
The converse is the local version of Myhill’s axiom of non-choice
(\mathrm{LANC})
. Let
P(x,y)
be an extensional property on

X\times Y
i.e.,
\forall_{x,x{{}^{\prime}},y,y{{}^{\prime}}\in X}\big([x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ P(x,y)]\Rightarrow P(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})\big)
. The principle

(\mathrm{LANC})
is the formula

\forall_{x\in X}\exists!_{y\in Y}P(x,y)\Rightarrow\exists_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}\forall_{x\in X}\big(P(x,f(x))\big).

Notice that
\mathrm{LANC}
provides the existence of a function for which we only know how its outputs behave with
respect to the equality of
Y
, and it gives no information on how
f
behaves definitionally.
If we define
Q_{x}(y):=P(x,y)
, then if we suppose
Q_{x}(f(x))
and
Q_{x}(g(x))
, for some
f,g\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
, we get
f(x)=_{Y}y=_{Y}g(x)
, and then
(\mathrm{LANC})
implies

\forall_{x\in X}\exists!_{y\in Y}P(x,y)\Rightarrow\exists!_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}\forall_{x\in X}\big(P(x,f(x))\big).

We can use
(\mathrm{LANC})
to view an arbitrary subset
(A,i_{A}^{X})
of
X
as an extensional subset of
X
.
If
(A,i_{A}^{X})\in\mathcal{P}(X)
, then the property
P_{A}
on
X
defined by

P_{A}(x):=\exists_{a\in A}\big(i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}x\big)
,
is extensional, and
(i_{A}^{X},j_{A}^{X}):X_{P_{A}}=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}(A,i_{A}^{X})
, for some function
j_{A}^{X}\colon X_{P_{A}}\to A
.
To show this, let
x,y\in X
such that
P_{A}(x)
and
x=_{X}y
. By transitivity of
=_{X}
, if
i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}x
, then

i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}y
. If
x\in X
and
a,b\in A
such that
i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}x=_{X}i_{A}^{X}(b)
, then
a=_{A}b
i.e.,

\forall_{x\in X_{P_{A}}}\exists!_{a\in A}\big(i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}x\big)
,
and since the property
Q(x,a):\Leftrightarrow i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}x
is extensional on
X_{P_{A}}\times A
, by
(\mathrm{LANC})

there is a (unique) function
j_{A}^{X}:X_{P_{A}}\to A
, such that for every
x\in X_{P}
we have that

i_{A}^{X}(j_{A}^{X}(x))=_{X}x
, and the required diagram commutes.
The principle
(\mathrm{LANC})
, which is also considered in
[
5
]
, is included in Myhill’s system
\mathrm{CST}
(see
[
80
]
)
as a principle of generating functions. This is in contrast to Bishop’s algorithmic approach to the concept of
function.

Note 2.9.10
.

In
[
19
]
, p. 67, a function
f:A\to B
is defined as a finite routine which,
applied to
any
element of
A
, produces an element
b\equiv f(a)
of
B
, such that
f(a)=_{B}f(a{{}^{\prime}})
,
whenever
a=_{A}a{{}^{\prime}}
. In
[
19
]
, p. 15, we read that
f
“affords an explicit, finite mechanical reduction
of the procedure for
constructing
f(a)
to the procedure for constructing
a
”.
The pattern of defining a function
f\colon X\to Y
by first defining an operation
f\colon X\rightsquigarrow Y
, and then proving that

f
is a function, is implicit in the more elementary parts of
[
9
]
and
[
19
]
, and more explicit
in the later parts of the books. E.g., in
[
19
]
, p. 199, an inhabited subset
U
of

\mathbb{C}

has the maximal extent property
, if there
is an operation
\mu
from
U
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
satisfying certain properties. One can show
afterwords

that
U
is open and
\mu
is a function on
U
. This property is used in Bishop’s proof
of the Riemann mapping theorem (see
[
19
]
, pp. 209–210).

Note 2.9.11
.

Regarding the set-character of
\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
, Bishop, in
[
19
]
, p. 67, writes:

When
X
is not countable, the set
\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
seems to have little practical interest, because
to get a hold on its structure is too hard. For instance, it has been asserted by Brouwer that all
functions in
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}})
are continuous, but no acceptable proof of this assertion is known.

Similar problems occur though, in function spaces where the domain of the functions is
a countable set. E.g., we cannot accept constructively (i.e., in the sense of Bishop) that the Cantor
space
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},2)
satisfies Markov’s principle, but no one that we know of has doubted the
set-character of
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},2)
. The possibility of doubting the set-character of the Baire space
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}})

is discussed by Beeson in
[
7
]
, p. 46.

Note 2.9.12
.

In intensional Martin-Löf Type Theory the type

\bigg(\prod_{x\colon X}f(x)=g(x)\bigg)\to f=g

is not provable (inhabited), and its inhabitance is known as the

axiom of function extensionality

(\mathrm{FunExt})
.
In
\mathrm{BST}
this axiom is part of the canonical definition of the function space
\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
. Because of this,
many results in
\mathrm{MLTT}+\mathrm{FunExt}
are translatable in
\mathrm{BST}
(see Chapter

).

Note 2.9.13
.

The totality
\mathbb{V}_{0}
is not mentioned by Bishop, although it is necessary, if we want to formulate the
fundamental notion of a set-indexed family of sets.
The defined
equality on the universe
\mathbb{V}_{0}
expresses that
\mathbb{V}_{0}
is
univalent
, as isomorphic sets are
equal in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
. In univalent type theory, which is
\mathrm{MLTT}
extended with Voevodsky’s

axiom of univalence

UA

(see
[
126
]
), the existence of a pair of quasi-inverses between types
A
and
B
implies
that they are equivalent in Voevodsky’s sense, and by the univalence axiom, also propositionally equal.
The axiom
UA
is partially translated in
\mathrm{BST}
as the canonical definition of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.
Because of this, results in
\mathrm{MLTT}+\textnormal{{UA}}
that do not raise the level of the universe are translatable in
\mathrm{BST}
.
For example, Proposition
is lemma 4.9.2 in book HoTT
[
126
]
, where
UA
is used
in its proof: if
e\colon X\simeq Y
, then
Z\to X\simeq Z\to Y
, and by

UA
we get
e=\textnormal{{idtoEqv}}(p)
, for some
p\colon X=_{\mathcal{U}}Y
. Notice that in the
formulation of this lemma the universe-level is not raised.

Note 2.9.14
.

The notion of a dependent operation is explicitly mentioned by Bishop in
[
9
]
, p. 65, and
repeated in
[
19
]
, p. 70, in the definition of the intersection of a family of subsets of a set
indexed by some set

T
:

an element
u
of
\bigcap_{t\in T}\lambda(t)
is a finite routine which
associates an element
x_{t}
of
\lambda(t)
with each element
t
of
T
, such that

i_{t}(x_{t})=i_{t{{}^{\prime}}}(x_{t{{}^{\prime}}})
whenever
t,t{{}^{\prime}}\in T
.

This definition corresponds to our Definition
4.3.1
.

Note 2.9.15
.

Bishop’s definition of a subset of a set is related to the notion of a subobject in Category Theory
(see
[
3
]
, p. 89, and
[
54
]
, p. 75). In practice the subsets of a set
X
are defined through an
extensional property on
X
. In
[
20
]
, p. 7, this approach to the notion of a subset is considered
as its
definition
. Note
that there the implication
x=_{X}y\Rightarrow(P(y)\Rightarrow P(x))
is also included in
the definition of an extensional property, something which follows though, from the symmetry of
=_{X}
.
Such a form of separation axiom is used implicitly in
[
9
]
and in
[
19
]
. Myhill used in
his system
\mathrm{CST}
the axiom of bounded separation to implement the notion of an extensional subset of
X
.
This axiom is also
included in Aczel’s system
\mathrm{CZF}
(see
[
1
]
, p. 26).

Note 2.9.16
.

One could have defined the equality
=_{A\cup B}
without relying on the non-dependent assignment routine

i_{A\cup B}^{X}
. If we define first

z=_{A\cup B}w:\Leftrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllllll}i_{A}^{X}(z)=_{X}i_{A}(w)&\mbox{, $z,w\in A$}\\
\\
i_{A}^{X}(z)=_{X}i_{B}(w)&\mbox{, $z\in A\ \&\ w\in B$}\\
\\
i_{B}^{X}(z)=_{X}i_{B}(w)&\mbox{, $z,w\in B$}\\
\\
i_{B}^{X}(z)=_{X}i_{A}(w)&\mbox{, $z\in B\ \&\ w\in A$,}\end{array}\right.

we can define afterwords the
operation

i_{A\cup B}^{X}:A\cup B\to X
as in
Definition
2.6.5
.
In this way the non-dependent assignment routine
i_{A\cup B}^{X}
is defined on a set, and it is an operation.
Bishop avoids this definition, probably because this pattern cannot be extended to the definition of a union of a family of
subsets (see Definition
4.2.1
). In that case,
we cannot write down the corresponding case distinction for
z=_{A\cup B}w
. Moreover,
the proof of
\big(A\cup B,i_{A\cup B}^{X}\big)\subseteq X
is immediate, if one
uses Definition
2.6.5
.

Note 2.9.17
.

The definition of the empty subset
\emptyset_{X}
of a set
X
, given in
[
9
]
, p. 65, can be
formulated as follows. Let
X
be a set and
x_{0}\in X
. The totality
\emptyset_{X}
is defined by

z\in\emptyset_{X}:\Leftrightarrow x_{0}\in X\ \&\ 0=_{{\mathbb{N}}}1
.
Let
i_{\emptyset}^{X}\colon\emptyset_{X}\rightsquigarrow X
be the non-dependent assignment routine, defined by
i(z):=x_{0}
,
for every
z\in\emptyset_{X}
, and let
z=_{\emptyset_{X}}w:\Leftrightarrow i(z)=_{X}i(w):\Leftrightarrow x_{0}=_{X}x_{0}.

The pair
(\emptyset_{X},i_{\emptyset}^{X})
is the
empty subset
of
X
.
One can show that
=_{\emptyset_{X}}
is an equality on
\emptyset_{X}
, and hence
\emptyset_{X}
can be
considered to be a set. The assignment routine
i_{\emptyset}^{X}
is an embedding of
\emptyset_{X}
into
X
, and hence

(\emptyset_{X},i_{\emptyset}^{X})
is a subset of
X
. As Bishop himself writes in
[
9
]
, p. 65, “the definition
of
\emptyset
is negativistic, and we prefer to mention the void set as seldom as possible”.
In
[
19
]
, p. 69, Bishop and Bridges define two subsets
A,B
of
X
to be disjoint, when

A\cap B
“is the void subset of
X
”.
Clearly, this “is” cannot be
A\cap B:=\emptyset_{X}
. If we interpret it as

A\cap B=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\emptyset_{X}
, we need the existence of certain functions
from
\emptyset_{X}
to
A\cap B
and from
A\cap B
to
\emptyset_{X}
. The latter approach is
followed in
\mathrm{MLTT}
for the empty type. Following Bishop, we refrain from elaborating this negatively defined notion.

Note 2.9.18
.

If
(A,i_{A}^{X})\subseteq A
,
(B,i_{B}^{Y})\subseteq Y
, and
f\colon X\to Y
, the

extensional image

f[A]
of

A
under
f
is defined through the extensional property

P(y):=\exists_{a\in A}\big(f(i_{A}(a))=_{Y}y\big)
.
Similarly, the
extensional pre-image

f^{-1}[B]
of

B
under
f
is defined through the extensional property

Q(x):=\exists_{b\in B}\big(f(x)=_{Y}i_{B}(b)\big)
.
The subset
f(A)
of
Y
contains exactly the outputs
f(i_{A}^{X}(a))
of
f
, for every
a\in A
, while
the subset
f[A]
of
Y
contains all the elements of
Y
that are
=_{Y}
-equal to some output
f(i_{A}(a))

of
f
, for every
a\in A
. It is useful to keep the “distinction” between the subsets
f(A)
,
f[A]
, and

f^{-1}(B)
,
f^{-1}[B]
.
We need the equality in
\mathcal{P}(X)
of a subset of
X
to its extensional version (see Note
2.9.9
),
hence the principle
\mathrm{LANC}
, to get

f(A)=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}f[A]
and
f^{-1}(B)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}f^{-1}[B]
.

Note 2.9.19
.

There are instances in Bishop’s work indicating that the powerset of a set is treated as a set.
In
[
9
]
, p. 68, and in
[
19
]
, p. 74,
the following “function” is defined

j:\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)\to\mathcal{P}(X),\ \ \ \ (A^{1},A^{0})\mapsto A^{1}.

This is in complete contrast to our interpretation of a function as an operation between sets. Of course,
such a rule is an exception in
[
9
]
and
[
19
]
.
In the definition of an integration space, see
[
19
]
, p. 216, the “set”
\mathcal{F}(X,Y)
of all
strongly extensional partial functions from
X
to
Y
requires quantification over
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.
Such a quantification is also implicit in the definition of a measure space given in
[
19
]
, p. 282,
and in the definition of a complete measure space
in
[
19
]
, p. 289. These definitions appeared first in
[
18
]
, p. 47, and p. 55, respectively.
The powerset is repeatedly used as a set in
[
20
]
and
[
76
]
. It is not known if the treatment
of the powerset as a set implies some constructively unacceptable principle.

Note 2.9.20
.

There are instances in Bishop’s work indicating that the powerset of a set is
not
treated as a set.
See e.g., the definition of a set-indexed family of sets in
[
19
]
, p. 78 (our Definition
3.1.1
).
Similarly, in the definition of a family of subsets of a set
A
indexed
by some set
T
(see
[
19
]
, p. 69), the notion of a finite routine that assigns a
subset of
A
to an element of
T
is used, and not the notion of a function from
T
to
\mathcal{P}(A)
.
In the definition of a measure space in
[
9
]
, p. 183, a subfamily of a given family of
complemented sets is considered in order to avoid quantification over the class of all complemented subsets in the
formulations of the definitional clauses of a measure space (see Note
6.6.6
).
The powerset axiom is also avoided in Myhill’s formalization
[
80
]
of
\mathrm{BISH}
and in Aczel’s subsequent system

\mathrm{CZF}
of constructive set theory (see
[
1
]
). Although, as we said, it is not known if the use
of the powerset as a set implies some constructively unacceptable principle, it is not accepted in
any
predicative
development of constructive mathematics.

Note 2.9.21
.

The notion of a partial function was introduced by Bishop and Cheng in
[
18
]
, p. 1, and this definition,
together with the introduced term “partial function”, was also included in Chapter 3
of
[
19
]
, p. 71. The totality of partial functions
\mathcal{F}(X)
from a set
X
to
{\mathbb{R}}
is crucial to the
definition of an integration space in the new measure theory developed in
[
18
]
, and seriously
extended in
[
19
]
. Only the basic algebraic operations on
\mathcal{F}(X)
were defined
in
[
19
]
, p. 71. The composition of partial functions is mentioned in
[
39
]
,
pp. 66–67.
A notion of a partial dependent operation can be defined as follows. If
A,I
are sets, a partial dependent
operation
is a triplet
(A,i_{A}^{I},\Phi_{A}^{\lambda_{0}})
, where

(A,i_{A})\subseteq I
,
\lambda_{0}\colon A\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
, and
\Phi_{A}^{\lambda_{0}}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{a\in A}\lambda_{0}(a)
. If
\lambda_{0}(a):=Y
, for every
a\in A
, then the corresponding
partial dependent operation is reduced to a partial function in
\mathcal{F}(I,Y)
.

Note 2.9.22
.

In the study of various subsets of a set
X
we avoided to define the complement of a subset,
since this requires a negative definition. Recall that the negatively defined notion of empty subset of a set
is not really used. In
[
9
]
Bishop introduced a
positive notion of the complement of a subset of a set
X
, the notion of a complemented subset of
X
. For
its definition we need a notion of a fixed inequality on
X
, which is compatible with the given equality of

X
. In this way we can express the disjointness of two subsets
A,B
of a set
X
in a positive way. Usually,

A,B
are called
disjoint
, if
A\cap B
is not inhabited.
It is computationally more informative though, if a positive way is found to express disjointness of subsets.
In
[
25
]
a positive notion of
apartness
is used as a foundation of constructive topology.

Note 2.9.23
.

The definitions of
\bm{A}\cap\bm{B},\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}
and
\bm{A}-\bm{B}
appear in
[
9
]
, p. 66,
where
\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}
and
\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}
are special cases of the complemented subsets

\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
and
\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
, respectively (see Proposition
4.9.2
). There the inequality on
X
is induced by an inhabited set of functions from
X
to
{\mathbb{R}}
. The
definition of
\bm{A}\times\bm{C}
appears in
[
9
]
, p. 206, in the section of the product measures.
One can motivate these definitions applying a “classical” thinking. If
x\in X
, recall the definitions

x\in\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\ \ \ \&\ \ \ x\notin\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}.

Interpreting the connectives in a classical way, we get

x\in\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow x\in\bm{A}\ \vee\ x\in\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\ \vee\ x\in B^{1}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\cup B^{1},

x\notin\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow x\notin\bm{A}\ \&\ x\notin\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}\ \&\ x\in B^{1}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\cap B^{1},

x\in\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow x\in\bm{A}\ \&\ x\in\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\ \&\ x\in B^{1}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\cap B^{1},

x\notin\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow x\notin\bm{A}\ \vee\ x\notin\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}\ \vee\ x\in B^{1}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\cup B^{1},

x\in-\bm{A}\Leftrightarrow x\notin\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}\ \ \ \ \&\ \ \ \ x\notin-\bm{A}\Leftrightarrow x\in\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1},

(x,y)\in\bm{A}\times\bm{C}\Leftrightarrow x\in\bm{A}\ \&\ y\in\bm{C}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}\ \&\ y\in B^{1}:\Leftrightarrow(x,y)\in A^{1}\times B^{1},

(x,y)\notin\bm{A}\times\bm{C}\Leftrightarrow x\notin\bm{A}\ \vee\ y\notin\bm{C}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}\ \vee\ y\in B^{0}:\Leftrightarrow(x,y)\in(A^{0}\times Y)\cup(X\times B^{0}).

Note 2.9.24
.

In
[
18
]
, pp. 16–17, and in
[
19
]
, p. 73,
the operations between the complemented subsets of a set
X
follow Definition
2.8.9

in order to employ the good behaviour of the corresponding characteristic functions in the new measure theory.
In the measure theory of
[
9
]
, where the characteristic functions of complemented subsets are not crucial,
the operations between complemented subsets are defined according to Definition
2.8.4
.
Bishop and Cheng use the notation
\bm{A}\times\bm{B}
instead of
\bm{A}\otimes\bm{B}
.
As it is evident from the previous figures, the
1
- and
0
-components of the complemented subsets in the Bishop-Cheng definition are subsets of the corresponding
1
- and
0
-components of the complemented subsets in the Bishop definition from
[
9
]
. Actually, the definitions of the operations of complemented subsets
in
[
9
]
associate to the
1
-component of the complemented subset a maximal complement. The two sets of operations though, share the same algebraic and set-theoretic properties. They only behave differently
with respect to their characteristic functions.
Based on the work
[
115
]
of Shulman, we can motivate the second set of operations in a way similar to
the motivation provided for the first set of operations in Note
2.9.23
.
Keeping the definitions of
x\in\bm{A}
and
x\notin\bm{B}
, we can apply a “linear”
interpretation of the connectives
\vee
and
\&
. As it is mentioned in
[
115
]
, p. 2, the multiplicative
version
P\ \textnormal{{par}}\ Q
of
P\vee Q
in linear logic represents the pattern
“if not
P
, then
Q
; and if not
Q
, then
P
”. Let

x\in\bm{A}\vee\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow[x\notin\bm{A}\Rightarrow x\in\bm{B}]\ \&\ [x\notin\bm{B}\Rightarrow x\in\bm{A}].

With the use of Ex falsum quodlibet the implication
x\notin\bm{A}\Rightarrow x\in\bm{B}
holds if
x\in\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{1}
, or
if
x\notin\bm{A}:\Leftrightarrow x\in A^{0}
and
x\in\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in B^{1}
i.e., if
x\in A^{0}\cap B^{1}
. Hence,
the first implication holds if
x\in A^{1}\cup(A^{0}\cap B^{1})
. Similarly, the second holds if

x\in B^{1}\cup(B^{0}\cap A^{1})
. Thus

x\in\bm{A}\vee\bm{B}\Leftrightarrow x\in[A^{1}\cup(A^{0}\cap B^{1})]\cap[B^{1}\cup(B^{0}\cap A^{1})],

and the last intersection is equal to
\textnormal{{Dom}}(\bm{A}\vee\bm{B})
! One then can define
x\notin\bm{A}\vee\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\notin\bm{A}\ \&\ x\notin\bm{B}
, and
x\in\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in\bm{A}\ \&\ x\in\bm{B}
,
and
x\notin\bm{A}\wedge\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow x\in(-\bm{A})\vee(-\bm{B})
.

For the relation of complemented subsets to the Chu construction see
[
103
]
.

Chapter 3
Families of sets

We develop the basic theory of set-indexed families of sets and of family-maps between them.
We study the exterior union of a family of sets
\Lambda
, or the
\sum
-set of
\Lambda
, and the set
of dependent functions over
\Lambda
, or the
\prod
-set of
\Lambda
. We prove the distributivity of

\prod
over
\sum
for families of sets indexed by a product of sets, which is the translation
of the type-theoretic axiom of choice into
\mathrm{BST}
. Sets of sets are special set-indexed families of sets
that allow “lifting” of functions on the index-set to functions on them. The direct families of sets and the
set-relevant families of sets are introduced. The index-set of the former is a directed set, while the
transport maps of the latter are more than one and appropriately indexed. With the use of the introduced
universe
\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}
of sets and impredicative sets we study families of families of sets.

3.1
Set-indexed families of sets

Roughly speaking, a family of sets indexed by some set
I
is an
assignment routine
\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
that
behaves like a function i.e., if
i=_{I}j
, then
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
. Next follows
an exact formulation of this description that reveals the witnesses of the
equality
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

If
I
is a set,
a family of sets indexed by
I
, or an
I
-family
of sets, is a pair
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
, where

, and
\lambda_{1}
, a

\lambda_{0}

\lambda_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(j)\big),\ \ \ \lambda_{1}(i,j):=\lambda_{ij},\ \ \ (i,j)\in D(I),

of

\mathbb{V}_{0}
, therefore it is also natural not to consider
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
to be a class. Hence,
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
is a totality “between” a (predicative) set and a class. For this reason, we say that
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
is an

impredicative set
. Next follows an obvious generalisation of a family-map.

Proposition 3.1.7
.

The operations on families of sets generate operations on family-maps.
