3.2
The exterior union of a family of sets

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-family of sets. The
exterior union, or
disjoint union, or the
\sum
-set

\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
of
\Lambda
, and its canonical equality
are defined by

w\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\exists_{i\in I}\exists_{x\in\lambda_{0}(i)}\big(w:=(i,x)\big),

(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y.

The
\sum
-set of the
\mathbb{2}
-family
\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}
of the sets
X
and
Y

is the coproduct of
X
and
Y
, and we write

X+Y:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i).

Proposition 3.2.2
.

(i)

The equality on
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
satisfies the conditions of an equivalence relation.

(ii)

Let
(I,=_{I},\neq_{I})
be a discrete set and
\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
an inequality on
\lambda_{0}(i)
, for every

i\in I
. If the transport map
\lambda_{ij}
is strongly extensional, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
, then the relation

(i,x)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow i\neq_{I}j\ \vee\ \big(i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\big)

is an inequality on
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. If
(\lambda_{0}(i),=_{\lambda_{0}(i)},\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)})
is a discrete set, for every
i\in I
, then

\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}},\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big)
is discrete. Moreover, if
\neq_{I}
is tight, and if, for every

i\in I
, the inequality
\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
is tight, then the inequality

\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}
is tight.

Proof.

(i) Let

(i,x),(j,y)
,
(k,z)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. Since
i=_{I}i
and
\lambda_{ii}:=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
, we get
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i,x)
. If
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
, then
j=_{I}i
and

\lambda_{ji}(y)=\lambda_{ji}(\lambda_{ij}(x))=\lambda_{ii}(x):=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}(x):=x,

hence
(j,y)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i,x)
. If
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
and

(j,y)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(k,z)
, then
i=_{I}j\ \&\ j=_{I}k\Rightarrow i=_{I}k
, and

\lambda_{ik}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(k)}}(\lambda_{jk}\circ\lambda_{ij})(x):=\lambda_{jk}(\lambda_{ij}(x))=_{{\lambda_{0}(k)}}\lambda_{jk}(y)=_{{\lambda_{0}(k)}}z.

(ii) The condition
(\mathrm{Ap}_{1})
of Definition
2.2.5
is trivially satisfied. To show condition

(\mathrm{Ap}_{2})
, we suppose
first that
i\neq_{I}j
, hence by the corresponding condition of
\neq_{I}
we get
(j,y)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i,x)
. If
i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
, we show that
\lambda_{ji}(y)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)}x
. By the extensionality of

\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}
(Remark
2.2.6
)
the inequality
\lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
implies the inequality

\lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}\big(\lambda_{ji}(y)\big)
, and since

\lambda_{ij}
is strongly extensional, we get
x\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\lambda_{ji}(y)
. To show
condition
(\mathrm{Ap}_{3})
, let
(i,x)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
, and let

(k,z)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. If
i\neq_{I}j
, then by condition
(\mathrm{Ap}_{3})
of
\neq_{I}
we get

k\neq_{I}i
, or
k\neq_{I}j
, hence
(k,z)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i,x)
, or

(k,z)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
. Suppose next
i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
. Since the set
(I,=_{I},\neq_{I})
is discrete,
k\neq_{I}i
, or
k=_{I}i=_{I}j
.
If
k\neq_{I}i
, then what we want to show follows immediately. If
k=_{I}i=_{I}j
, then by the extensionality of

\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}
and the strong extensionality of the transport map

\lambda_{kj}
we have that

\lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\Rightarrow\lambda_{kj}\big(\lambda_{ik}(x)\big)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{kj}\big(\lambda_{jk}(y)\big)\Rightarrow\lambda_{ik}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(k)}\lambda_{jk}(y).

Hence, by condition
(\mathrm{Ap}_{3})
of
\neq_{\lambda_{0}(k)}
we get
\lambda_{ik}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(k)}z
, or

\lambda_{jk}(y)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(k)}z
, hence
(i,x)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(k,z)
, or

(j,y)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(k,z)
. Suppose next that

(\lambda_{0}(i),=_{\lambda_{0}(i)},\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)})
is a discrete set, for every
i\in I
. We show that

(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
i.e.,
i=_{I}j
and
\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}y
, or

(i,x)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
i.e.,

i\neq_{I}j
or
i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
. Since
(I,=_{I},\neq_{I})
is discrete,

i=_{I}j
, or
i\neq_{I}j
. In the first case, and since
(\lambda_{0}(j),=_{\lambda_{0}(j)},\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)})
is discrete, we get
\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
or

\lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
, and what we want follows immediately. If
i\neq_{I}j
, we get

(i,x)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
.
Finally, we suppose that
\neq_{I}
is tight, and that
\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
is tight, for every

i\in I
. Let
\neg\big[(i,x)\neq_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)\big]
i.e.,

\big[i\neq_{I}j\ \vee\ \big(i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\big)\big]\Rightarrow\bot.

From this hypothesis we get the conjunction
2
2

2

Here we use the logical implication

\big((\phi\vee\psi)\Rightarrow\bot\big)\Rightarrow[(\phi\Rightarrow\bot)\ \&\ (\psi\Rightarrow\bot)]
.

\big[i\neq_{I}j\Rightarrow\bot\big]\ \ \&\ \ \big[\big(i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\big)\Rightarrow\bot\big].

By the tightness of
\neq_{I}
we get
i=_{I}j
. The implication

\big(i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\big)\Rightarrow\bot
logically implies the implication
(i=_{I}j)\Rightarrow\big(\lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\Rightarrow\bot\big)
,
and since
its premiss
i=_{I}j
is derived by the tightness of
\neq_{I}
, by Modus Ponens we get

\lambda_{ij}(x)\neq_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\Rightarrow\bot
. Since
\neq_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
is tight, we conclude that

\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
, hence
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
.
∎

The totality
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is considered to be a set.
By the definition of
X+Y

\displaystyle w\in X+Y

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\exists_{x\in\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)}\big(w:=(i,x)\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists_{x\in X}\big(w:=(0,x)\big)\ \vee\ \exists_{y\in Y}\big(w:=(1,y)\big),

(i,x)=_{X+Y}(i{{}^{\prime}},x{{}^{\prime}})\Leftrightarrow(i=_{\mathbb{2}}i{{}^{\prime}}=_{\mathbb{2}}0\ \&\ x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}})\ \vee\ (i=_{\mathbb{2}}i{{}^{\prime}}=_{\mathbb{2}}1\ \&\ x=_{Y}x{{}^{\prime}}).

One could have defined
X+Y
independently from
\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}
, and then prove

X+Y=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)
.

Corollary 3.2.3
.

If
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
,
(Y,=_{Y},\neq_{Y})
are discrete,

(X+Y,=_{X+Y},\neq_{X+Y})
is
discrete.

Proof.

Since
(\mathbb{2},=_{\mathbb{2}},\neq_{\mathbb{2}})
is a discrete set, we use Proposition
3.2.2
(ii).
∎

Definition 3.2.4
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}),M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
be
I
-families of sets.
The coproduct family of
\Lambda
and
M
is the pair

\Lambda+M:=(\lambda_{0}+\mu_{0},\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1})
, where
(\lambda_{0}+\mu_{0})(i):=\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)
,
for every
i\in I
, and the map
\big(\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1}\big)_{ij}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)\to\lambda_{0}(j)+\mu_{0}(j)
is defined by

\big(\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1}\big)_{ij}(w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(0,\lambda_{ij}(x)\big)&\mbox{, $w:=(0,x)$}\\
\big(1,\mu_{ij}(y)\big)&\mbox{, $w:=(1,y)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ w\in\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i).

It is straightforward to show that
\Lambda+M
is an
I
-family of sets.

Proposition 3.2.5
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}),M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})
, and
N:=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1})
be
I
-families of sets.
If
\Phi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow N
and
\Psi\colon M\Rightarrow N
, then
\Phi+\Psi\colon\Lambda+M\Rightarrow N
is
the coproduct family-map of
\Phi
and

\Psi
, where,
for every
i\in I
, the map
(\Phi+\Psi)_{i}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)\to\nu_{0}(i)
is defined by

(\Phi+\Psi)_{i}(w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Phi_{i}(x)&\mbox{, $w:=(0,x)$}\\
\Psi_{i}(y)&\mbox{, $w:=(1,y)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ w\in\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i).

Proof.

If
i=_{I}j
, the following diagram is commutative
\nu_{0}(i)
\nu_{0}(j)
,
{\lambda_{0}(j)+\mu_{0}(j)}
{\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)}
\nu_{ij}
{(\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1})_{ij}}
(\Phi+\Psi)_{i}
(\Phi+\Psi)_{j}

since by the commutativity of the following left diagram
\nu_{0}(i)
\nu_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\nu_{0}(i)
\nu_{0}(j)
,
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(i)
\nu_{ij}
\lambda_{ij}
\Phi_{i}
\Phi_{j}
\nu_{ij}
\mu_{ij}
\Psi_{i}
\Psi_{j}

\displaystyle(\Phi+\Psi)_{j}\big((\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1})_{ij}(0,x)\big)

\displaystyle:=(\Phi+\Psi)_{j}\big(0,\lambda_{ij}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{j}\big(\lambda_{ij}(x)\big)

\displaystyle=\nu_{ij}\big(\Phi_{i}(x)\big)

\displaystyle:=\nu_{ij}\big((\Phi+\Psi)_{i}(0,x)\big);\ \ \ \ x\in\lambda_{0}(i).

By the commutativity of the right diagram,

(\Phi+\Psi)_{j}\big((\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1})_{ij}(1,y)\big)=\nu_{ij}\big((\Phi+\Psi)_{i}(1,y)\big)
.
∎

Proposition 3.2.6
.

If
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
,
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, then

\sum_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)\big)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\bigg(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)+\bigg(\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)\bigg).

Proof.

Let
f\colon\sum_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)\big)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)+\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
be defined by

f(i,w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(0,(i,x)\big)&\mbox{, $w:=(0,x)$}\\
\big(1,(i,y)\big)&\mbox{, $w:=(1,y)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ i\in I,\ w\in\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i).

Clearly,
f
is a well-defined operation. To show that
f
is a function, we suppose that

(i,w)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}(\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i))}}(j,u):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ (\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1})_{ij}(w)=_{{}\lambda_{0}(j)+\mu_{0}(j)}u,

and we show that
f(i,w)=f(j,u)
. The equality
(\lambda_{1}+\mu_{1})_{ij}(w)=_{{}\lambda_{0}(j)+\mu_{0}(j)}u
amounts to

\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}x{{}^{\prime}}
, if
w:=(0,x)
and
u:=(0,x{{}^{\prime}})
, or to

\mu_{ij}(y)=_{\mu_{0}(j)}y{{}^{\prime}}
, if
w:=(1,y)
and
u:=(1,y{{}^{\prime}})
. With the use of these equalities and the
definition of the canonical equality on the coproduct it
is straightforward to show that
\big(0,(i,x)\big)=\big(0,(j,x{{}^{\prime}})\big)
, or

\big(1,(i,y)\big)=\big(1,(j,y{{}^{\prime}})\big)
, hence
f(i,w)=f(j,u)
. Let
g\colon\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)+\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)\big)
be defined by

g(U):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\big(i,(0,x)\big)&\mbox{, $U:=\big(0,(i,x)\big)$}\\
\big(i,(1,y)\big)&\mbox{, $U:=\big(1,(i,y)\big)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ U\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)+\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i).

Proceeding similarly, we show that the operation
g
is a function. It is straightforward to show that
(f,g)\colon\sum_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)+\mu_{0}(i)\big)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)+\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
.
∎

Proposition 3.2.7
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
,
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, and
\Psi:\Lambda\Rightarrow M
.

(i)

For every
i\in I
the operation

e_{i}^{\Lambda}:\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

e_{i}^{\Lambda}(x):=(i,x)
, for every
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
,
is an embedding.

(ii)

The operation

\Sigma\Psi:\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

\Sigma\Psi(i,x):=(i,\Psi_{i}(x));\ \ \ \ (i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),

is a function,
such that for every

i\in I
the following diagram commutes
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\Sigma\Psi
\Psi_{i}
e_{i}^{\Lambda}
e_{i}^{M}

(iii)

If
\Psi_{i}
is an embedding, for every
i\in I
, then
\Sigma\Psi
is an
embedding.

(iv)

If
\Psi_{i}
is a surjection, for every
i\in I
, then
\Sigma\Psi
is an
surjection.

(v)

If
\Phi:M\Rightarrow\Lambda
, where
\Phi_{i}
is a modulus of surjectivity for
\Psi_{i}
, for every
i\in I
,
then a modulus of surjectivity for
\Sigma\Psi
is the operation

\sigma\Psi\colon\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
,
defined by

\sigma\Psi(i,y):=(i,\Phi_{i}(y));\ \ \ \ (i,y)\in\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i).

Proof.

(i) If
x,y\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, then
e_{i}^{\Lambda}(x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}e_{i}^{\Lambda}(y)

if and only if
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i,y)
, which is equivalent to

\lambda_{ii}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}y:\Leftrightarrow x=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}y
.

(ii) If
(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y)
i.e.,
i=_{I}j
and
\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
, we show that
(i,\Psi_{i}(x))=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(j,\Psi_{j}(y))

i.e.,
i=_{I}j
and
\mu_{ij}(\Psi_{i}(x))=_{\mu_{0}(j)}\Psi_{j}(y)
. Since
\Psi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow M
,
we get
\mu_{ij}(\Psi_{i}(x))=_{\mu_{0}(j)}\Psi_{j}(\lambda_{ij}(x))=_{\mu_{0}(j)}\Psi_{j}(y)
. The required commutativity of the diagram is immediate to show.

(iii) Since
\Psi
is a family-map from
\Lambda
to
M
, we have that

\displaystyle\Sigma\Psi(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}\Sigma\Psi(j,y)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow(i,\Psi_{i}(x))=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(j,\Psi_{j}(y))

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \mu_{ij}(\Psi(x))=_{\mu_{0}(j)}\Psi_{j}(y)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \Psi_{j}(\lambda_{ij}(x))=_{\mu_{0}(j)}\Psi_{j}(y)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y).

(iv) Let
(i,y)\in\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
. Since
\Psi_{i}
is a surjection, there is
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
such that

\Psi_{i}(x)=y
. Hence
\Sigma\Psi(i,x):=(i,\Psi_{i}(x))=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(i,y)
,
since
\mu_{ii}\big(\Psi_{i}(x)\big):=\Psi_{i}(x)=_{\mu_{0}(i)}y
.

(v) If
y\in\mu_{0}(i)
, then
\Psi_{i}(\Phi_{i}(y))=_{\mu_{0}(i)}y
. To show that the operation

\sigma\Psi
is a function, we suppose
(i,y)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(j,z):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \mu_{ij}(y)=_{\mu_{0}(j)}z
and we show that

(i,\sigma_{i}(y))=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,\sigma_{j}(z)):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(\Phi_{i}(y))=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\Phi_{j}(z)
. Since
\Phi_{j}\colon\mu_{0}(j)\to\lambda_{0}(j)
,
we have that
\mu_{ij}(y)=_{\mu_{0}(j)}z\Rightarrow\Phi_{j}\big(\mu_{ij}(y)\big)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\Phi_{j}(z)
.
By the commutativity of the diagram
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
,
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{ij}
\mu_{ij}
\Phi_{i}
\Phi_{j}

\Phi_{j}(z)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\Phi_{j}\big(\mu_{ij}(y)\big)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}(\Phi_{i}(y))
.
Since
\mu_{ii}\big(\Psi_{i}(\Phi_{i}(y))\big):=\Psi_{i}(\Phi_{i}(y))\big)=_{\mu_{0}(i)}y
,

\Sigma\Psi\big(\sigma\Psi(i,y)\big):=\Sigma\Psi\big((i,\Phi_{i}(y))\big):=\big(i,\Psi_{i}(\Phi_{i}(y))\big)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(i,y).\qed

Definition 3.2.8
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-family of sets. The
first projection on

\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the operation

\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda}\colon\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow I
,
defined by
pr_{1}^{\Lambda}(i,x):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(i,x):=i
, for every
(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.
We may only write
\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}
, if
\Lambda
is clearly understood from the context.

By the definition of the canonical equality on
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
we get
that
\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda}
is a function.

Definition 3.2.9
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-family of sets.
The
\sum
-indexing of
\Lambda
is the pair

\Sigma^{\Lambda}:=(\sigma_{0}^{\Lambda},\sigma_{1}^{\Lambda})
, where
\sigma_{0}^{\Lambda}\colon\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
is defined by

\sigma_{0}^{\Lambda}(i,x):=\lambda_{0}(i)
, for every
(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, and

\sigma_{1}^{\Lambda}\big((i,x),(j,y)\big):=\lambda_{ij}
, for every

\big((i,x),(j,y)\big)\in D\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\big)
.

Clearly,
\Sigma^{\Lambda}
is a family of sets over
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, and

\Sigma\colon\Sigma^{\Lambda}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda}}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\Lambda
(see
Definition
3.1.5
), where, if
w:=(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, we define

\Sigma_{w}:\lambda_{0}(i)\to\lambda_{0}({\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda}}(w))
to be the identity
\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
.

Definition 3.2.10
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-family of sets. The
second projection on

\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the dependent operation

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}(i,x):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(i,x):=x
, for every
(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.
We may only write
\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}
, when the family of sets
\Lambda
is clearly understood from the context.

In Remark
3.3.2
we show that
\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}
is a dependent function over the family

\Sigma^{\Lambda}
.

3.3
Dependent functions over a family of sets

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-family of sets.
The totality
\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
of dependent functions over

\Lambda
, or the

\prod
-set of
\Lambda
, is defined by

\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\Theta\in\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})\ \&\ \forall_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\big(\Theta_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}(\Theta_{i})\big),

and it is equipped with the canonical equality and the canonical inequality
of the set
\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})
.
If
X
is a set and
\Lambda^{X}
is the constant
I
-family
X

(
see Definition
3.1.1
)
,
we use the notation

X^{I}:=\prod_{i\in I}X.

Clearly, the property
P(\Phi):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\big(\Theta_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}(\Theta_{i})\big)

is extensional on
\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})
,
the equality on
\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is an equivalence relation.

\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is considered to be a set.

Remark 3.3.2
.

If
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
is an
I
-family of sets and

\Sigma^{\Lambda}:=(\sigma_{0}^{\Lambda},\sigma_{1}^{\Lambda})
is the
\sum
-indexing of
\Lambda
, then

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}
is a dependent function over
\Sigma^{\Lambda}
.

Proof.

By Definition
3.2.10
the second projection
\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}
of
\Lambda
is the dependent
assignment

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}(i),

such that
\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}(i,x):=x
, for every
(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. It suffices to show that
if
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
, then

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}(j,y):=y=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}(x):=\sigma_{1}^{\Lambda}\big((i,x),(j,y)\big)\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}(i,x)\big).\qed

Remark 3.3.3
.

(i)

If
\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}
is the
\mathbb{2}
-family of the sets
X
and
Y
, then

\prod_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}X\times Y
.

(ii)

If
I,A
are sets, and
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0}^{A},\lambda_{1})
is the constant
I
-family
A
, then

A^{I}=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\mathbb{F}(I,A)
.

Proof.

(i) Let
f\colon\prod_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)\rightsquigarrow X\times Y
be defined by
f(\Phi):=(\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
,
for every
\Phi\in\prod_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)
. Let

g\colon X\times Y\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)
be defined by
g(x,y):=\Phi_{(x,y)}
,
for every
(x,y)\in X\times Y
. It is easy to show that
f,g
are well-defined functions and
(f,g)\colon\prod_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}X\times Y
.

(ii) Let
h\colon A^{I}\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{F}(I,A)
be defined by
h(\Phi):=h_{\Phi}\colon I\to A
, where

h_{\Phi}(i):=\Phi_{i}
, for every
\Phi\in A^{I}
and
i\in I
. Let

k\colon\mathbb{F}(I,A)\rightsquigarrow A^{I}
be defined by
k(e):=\Phi_{e}
, where
[\Phi_{e}]_{i}:=e(i)
,
for every
e\in\mathbb{F}(I,A)
and
i\in I
. Then
h,k

are well-defined functions and
(h,k)\colon A^{I}=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\mathbb{F}(I,A)
.
∎

Corollary 3.3.4
.

If
\Lambda,M\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
and
\Psi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mu_{0}(i)\big)
,
the following are equivalent:

(i)

\Psi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow M
.

(ii)

\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\big[\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0},\mu_{0})\big](i)
.

Proof.

If
i=_{I}j
, the commutativity of the following left diagram
\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(j)
,
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\mu_{ij}
\lambda_{ij}
\Phi_{i}
\Phi_{j}
\mu_{ij}
\lambda_{ji}
\Phi_{i}
\Phi_{j}

is equivalent to the commutativity of the above right one, hence the defining condition for
\Psi\in\textnormal{{Map}}(\Lambda,M)

is equivalent to the defining condition
\Psi_{j}=\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{1},\mu_{1})_{ij}(\Psi_{i}):=\mu_{ij}\circ\Psi_{i}\circ\lambda_{ji}
for
\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(i)\big)

(see Definition
3.1.6
(ii)).
∎

Proposition 3.3.5
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
,
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, and
\Psi:\Lambda\Rightarrow M
.

(i)

If
i\in I
, the operation

\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}:\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

\Theta\mapsto\Theta_{i},
is a function.

(ii)

The operation

\Pi\Psi:\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

[\Pi\Psi(\Theta)]_{i}:=\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i});\ \ \ \ i\in I,

is a function,
such that for every
i\in I
the following diagram commutes
\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\Pi\Psi
\Psi_{i}
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}
\pi_{i}^{M}

(iii)

If
\Psi_{i}
is an embedding, for every
i\in I
, then
\Pi\Psi
is an
embedding.

(iv)

If
\Phi:M\Rightarrow\Lambda
such that
\Phi_{i}
is a modulus of surjectivity for
\Psi_{i}
, for every
i\in I
,
the operation
\pi\Psi\colon\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is a modulus of surjectivity for

\prod\Psi
, where

\big[\pi\Psi(\Omega)\big]_{i}:=\Phi_{i}(\Omega_{i});\ \ \ \ \Omega\in\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i),\ i\in I.

Proof.

(i) This follows immediately from the definition of equality on
\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.

(ii) First we show that
\Pi\Psi
is well-defined i.e.,
\Pi\Psi(\Theta)\in\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
. If
i=_{I}j
, then by the commutativity of the following left diagram from
the definition of a family-map
\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
,
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{ij}
\lambda_{ij}
\Psi_{i}
\Psi_{j}
\lambda_{ij}
\mu_{ij}
\Phi_{i}
\Phi_{j}

[\Pi\Psi(\Theta)]_{j}:=\Psi_{j}(\Theta_{j})=\Psi_{j}\big(\lambda_{ij}(\Theta_{i})\big)=\mu_{ij}\big(\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i})\big):=\mu_{ij}\big([\Pi\Psi(\Theta)]_{i}\big).

It is immediate to show that
\Pi\Psi
is a function and that the required diagram commutes.

(iii) If
\Theta,\Theta{{}^{\prime}}\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, then

\displaystyle\Pi\Psi(\Theta)=_{{\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}\Pi\Psi(\Theta{{}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i})=_{\mu_{0}(i)}\Psi_{i}(\Theta{{}^{\prime}}_{i})\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\Theta_{i}=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\Theta{{}^{\prime}}_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\Theta=_{{\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\Theta{{}^{\prime}}.

(iv) First we show that
\pi\Psi
is well-defined i.e.,
\pi\Psi(\Omega)\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. If

i=_{I}j
, and since
\Phi:M\Rightarrow\Lambda
, by the commutativity of the above right diagram

\big[\pi\Psi(\Omega)\big]_{j}:=\Phi_{j}(\Omega_{j})=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\Phi_{j}\big(\mu_{ij}(\Omega_{i})\big)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}\big(\Phi_{i}(\Omega_{i})\big):=\lambda_{ij}\big(\big[\pi\Psi(\Omega)\big]_{i}\big).

It is immediate to show that
\pi\Psi
is a function. Finally we show that
\Pi\Psi\big(\pi\Psi(\Omega)\big)=\Omega
,
for every
\Omega\in\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
. If
i\in I
, and since
\Phi_{i}
is a modulus of
surjectivity for
\Psi_{i}
,
we get

\big[\Pi\Psi\big(\pi\Psi(\Omega)\big)\big]_{i}:=\Psi_{i}\big(\big[\pi\Psi(\Omega)\big]_{i}\big):=\Psi_{i}\big(\Phi_{i}(\Omega_{i})\big)=\Omega_{i}.\qed

Proposition 3.3.6
.

If
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
,
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, then

\prod_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(i)\big)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\bigg(\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)\bigg),

\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,M)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mu_{0}(i)\big).

Proof.

Let the operation
f\colon\prod_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(i)\big)\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\times\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
be defined by
f(\Phi):=\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi),\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(\Phi)\big)
, for every

\Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(i)\big)
, where

\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi)_{i}:=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi_{i})
and
\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(\Phi)_{i}:=\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(\Phi_{i})
, for every
i\in I
.
Using Definition
3.1.6
(i),

\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi)_{j}:=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi_{j})=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}\big((\lambda_{1}\times\mu_{1})_{ij}(\Phi_{i})\big):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}\big(\lambda_{ij}(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi)_{i}),\mu_{ij}(\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(\Phi)_{i}\big):=\lambda_{ij}(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi)_{i}),

hence
\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi)\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.
Similarly,
\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(\Phi)\in\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
. It is immediate to show that the operation

f
is a function.
Let
g\colon\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\times\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(i)\big)
be defined by

g(\Psi,\Xi):=\Phi
, for every
\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
and
\Xi\in\prod_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
,
where
\Phi_{i}:=(\Psi_{i},\Xi_{i})
, for every
i\in I
.
We show that
g
is well-defined i.e.,
\Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(i)\big)
.
If
i=_{I}j
, then

(\lambda_{1}\times\mu_{1})_{ij}(\Phi_{i}):=(\lambda_{1}\times\mu_{1})_{ij}(\Psi_{i},\Xi_{i}):=\big(\lambda_{ij}(\Psi_{i}),\mu_{ij}(\Xi_{i})\big)=(\Psi_{j},\Xi_{j}):=\Phi_{j}.

Clearly,
f,g
are inverse to each other. For the equality

\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,M)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mu_{0}(i)\big)
, we use Coroallry
3.3.4

and the corresponding identity maps are its witnesses.
∎

3.4
Subfamilies of families of sets

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
and
h\colon J\to I
. The pair

\Lambda\circ h:=(\lambda_{0}\circ h,\lambda_{1}\circ h)
, defined in Definition
3.1.6
,
is called the
h
-subfamily of
\Lambda
, and we write

(\Lambda\circ h)_{J}\leq\Lambda_{I}
. If
J:={\mathbb{N}}
,
we call
\Lambda\circ h
the
h
-subsequence of
\Lambda
.

Remark 3.4.2
.

If
\Lambda\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
, then
\Lambda\circ h\in\textnormal{{Set}}(J)
if and only
h
is an embedding.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda\circ h\in\textnormal{{Set}}(J)
and
h(j)=_{I}h(j{{}^{\prime}})
,
hence

(\lambda_{h(j)h(j{{}^{\prime}})},\lambda_{h(j{{}^{\prime}})h(j)})\colon\lambda_{0}(h(j))=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(h(j{{}^{\prime}}))
,
and
j=_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}
. If
h
is an embedding and
(\lambda_{0}\circ h)(j)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}(\lambda\circ h)(j{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\lambda_{0}(j(j))=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(h(j{{}^{\prime}}))
, then
h(j)=_{I}h(j{{}^{\prime}})
, since
\Lambda\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
,
and hence
j=_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.3
.

Let
\Lambda,M\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
,
h\in\mathbb{F}(J,I)
and
g\in\mathbb{F}(I,K)
.

(i)

\Lambda\circ\mathrm{id}_{I}:=\Lambda
.

(ii)

(\Lambda\circ g)\circ h:=\Lambda\circ(g\circ h)
.

(iii)

If
\Phi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow M
, then
\Phi\circ h\colon\Lambda\circ h\Rightarrow M\circ h
, where

(\Phi\circ h)_{j}\colon\lambda_{0}(h(j))\to\mu_{0}(h(j)),\ \ \ \ (\Phi\circ h)_{j}:=\Phi_{h(j)};\ \ \ \ j\in J.

(iv)

If
\Phi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow M
, then
\Phi^{h}\colon\Lambda\circ h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h}}{{\Rightarrow}}M
, where

\Phi_{j}^{h}\colon\lambda_{0}(h(j))\to\mu_{0}(h(j)),\ \ \ \ \Phi_{j}^{h}:=\Phi_{h(j)};\ \ \ \ j\in J.

(iv)

(\Lambda\circ h)\times(M\circ h):=(\Lambda\times M)\circ h
.

(v)

\mathbb{F}\big((\Lambda\circ h),(M\circ h)\big):=\mathbb{F}(\Lambda,M)\circ h
.

Proof.

All cases are straightforward to show.
∎

Proposition 3.4.4
.

Let
\Lambda\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, and
h\colon J\to I
.

(i)

The operation
\sum_{h}\colon\sum_{j\in J}\lambda_{0}(h(j))\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

\sum_{h}(j,u):=(h(j),u);\ \ \ \ (j,u)\in\sum_{j\in J}\lambda_{0}(h(j)),

is a function, and it is an embedding if
h
is an embedding.

(ii)

The operation
\prod_{h}\colon\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\prod_{j\in J}\lambda_{0}(h(j))
,
defined by

\Phi\mapsto\prod_{h}(\Phi),\ \ \ \ \bigg(\prod_{h}\Phi\bigg)_{j}:=\Phi_{h(j)};\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\ j\in J,

is a function, and if
h
is an embedding, then
\prod_{h}
is an embedding.

Proof.

(i) By definition we have that

(j,u)=_{{\sum_{j\in J}(\lambda_{0}\circ h)(j)}}(j{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow j=_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ \lambda_{h(j)h(j{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{{\lambda_{0}(h(j{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}},

(h(j),u)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(h(j{{}^{\prime}}),u{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow h(j)=_{I}h(j{{}^{\prime}})\ \&\ \lambda_{h(j)h(j{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{{\lambda_{0}(h(j{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}.

Since
h
is a function, the operation
\sum_{h}
is a function. If
h
is an embedding, it is immediate to
show that
\sum_{h}
is an embedding.

(ii) First we show that
\prod_{h}
is well-defined. If
j=_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}
, then

\bigg(\prod_{h}\Phi\bigg)_{j^{\prime}}:=\Phi_{h(j{{}^{\prime}})}=_{{\lambda_{0}(h(j{{}^{\prime}}))}}\lambda_{h(j)h(j{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\Phi_{h(j)}\big):=(\lambda_{1}\circ h)_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}\bigg(\bigg(\prod_{h}\Phi\bigg)_{j}\bigg).

It is immediate to show that
\prod_{h}
is a function. Let
h
be a surjection and let
\Phi,\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
such that
\prod_{h}(\Phi)=_{{\prod_{j\in J}\lambda_{0}(h(j))}}\prod_{h}(\Theta)
.
If
i\in I
, let
j\in J
with
h(j)=_{I}i
. As

\Phi_{i}=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\lambda_{h(j)i}\Phi_{h(j)}
and
\Theta_{i}=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\lambda_{h(j)i}\Theta_{h(j)}
,
and since
\Phi_{h(j)}=_{\lambda_{0}(h(j))}\Theta_{h(j)}
, we get
\Phi_{i}=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\Theta_{i}
.
∎

3.5
Families of sets over products

Proposition 3.5.1
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}),K:=(k_{0},k_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
and
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}),N:=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J)
.

(i)

\Lambda\otimes M:=(\lambda_{0}\otimes\mu_{0},\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I\times J)
, where

\lambda_{0}\otimes\mu_{0}\colon I\times J\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
is defined by

(\lambda_{0}\otimes\mu_{0})(i,j):=\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j);\ \ \ \ (i,j)\in I\times J,

(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\to\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\times\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}}),

(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}(u,w):=\big(\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u),\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j).

(ii)

If
\Phi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow K
and
\Psi\colon M\Rightarrow N
, then
\Phi\otimes\Psi\colon\Lambda\otimes M\Rightarrow K\otimes N
, where, for every
(i,j)\in I\times J
,

(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\to k_{0}(i)\times\nu_{0}(j),

(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i,j)}(u,w):=\big(\Phi_{i}(u),\Psi_{j}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j).

(iii)

The following equalities hold

\sum_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\bigg(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\sum_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg),

\prod_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\bigg(\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\prod_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg).

Proof.

(i) The proof is straightforward.

(ii) We show the required following commutativity by the following supposed ones by
k_{0}(i)\times\nu_{0}(j)
k_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\times\nu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})
\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\times\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})
\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)
(k_{1}\otimes\nu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}
(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}
(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i,j)}
(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}
k_{0}(i)
k_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})
\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})
\lambda_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})
,
\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})
\mu_{0}(j)
k_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}
\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}
\Phi_{i}
\Phi_{i^{\prime}}
\nu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}
\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}
\Psi_{j}
\Psi_{j{{}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}\big((\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}\big)

\displaystyle:=(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u),\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(\Phi_{i{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u)\big),\Psi_{j{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(k_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\Phi_{i}(u)\big),\nu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\Psi_{j}(w)\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=(k_{1}\otimes\nu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\Phi_{i}(u),\Psi_{j}(w)\big)

\displaystyle:=(k_{1}\otimes\nu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}\big((\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i,j)}(u,w)\big).

(iii) For the equality on
\sum_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)
we have that

\big((i,j),(u,w)\big)=_{{\sum_{(i,j)\in I\times J}(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j))}}\big((i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}}),(u{{}^{\prime}},w{{}^{\prime}})\big):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}i{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ j=_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\

(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}})}(u,w)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\times\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})}}(u{{}^{\prime}},w{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ \mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)=_{{\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})}}w{{}^{\prime}}.

For the equality on
\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\times\big(\sum_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\big)
we have that

\big((i,u),(j,w)\big)=_{{\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\times\big(\sum_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\big)}}\big((i{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}),(j{{}^{\prime}},w{{}^{\prime}})\big):\Leftrightarrow

(i,u)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}})\ \&\ (j,w)=_{{\sum_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)}}(j{{}^{\prime}},w{{}^{\prime}}),

i.e., if
i=_{I}i{{}^{\prime}}
and
\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}
, and
j=_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}

and
\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)=_{{\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})}}w{{}^{\prime}}
. As the equality conditions for the two sets are equivalent,
the operation
\phi\colon\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\times\big(\sum_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\big)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)
, defined by the rule

\big((i,u),(j,w)\big)\mapsto\big((i,j),(u,w)\big)
, together with the operation
\theta\colon\sum_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)\rightsquigarrow\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\times\big(\sum_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\big)
, defined by the inverse rule

\big((i,j),(u,w)\big)\mapsto\big((i,u),(j,w)\big)
, are well-defined functions that witness the required equality
of the two sets in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.

(iv) We proceed similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.3.6
.
∎

Next we define new families of sets generated by a given family of sets indexed by the product

X\times Y
of
X
and
Y
. These families will also be used in section
.

Definition 3.5.2
.

Let
X,Y
be sets, and let
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
be an
(X\times Y)
-family of sets.

(i)

If
x\in X
, the
x
-component of
R
is the pair

R^{x}:=(\rho_{0}^{x},\rho_{1}^{x})
, where the assignment routines
\rho_{0}^{x}\colon Y\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\rho_{1}^{x}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y)}\mathbb{F}\big(\rho_{0}^{x}(y),\rho_{0}^{x}(y{{}^{\prime}})\big)
are
defined by
\rho_{0}^{x}(y):=\rho_{0}(x,y)
, for every
y\in Y
, and

\rho_{1}^{x}(y,y{{}^{\prime}}):=\rho_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}^{x}:=\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}
, for every
(y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y)
.

(ii)

If
y\in Y
, the
y
-component of
R

is the pair

R^{y}:=(\rho_{0}^{y},\rho_{1}^{y})
, where the assignment routines
\rho_{0}^{y}\colon Y\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\rho_{1}^{y}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\big(\rho_{0}^{y}(x),\rho_{0}^{y}(x{{}^{\prime}})\big)
are
defined by
\rho_{0}^{y}(x):=\rho_{0}(x,y)
, for every
x\in X
, and

\rho_{1}^{y}(x,x{{}^{\prime}}):=\rho_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}^{y}:=\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}
, for every
(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)
.

(iii)

Let
\sum^{1}R:=(\sum^{1}\rho_{0},\sum^{1}\rho_{1})
, where

\sum^{1}\rho_{0}:X\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x{{}^{\prime}})\bigg)\ \ \ \ \mbox{are defined by }

\bigg(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\bigg)(x):=\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{x}(y):=\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ x\in X,

\bigg(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)(x,x{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y);\ \ \ \ (x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X),

\bigg(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(y,u):=\big(y,\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big);\ \ \ \ (y,u)\in\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(iv)

Let
\sum^{2}R:=(\sum^{2}\rho_{0},\sum^{2}\rho_{1})
, where

\sum^{2}\rho_{0}:Y\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\sum^{2}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\sum^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\sum^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y{{}^{\prime}})\bigg)\ \ \ \ \mbox{are defined by }

\bigg(\sum^{2}\rho_{0}\bigg)(y):=\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{y}(x):=\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ y\in Y,

\bigg(\sum^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)(y,y{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\sum^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}});\ \ \ \ (y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y),

\bigg(\sum^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}(x,w):=\big(x,\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (x,w)\in\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(v)

Let
\prod^{1}R:=(\prod^{1}\rho_{0},\prod^{1}\rho_{1})
, where

\prod^{1}\rho_{0}:X\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\prod^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\prod^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x{{}^{\prime}})\bigg)\ \ \ \ \mbox{are defined by }

\bigg(\prod^{1}\rho_{0}\bigg)(x):=\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{x}(y):=\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ x\in X,

\bigg(\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)(x,x{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y);\ \ \ \ (x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X),

\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y}:=\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(\Theta_{y})\big);\ \ \ \ \Theta\in\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y),\ y\in Y.

(vi)

Let
\prod^{2}R:=(\prod^{2}\rho_{0},\prod^{2}\rho_{1})
, where

\prod^{2}\rho_{0}:Y\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\prod^{2}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\prod^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\prod^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y{{}^{\prime}})\bigg)\ \ \ \ \mbox{are defined by }

\bigg(\prod^{2}\rho_{0}\bigg)(y):=\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{y}(x):=\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ y\in Y,

\bigg(\prod^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)(y,y{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\prod^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}});\ \ \ \ (y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y),

\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi)\bigg]_{x}:=\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(\Phi_{x})\big);\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y),\ x\in X.

It is easy to show
that
R^{y},\sum^{1}R,\prod^{1}R\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X)
and
R^{x},\sum^{2}R,\prod^{2}R\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(Y)
.

Proposition 3.5.3
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1}),S:=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X\times Y)
, and

\Phi\colon R\Rightarrow S
.

(i)

Let
\Phi^{x}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\rho_{0}^{x}(y),\sigma_{0}^{x}(y)\big)
, where

\Phi^{x}_{y}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\colon\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\to\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)
.

(ii)

Let
\Phi^{y}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(R^{y}(x),S^{y}(x)\big)
, where

\Phi^{y}_{x}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\colon\rho_{0}^{y}(x)\to\sigma^{y}_{0}(x)
.

(iii)

Let
\sum^{1}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\sum^{1}\sigma_{0}\big)(x)\big)
, where, for every
x\in X
, we define

\bigg(\sum^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}\colon\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{y}(x)\to\sum_{y\in Y}\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)

\bigg(\sum^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(y,u):=\big(y,\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big);\ \ \ \ (y,u)\in\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(iv)

If
\sum^{2}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\sum^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\sum^{2}\sigma_{0}\big)(y)\big)
, where, for every
y\in Y
, we define

\bigg(\sum^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}\colon\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\to\sum_{x\in X}\sigma^{y}_{0}(x)

\bigg(\sum^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}(x,w):=\big(x,\Phi_{(x,y)}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (x,w)\in\sum_{x\in X}\rho(x,y).

(v)

Let
\prod^{1}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\prod^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\prod^{1}\sigma_{0}\big)(x)\big)
, where, for every
x\in X
, we define

\bigg(\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}\colon\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{y}(x)\to\prod_{y\in Y}\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)

\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\big(\Theta_{y}))\big);\ \ \ \ \Theta\in\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(vi)

Let
\prod^{2}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\prod^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\prod^{2}\sigma_{0}\big)(y)\big)
, where, for every
y\in Y
, we define

\bigg(\prod^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}\colon\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\to\prod_{x\in X}\sigma^{y}_{0}(x)

\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}(\Theta)\bigg]_{x}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\big(\Theta_{x})\big);\ \ \ \ \Theta\in\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

Then
\Phi^{x}\colon R^{x}\Rightarrow S^{x}
,
\Phi^{y}\colon R^{y}\Rightarrow S^{y}
,

\sum^{1}\Phi\colon\big(\sum^{1}R\big)\Rightarrow\big(\sum^{1}S\big)
,

\sum^{2}\Phi\colon\big(\sum^{2}R\big)\Rightarrow\big(\sum^{2}S\big)
,

\prod^{1}\Phi\colon\big(\prod^{1}R\big)\Rightarrow\big(\prod^{1}S\big)
, and

\prod^{2}\Phi\colon\big(\prod^{2}R\big)\Rightarrow\big(\prod^{2}S\big)
.

Proof.

The proofs of (ii), (iv) and (vi) are like the proofs of (i), (iii), and (v), respectively.

(i) It is immediate to show that the operation
\Phi^{x}_{y}\colon\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\rightsquigarrow\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)

is a function. If
y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}
, the commutativity of the following left diagram from the hypothesis
\Phi\colon R\Rightarrow S
\sigma_{0}(x,y)
\sigma_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})
\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})
\rho_{0}(x,y)
\sigma_{0}^{x}(y)
\sigma_{0}^{x}(y{{}^{\prime}})
\rho_{0}^{x}(y{{}^{\prime}})
\sigma_{0}^{x}(y)
\sigma_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}
\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}
\Phi_{(x,y)}
\Phi_{(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}
\sigma^{x}_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}
\rho^{x}_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}
\Phi_{y}^{x}
\Phi^{x}_{y{{}^{\prime}}}

implies the required commutativity of the right above diagram, as these are the same diagrams.

(iii) First we explain why the operation
\big(\sum^{1}\Phi\big)_{x}
is a function. If

(y,u)=_{{\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{y}(x)}}(y{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ \rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{{\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}},

\big(y,\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big)=_{{\sum_{y\in Y}\sigma_{0}^{y}(x)}}\big(y,\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big):\Leftrightarrow y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ \sigma_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big)=_{{\sigma_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}}\Phi_{(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(u{{}^{\prime}}).

From our hypothesis the second equality is equivalent to

\sigma_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big)=_{{\sigma_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}}\Phi_{(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(u)\big),

which is the commutativity of the above left diagram. If
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
, and since

\Phi_{(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\circ\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}=\sigma_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\circ\Phi_{(x,y)}

we get the commutativity of the following left diagram by

{\sum_{y\in Y}\sigma_{0}(x,y)}
{\sum_{y\in Y}\sigma_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}
{\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}
{\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)}
{\prod_{y\in Y}\sigma_{0}(x,y)}
{\prod_{y\in Y}\sigma_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}
{\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}
{\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)}
{\big(\sum^{1}\sigma_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}}
{\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}}
{\big(\sum^{1}\Phi\big)_{x}}
{\big(\sum^{1}\Phi\big)_{x{{}^{\prime}}}}
{\big(\prod^{1}\sigma_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}}
{\big(\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}}
{\big(\prod^{1}\Phi\big)_{x}}
{\big(\prod^{1}\Phi\big)_{x{{}^{\prime}}}}

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x{{}^{\prime}}}\bigg(\bigg(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(y,u)\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(\sum^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x{{}^{\prime}}}\big(y,\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(y,\Phi_{(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\big(\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(y,\sigma_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\big(\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(\sum^{1}\sigma_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\big(y,\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(\sum^{1}\sigma_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\bigg(\bigg(\sum^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(y,u)\bigg).

(v) First we explain why the operation
\big(\prod^{1}\Phi\big)_{x}
is well-defined. If
y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}
and

\Theta\in\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{x}(y)
, then by the
commutativity of the above left diagram we have that

\displaystyle\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y{{}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle:=\Phi_{(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\Theta_{y{{}^{\prime}}}\big)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\rho_{(xy)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(\Theta_{y})\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\Phi_{(x,y)}(\Theta_{y})\big)

\displaystyle:=\sigma^{x}_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}\bigg(\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y}\bigg).

Clearly, the operation
\big(\prod^{1}\Phi\big)_{x}
is a function. If
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
,
and by the commutativity of the first diagram in the proof of (iii) we get the commutativity of the
above right diagram

\displaystyle\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x{{}^{\prime}}}\bigg(\bigg(\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Theta)\bigg)\bigg]_{y}

\displaystyle:=\Phi_{(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y}\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\Phi_{(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\big(\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(\Theta_{y})\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\big(\Phi_{(x,y)}(\Theta_{y})\big)

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}\bigg(\bigg[\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y}\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigg[\bigg(\prod^{1}\sigma_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\bigg(\prod^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(\Theta)\bigg)\bigg]_{y}.\qed

Proposition 3.5.4
.

If
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X\times Y)
, the following equalities hold.

\sum_{x\in X}\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\sum_{y\in Y}\sum_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y),

\prod_{x\in X}\prod_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

Proof.

The proof is straightforward.
∎

3.6
The distributivity of
\prod
over
\sum

We prove the translation of the type-theoretic axiom of choice in
\mathrm{BST}
(Theorem
3.6.4
).

Lemma 3.6.1
.

Let
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
,
R^{x}:=(\rho_{0}^{x},\rho_{1}^{x})
and
\sum^{1}R:=(\sum^{1}\rho_{0},\sum^{1}\rho_{1})
be
the families of sets of Definition
3.5.2
.
If
\Phi\in\prod_{x\in X}\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x)
,
the operation
f_{\Phi}\colon X\rightsquigarrow Y
, defined by
x\mapsto\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}(\Phi_{x})
, for every
x\in X
,
is a function from
X
to
Y
.

Proof.

If
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
, then
\Phi_{x{{}^{\prime}}}=\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi_{x})
.
Since
\Phi_{x}\in\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)
, there are

y\in Y
and
u\in\rho_{0}(x,y)
such that
\Phi_{x}:=(y,u)
. Hence
f_{\Phi}(x):=y
and

f_{\Phi}(x{{}^{\prime}}):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}(\Phi_{x{{}^{\prime}}})=_{Y}\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}\bigg(\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi_{x})\bigg):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}\big(y,\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big):=y.\qed

Lemma 3.6.2
.

Let
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
,
R^{x}:=(\rho_{0}^{x},\rho_{1}^{x})
and
\sum^{1}R:=(\sum^{1}\rho_{0},\sum^{1}\rho_{1})
be
as above.
If
f\colon X\to Y
, the pair
N^{f}:=\big(\nu_{0}^{f},\nu_{1}^{f}\big)
is an
X
-family of sets, where
the assignment routines
\nu_{0}^{f}:X\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and

\nu_{1}^{f}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\big(\nu_{0}^{f}(x),\nu_{0}^{f}(x{{}^{\prime}})\big)

are given by
\nu_{0}^{f}(x):=\rho_{0}(x,f(x))
, for every
x\in X
, and

\nu_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}^{f}:=\rho_{(x,f(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f(x{{}^{\prime}}))}
, for every
(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)
,

Proof.

The proof is straightforward (see also
[
95
]
, p. 12).
∎

Lemma 3.6.3
.

If
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
and
N^{f}:=\big(\nu_{0}^{f},\nu_{1}^{f}\big)
are families of sets as above,
then the pair
\Xi:=(\xi_{0},\xi_{1})
is an
\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
-family of sets, where
the assignment routines

\xi_{0}:\mathbb{F}(X,Y)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and
\xi_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(f,f{{}^{\prime}})\in D(\mathbb{F}(X,Y))}\mathbb{F}\big(\xi_{0}(f),\xi_{0}(f{{}^{\prime}})\big)
are defined by

\xi_{0}(f):=\prod_{x\in X}\nu_{0}^{f}(x):=\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f(x));\ \ \ \ f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y),

\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}:\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f(x))\to\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x));\ \ \ \ (f,f{{}^{\prime}})\in D(\mathbb{F}(X,Y)),

\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x}:=\rho_{(x,f(x))(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))}(H_{x});\ \ \ \ H\in\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f(x)),\ \ x\in X.

Proof.

First we show that the operation
\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}
is well-defined i.e., if

H\in\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f(x)):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\big(H_{x{{}^{\prime}}}=\nu^{f}_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(H_{x}):=\rho_{(x,f(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f(x{{}^{\prime}}))}(H_{x})\big),\ \ \ \mbox{then}

\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\in\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x)):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\big(\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x{{}^{\prime}}}=\nu^{f}_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x}\big)

If
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
, then
f(x)=_{Y}f(x{{}^{\prime}})=_{Y}f{{}^{\prime}}(x{{}^{\prime}})=_{Y}f{{}^{\prime}}(x)
, and

\displaystyle\nu^{f}_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x}\big)

\displaystyle:=\rho_{(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f{{}^{\prime}}(x{{}^{\prime}}))}\big(\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x}\big)

\displaystyle:=\rho_{(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f{{}^{\prime}}(x{{}^{\prime}}))}\big(\rho_{(x,f(x))(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))}(H_{x})\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{(x,f(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f{{}^{\prime}}(x{{}^{\prime}}))}(H_{x})

\displaystyle=\rho_{(x{{}^{\prime}},f(x{{}^{\prime}}))(x{{}^{\prime}},f{{}^{\prime}}(x{{}^{\prime}}))}\big(\rho_{(x,f(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f(x{{}^{\prime}}))}(H_{x})\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{(x{{}^{\prime}},f(x{{}^{\prime}}))(x{{}^{\prime}},f{{}^{\prime}}(x{{}^{\prime}}))}(H_{x{{}^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle:=\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x{{}^{\prime}}}.

It is immediate to see to show that
\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}
is a function. If
f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
, then

\big[\xi_{ff}(H)\big]_{x}:=\rho_{(x,f(x))(x,f(x))}(H_{x}):=\mathrm{id}_{\rho_{0}(x,f(x))}(H_{x}):=H_{x}.

Moreover, if
f=_{\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}f{{}^{\prime}}=_{\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}f{{}^{\prime\prime}}
, the following diagram is commutative:
\xi_{0}(f{{}^{\prime}})
\xi_{0}(f{{}^{\prime\prime}})
\xi_{0}(f)
\xi_{f{{}^{\prime}}f{{}^{\prime\prime}}}
\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}
\ \xi_{ff{{}^{\prime\prime}}}

\displaystyle\big[(\xi_{f{{}^{\prime}}f{{}^{\prime\prime}}}\circ\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}})(H)\big]_{x}

\displaystyle:=\big[\xi_{f{{}^{\prime}}f{{}^{\prime\prime}}}\big(\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big)\big]_{x}

\displaystyle:=\rho_{(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))(x,f{{}^{\prime\prime}}(x))}\big(\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x}\big)

\displaystyle:=\rho_{(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))(x,f{{}^{\prime\prime}}(x))}\big(\rho_{(x,f(x))(x,f{{}^{\prime}}(x))}(H_{x})\big)

\displaystyle=\rho_{(x,f(x))(x,f{{}^{\prime\prime}}(x))}(H_{x})

\displaystyle:=\big[\xi_{ff{{}^{\prime\prime}}}(H)\big]_{x}.\qed

Theorem 3.6.4

(Distributivity of
\prod
over
\sum
)
.

Let
X,Y
be sets,
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
,
R^{x}:=(\rho_{0}^{x},\rho_{1}^{x})
, and

\sum^{1}R:=(\sum^{1}\rho_{0},\sum^{1}\rho_{1})
as above. If

\Phi\in\prod_{x\in X}\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x):=\prod_{x\in X}\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y),\ \ \ \mbox{there is}

\Theta_{\Phi}\in\prod_{x\in X}\nu_{0}^{f_{\Phi}}(x):=\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f_{\Phi}(x)),

where
f_{\Phi}\colon X\to Y
is defined in Lemma
3.6.1
.
The following operation is a function:

\textnormal{{ac}}:\prod_{x\in X}\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}\prod_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f(x))

\Phi\mapsto\big(f_{\Phi},\Theta_{\Phi}\big);\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\prod_{x\in X}\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

Proof.

Since by Remark
3.3.2
,

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{R^{x}}\in\prod_{w\in\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{x}(y)}\rho_{0}^{x}(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}(w)):=\prod_{w\in\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)}\rho_{0}(x,\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}(w)),

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{R^{x}}(\Phi_{x})\in\rho_{0}(x,\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}(\Phi_{x})):=\rho_{0}(x,f_{\Phi}(x)).

Hence, the dependent operation

\Theta_{\Phi}\in\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\nu_{0}^{f_{\Phi}}(x):=\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,f_{\Phi}(x))
,
defined by

\Theta_{\Phi}(x):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{R^{x}}(\Phi_{x}):=u;\ \ \ \ \Phi_{x}:=(y,u),\ \ y:=f_{\Phi}(x),\ \ x\in X,

is well-defined. To show that
\Theta_{\Phi}\in\prod_{x\in X}\nu_{0}^{f_{\Phi}}(x)
, let
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
. Since,

\Theta_{\Phi}(x{{}^{\prime}}):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{R^{x{{}^{\prime}}}}(\Phi_{x{{}^{\prime}}}):=u{{}^{\prime}};\ \ \ \ \Phi_{x{{}^{\prime}}}:=(y{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}),\ \ y{{}^{\prime}}:=f_{\Phi}(x{{}^{\prime}}),

we need to show that
u{{}^{\prime}}=_{{\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}}\nu_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}^{f_{\Phi}}(u)
. Since

\Phi\in\prod_{x\in X}\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x)
, we have that

(y{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}):=(f_{\Phi}(x{{}^{\prime}}),u{{}^{\prime}}):=\Phi_{x{{}^{\prime}}}=_{{\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}}\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{1}\big)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi_{x})=_{{\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}}\big(y,\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big).

By the last equality we get
y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}
and

\displaystyle\rho_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}^{x{{}^{\prime}}}\big(\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big)=_{{\rho_{0}^{x{{}^{\prime}}}(y{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\rho_{(x{{}^{\prime}},y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big)=_{{\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{{\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}.

Hence,

\nu_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}^{f_{\Phi}}(u):=\rho_{(x,f_{\Phi}(x))(x{{}^{\prime}},f_{\Phi}(x{{}^{\prime}}))}(u):=\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{{\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}}u{{}^{\prime}}.

To show that the operation
ac
is a function, we suppose that
\Phi=_{{\prod_{x\in X}\mu_{0}(x)}}\Phi{{}^{\prime}}
, and we show that
\textnormal{{ac}}(\Phi)=_{{\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}\xi_{0}(f)}}\textnormal{{ac}}(\Phi{{}^{\prime}})
i.e.,

\big(f_{\Phi},\Theta_{\Phi}\big)=_{{\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}\xi_{0}(f)}}\big(f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}},\Theta_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}\big):\Leftrightarrow f_{\Phi}=_{\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}\ \&\ \xi_{f_{\Phi},f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}}(\Theta_{\Phi})=_{\xi_{0}(f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}})}\Theta_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}.

By definition,
\Phi=_{{\prod_{x\in X}\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x)}}\Phi{{}^{\prime}}
if and only if

\Phi_{x}=_{{\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x)}}\Phi_{x}{{}^{\prime}}
,
for every
x\in X
. By Lemma
3.6.1

f_{\Phi}(x):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}(\Phi_{x}):=y;\ \ \ \ \Phi_{x}:=(y,u),

f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}(x):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda^{x}}(\Phi{{}^{\prime}}_{x}):=y{{}^{\prime}};\ \ \ \ \Phi{{}^{\prime}}_{x}:=(y{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}).

Since
\Phi_{x}=_{{\big(\sum^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x)}}\Phi_{x}{{}^{\prime}}
, we get
y=_{Y}y{{}^{\prime}}
, and

\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}u{{}^{\prime}}
. From the first equality we get and hence
f_{\Phi}(x)=_{Y}f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}(x)
,
and from the second we conclude that

\big[\xi_{f_{\Phi},f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}}(\Theta_{\Phi})\big]_{x}:=\rho_{(x,f_{\Phi}(x))(x,f_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}(x))}\big([\Theta_{f}]_{x}\big):=\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(u)=_{\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}u{{}^{\prime}}:=\big[\Theta_{\Phi{{}^{\prime}}}\big]_{x}.\qed

3.7
Sets of sets

Definition 3.7.1
.

If
I
is a set, a set of sets indexed by
I
, or an
I
-set
of sets, is a pair
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
such that
the following condition
is satisfied:

Q(\Lambda):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i,j\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)\Rightarrow i=_{I}j\big).

Let
\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
be their totality, equipped with the canonical equality on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
.

Remark 3.7.2
.

If
\Lambda\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
and
M\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
such that
\Lambda=_{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)}M
, then
M\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
.

Proof.

Let
i,j\in I
,
f\colon\mu_{0}(i)\to\mu_{0}(j)
and
g\colon\mu_{0}(j)\to\mu_{0}(i)
, such that

f\circ g=\mathrm{id}_{\mu_{0}(j)}
and
g\circ f=\mathrm{id}_{\mu_{0}(i)}
. It suffices to show that

\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
. Let
\Phi\in\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,M)
and
\Psi\in\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(M,\Lambda)
such that

\Phi\circ\Psi=\mathrm{id}_{M}
and
\Psi\circ\Phi=\mathrm{id}_{\Lambda}
. We define

f{{}^{\prime}}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\to\lambda_{0}(j)
and
g{{}^{\prime}}\colon\lambda_{0}(j)\to\lambda_{0}(i)
by

f{{}^{\prime}}:=\Psi_{j}\circ f\circ\Phi_{i}\ \ \ \&\ \ \ g{{}^{\prime}}:=\Psi_{i}\circ g\circ\Phi_{j}

\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
.
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(i)
f{{}^{\prime}}
f
\Phi_{i}
\Psi_{j}
g{{}^{\prime}}
g
\Psi_{i}
\Phi_{j}

It is straightforward to show that
(f{{}^{\prime}},g{{}^{\prime}})\colon\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
.
∎

By the previous remark
Q(\Lambda)
is an extensional property on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
. Since
\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
is defined by
separation on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, which is impredicative,
\textnormal{{Set}}(I)
is also
an impredicative set. We can also see that by an argument similar to the one used for the
impredicativity of
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
.

If
X,Y
are not equal sets in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
, then with Ex falsum we get that the
\mathbb{2}
-family

\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}
of
X
and
Y
is a
\mathbb{2}
-set of sets.
Similarly, if
X_{n}
and
X_{m}
are not equal in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
, for every
n\neq m
, then with Ex falsum we get that
the
{\mathbb{N}}
-family
\Lambda^{{\mathbb{N}}}
of
(X_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is an
{\mathbb{N}}
-set of sets.
If
I
is a set with
(i,j)\in I\times I
such that
\neg(i=_{I}j)
,
then the constant
I
-family
A
, for some set
A
, is an
I
-family that is not an
I
-set of sets.
We can easily turn an
I
-family of sets
\Lambda
into an

I
-set of sets.

Definition 3.7.3
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
. The equality
=_{I}^{\Lambda}
on
I
induced
by
\Lambda

is given by
i=_{I}^{\Lambda}j:\Leftrightarrow\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
, for every
i,j\in I
.
The set
\lambda_{0}I
of sets
generated by
\Lambda
is the totality
I

equipped with the equality
=_{I}^{\Lambda}
. For simplicity, we write
\lambda_{0}(i)\in\lambda_{0}I
, instead of

i\in I
, when
I
is equipped with the equality
=_{I}^{\Lambda}
. The operation
\lambda_{0}^{*}:I\rightsquigarrow I
from

(I,=_{I})
to
(I,=_{I}^{\Lambda})
, defined by
i\mapsto i
, for every
i\in I
, is denoted by

\lambda_{0}^{*}:I\rightsquigarrow\lambda_{0}I
, and its definition is rewritten as
\lambda_{0}^{*}(i):=\lambda_{0}(i)
,
for every
i\in I
.

Clearly,
\lambda_{0}^{*}
is a function.
In the next proof the hypothesis of a set of sets is crucial.

Proposition 3.7.4
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-set of sets, and let
Y
be a set.
If
f\colon I\to Y
, there is a unique function
\lambda_{0}f\colon\lambda_{0}I\to Y
such that the
following diagram commutes
\lambda_{0}I
I
Y.
\ \lambda_{0}f
\lambda_{0}
f

Conversely, if
f\colon I\rightsquigarrow Y
and
f^{*}:\lambda_{0}I\to Y
such that
the corresponding diagram commutes, then
f
is a function and
f^{*}
is equal to the function from

\lambda_{0}I
to
Y
generated by
f
.

Proof.

The operation
\lambda_{0}f
from
\lambda_{0}I
to
Y
defined by

\lambda_{0}f(\lambda_{0}(i)):=f(i)
, for every
\lambda_{0}(i)\in\lambda_{0}I
, is a function,
since, for every
i,j\in I
, we have that
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)\Rightarrow i=_{I}j
, hence

f(i)=_{Y}f(j):\Leftrightarrow\lambda_{0}f(\lambda_{0}(i))=_{Y}\lambda_{0}f(\lambda_{0}(j))
. The commutativity
of the diagram follows from the reflexivity of
=_{Y}
.
If
g\colon\lambda_{0}I\to Y
makes the above diagram commutative, then for every
\lambda_{0}(i)
we have
that
g(\lambda_{0}(i))=_{Y}f(i)=:\lambda_{0}f(\lambda_{0}(i))
, hence
g=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)}\lambda_{0}f
. For the converse, if
i,j\in I
, then by the transitivity of
=_{Y}
we have that

i=_{I}j\Rightarrow\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
, hence

\Rightarrow f^{*}(\lambda_{0}(i))=_{Y}f^{*}(\lambda_{0}(j))
, and
f(i)=_{Y}f(j)
.
The proof of the fact that
f^{*}
is the function from
\lambda_{0}I
to
Y
generated by
f
is immediate.
∎

Proposition 3.7.7

3.8
Direct families of sets

Definition 3.8.1
.

Let
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
be a directed set, and

D^{\preccurlyeq}(I):=\big\{(i,j)\in I\times I\mid i\preccurlyeq_{I}j\big\}

the diagonal of
\preccurlyeq_{I}
.
A direct family of sets
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
,
or an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-family of sets,
is a pair
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
, where

\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
, and
\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}
,
a modulus of transport maps for
\lambda_{0}
, is defined by

\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(j)\big),\ \ \ \lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}(i,j):=\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq},\ \ \ (i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I),

such that the transport maps
\lambda_{ij}^{\prec}

of
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}
satisfy the following conditions:

(a)

For every
i\in I
, we have that
\lambda_{ii}^{\preccurlyeq}:=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
.

(b)

If
i\preccurlyeq_{I}j
and
j\preccurlyeq_{I}k
, the following diagram commutes
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(k).
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}
\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}
\ \lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}

If
X\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
, the constant
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-family

X
is the pair
C^{\preccurlyeq,X}:=(\lambda_{0}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq,X})
, where
\lambda_{0}^{X}(i):=X
,
and
\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq,X}(i,j):=\mathrm{id}_{X}
, for every
i\in I
and
(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)
.

Since in general
\preccurlyeq_{I}
is not symmetric, the transport map
\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}

does not necessarily have an inverse. Hence
\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}
is only a modulus of transport for

\lambda_{0}
, in the sense that determines the transport maps of
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}
, and not necessarily
a modulus of function-likeness for
\lambda_{0}
.

Definition 3.8.2
.

If
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
and
M^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
are
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-families of sets, a
direct family-map
\Phi
from
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}
to

, denoted by

\Phi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}
,
their set
\textnormal{{Map}}_{(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq},M^{\preccurlyeq})
,
and the totality
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
of
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-families are defined as
in Definition
3.1.3
.
The direct sum

\sum_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
over

\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}
is the totality
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
equipped with the equality

(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\big(i\preccurlyeq_{I}k\ \&\ j\preccurlyeq_{I}k\ \&\ \lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}(y)\big).

The totality
\prod_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)

of dependent functions over
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}
is defined by

\Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\Phi\in\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})\ \&\ \forall_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\big(\Phi_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}(\Phi_{i})\big),

and it is equipped with the equality of
\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})
.

Clearly, the property
P(\Phi):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\big(\Phi_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}(\Phi_{i})\big)

is extensional on
\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})
,
the equality on
\prod_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
is an equivalence relation.

\prod_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
is considered to be a set.

Proposition 3.8.3
.

The relation
(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y)

Proof.

=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}

and we show that

Since the transport functions
\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}
are not in general embeddings,
we cannot show in general that

is an embedding, as it is the case for the map

in Proposition

(i). The study of direct families of sets can be extended following
the study of set-indexed families of sets.

3.9
Set-relevant families of sets

In general, we may want to have more than one transport maps from
\lambda_{0}(i)
to
\lambda_{0}(j)
, if
i=_{I}j
.
In this case, to each
(i,j)\in D(I)
we associate a set of transport maps.

Definition 3.9.1
.

If
I
is a set, a set-relevant family of sets indexed by
I
,
is a triplet
\Lambda^{*}:=\big(\lambda_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda},\lambda_{2})
,
where
\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}:D(I)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
, and

\lambda_{2}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\bigcurlywedge_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(j)\big),\ \ \ \lambda_{2}\big((i,j),p\big):=\lambda_{ij}^{p},\ \ \ (i,j)\in D(I),\ \ \ p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j),

such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

For every
i\in I
there is
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,i)
such that
\lambda_{ii}^{p}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(i))}\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
.

(ii)

For every
(i,j)\in D(I)
and every
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)
there is some

q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(j,i)
such that
such that the following left diagram
commutes
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(k)
.
\lambda_{ji}^{q}
\lambda_{ij}^{p}
\ \mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
\lambda_{ij}^{p}
\ \lambda_{ik}^{r}
\lambda_{jk}^{q}

(iii)

If
i=_{I}j=_{I}k
, then for every
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)

and every

there is

r\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,k)
such that the above right diagram commutes.

We call
\Lambda^{*}
function-like, if

\forall_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\forall_{p,p{{}^{\prime}}\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)}\big(p=_{\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)}p{{}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow\lambda_{ij}^{p}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(j))}\lambda_{ij}^{p{{}^{\prime}}}\big)
.

It is immediate to show that if
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, then
\Lambda

generates a set-relevant family over
I
, where

\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j):=\mathbb{1}
, and
\lambda_{2}\big((i,j),p)\big):=\lambda_{ij}
, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
.

Definition 3.9.2
.

Let
\Lambda^{*}:=(\lambda_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda},\lambda_{2})
and
M:=(\mu_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\mu},\mu_{2})
be set-relevamt families of sets over
I
.
A covariant set-relevant family-map from
\Lambda^{*}
to
M^{*}
,
in symbols
\Psi\colon\Lambda^{*}\Rightarrow M^{*}
, is a dependent operation

\Psi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mu_{0}(i)\big)

such that for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
and for every
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)
there
is
q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\mu}(i,j)
such that the following diagram commutes
\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(j)
.
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\mu_{ij}^{q}
\lambda_{ij}^{p}
\Psi_{i}
\Psi_{j}

A contravariant set-relevant family-map is defined by the property:
for every

q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\mu}(i,j)
, there is
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)
such that the above diagram commutes.
Let
\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda^{*},M^{*})
be the totality of covariant set-relevant
family-maps
from
\Lambda^{*}
to
M^{*}
, which is equipped with the pointwise equality.
If
\Xi:M^{*}\Rightarrow N^{*}
, the composition set-relevant family-map

\Xi\circ\Psi\colon\Lambda^{*}\Rightarrow N^{*}
is defined, for every
i\in I
, by

(\Xi\circ\Psi)_{i}:=\Xi_{i}\circ\Psi_{i}
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(i)
\nu_{0}(i)
\nu_{0}(j)
.
\lambda_{ij}^{p}
\Psi_{j}
\mu_{ij}^{q}
\Psi_{i}
\Xi_{i}
\Xi_{j}
\nu_{ij}^{r}
(\Xi\circ\Psi)_{i}\
\ (\Xi\circ\Psi)_{j}

The composition of contravariant set-relevant family-maps is defined similarly
The identity set-relevant family-map
is defined by
\mathrm{Id}_{\Lambda^{*}}(i):=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
, for every
i\in I
.
Let
\textnormal{{Fam}}^{*}(I)
be the totality of set-relevant
I
-families,
equipped with the obvious canonical equality.

\mathrm{Id}_{\Lambda^{*}}
is both a covariant and a contravariant set-relevant family-map from
\Lambda^{*}
to itself.

Definition 3.9.3
.

Let
\Lambda^{*}:=\big(\lambda_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda},\lambda_{2}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}^{*}(I)
.
The exterior union
\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
of
\Lambda^{*}
is
the totality
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, equipped with the following equality

(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \exists_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)}\big(\lambda_{ij}^{p}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\big).

The totality
\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
of dependent
functions over
\Lambda^{*}
is defined by

\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\Theta\in\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})\ \&\ \forall_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\forall_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)}\big(\Theta_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}^{p}(\Theta_{i})\big),

and it is equipped with the pointwise equality.

A motivation for the definitions of
\sum^{*}_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
and
\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)

is provided in Note
.

Remark 3.9.4
.

The equalities on
\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
and
\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
satisfy
the conditions of an equivalence relation.

Proof.

Let
(i,x),(j,y)
and
(k,z)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. By definition there is

p\in\varepsilon^{\lambda}_{0}(i,i)
such that
\lambda_{ii}^{p}=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
,
hence
(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)}(i,x)
.
If
(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y)
, then
j=_{I}i
and there is
q\in\varepsilon^{\lambda}_{0}(ji)
such that

\lambda_{ji}^{q}(y)=\lambda_{ji}^{q}(\lambda_{ij}^{p}(x))=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}(x):=x
,
hence
(j,y)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{g}*\lambda_{0}(i)}(i,x)
. If
(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y)

and
(j,y)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)}(k,z)
, then from the hypotheses
i=_{I}j
and
j=_{I}k
, we
get
i=_{I}k
. From the hypotheses
\exists_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)}\big(\lambda_{ij}^{p}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y\big)
and
\exists_{q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(j,k)}\big(\lambda_{jk}^{q}(y)=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}z\big)
, let
r\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,k)

such that
\lambda_{ik}^{r}=\lambda_{jk}^{q}\circ\lambda_{ij}^{p}
. Hence

\lambda_{ik}^{r}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}\lambda_{jk}^{q}(\lambda_{ij}^{p}(x))=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}\lambda_{jk}^{q}(y)=z
. The proof for the equality on
\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
is trivial.
∎

Proposition 3.9.5
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda},\lambda_{2})
,
M:=(\mu_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\mu},\mu_{2})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}^{*}(I)
, and
\Psi:\Lambda^{*}\Rightarrow M^{*}
.

(i)

For every
i\in I
the operation

e_{i}^{\Lambda^{*}}:\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

e_{i}^{\Lambda^{*}}(x):=(i,x)
, for every
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
,
is a function.

(ii)

If
\Psi
is covariant, the operation

\Sigma^{*}\Psi:\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

\Sigma^{*}\Psi(i,x):=(i,\Psi_{i}(x))
, for every
(i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
,
is a function,
such that for every

i\in I
the following left diagram commutes
\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
\sum_{i\in I}^{*}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)
\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\Sigma^{*}\Psi
\Psi_{i}
e_{i}^{\Lambda^{*}}
e_{i}^{M^{*}}
\Pi^{*}\Psi
\Psi_{i}
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda^{*}}
\pi_{i}^{M^{*}}

(iii)

If
i\in I
, the operation

\pi_{i}^{\Lambda^{*}}:\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

\Theta\mapsto\Theta_{i},
is a function.

(iv)

If
\Psi
is contravariant, the operation

\Pi^{*}\Psi:\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in I}^{*}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

[\Pi^{*}\Psi(\Theta)]_{i}:=\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i})
, for every
i\in I
,
is a function,
such that for every
i\in I
the above right diagram commutes.

(v)

If
\Psi_{i}
is an embedding, for every
i\in I
, then
\Pi^{*}\Psi
is an
embedding.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proofs of Propositions
3.2.7
and
3.3.5
.
∎

The definitions of operations on
I
-families of sets and their family-maps extend to operations
on set-relevant
I
-families and their family-maps. An important example of a set-relevant family
of sets is that of a family of sets over a set with a proof-relevant equality
(see Definition
). For reasons that are going to be clear in the study of
these families of sets, the first definitional clause of a set-relevant family of sets over
I
involves
the equality
=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(i))}
instead of the definitional one. Next follows the
definition of the direct version of a set-relevant family of sets, the importance of which is explained
in Note
5.10.4
.

Definition 3.9.6
.

If
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
is a directed set, a set-relevant direct family of sets indexed by

I
, is a quadruple
\Lambda^{*,\preccurlyeq}:=\big(\lambda_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}},\Theta,\lambda_{2}^{\preccurlyeq})
,
where
\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}:D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
\Theta\in\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j)
is a modulus of
inhabitedness for
\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}
,
and

\lambda_{2}^{\preccurlyeq}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\bigcurlywedge_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(j)\big),\ \ \lambda_{2}^{\preccurlyeq}\big((i,j),p\big):=\lambda_{ij}^{p,\preccurlyeq},\ \ (i,j)\in D(I),\ \ p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j),

such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

For every
i\in I
there is
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,i)
such that
\lambda_{ii}^{p,\preccurlyeq}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(i))}\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
.

(ii)

If
i\preccurlyeq_{I}j\preccurlyeq_{I}k
, then for every
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j)
and every

q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(j,k)
there is

such that
\lambda_{jk}^{q,\preccurlyeq}\circ\lambda_{ij}^{p,\preccurlyeq}=\lambda_{ik}^{r,\preccurlyeq}
.

(iii)

For every
(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)
and every
p,p{{}^{\prime}}\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j)

and every
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
there is
k\in I
such that
j\preccurlyeq_{I}k
and there is

q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(j,k)
such that

\lambda_{jk}^{q,\preccurlyeq}\big(\lambda_{ij}^{p,\preccurlyeq}(x)\big)=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}\lambda_{jk}^{q,\preccurlyeq}\big(\lambda_{ij}^{p{{}^{\prime}},\preccurlyeq}(x)\big).

The modulus of inhabitedness
\Theta
for
\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}
and the last condition in the
previous definition guarantee that the equality on the corresponding
\sum
-set of a set-relevant direct family of sets
satisfies the conditions of an equivalence relation.

Definition 3.9.7
.

Let
\Lambda^{*,\preccurlyeq}:=\big(\lambda_{0},\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}},\Theta,\lambda_{2}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)
a
set-relevant family of sets over a directed set
(I,\preccurlyeq)
. Its exterior union
\sum_{i\in I}^{*,\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the totality

\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
equipped with the equality

(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}^{*,\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\bigg(i,j\preccurlyeq_{I}k\ \&\ \exists_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,k)}\exists_{q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(j,k)}\big(\lambda_{ik}^{p,\preccurlyeq}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(k)}}\lambda_{jk}^{q,\preccurlyeq}(y)\big)\bigg).

The set

of dependent functions over

is defined by

\Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}^{*,\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\Phi\in\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})\ \&\ \forall_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\forall_{p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda,\preccurlyeq}(i,j)}\big(\Phi_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}^{p,\preccurlyeq}(\Phi_{i})\big),

and it is equipped with the pointwise equality.

Proposition 3.9.8

The equality on

Proof.
