3.10
Families of families of sets, an impredicative interlude

We define the notion of a family of families of sets
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
, where each family of sets

\Lambda^{i}
is indexed by some set
\mu_{0}(i)
, and
i\in I
. As expected, the index-sets are given by
some family
M\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, and
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
must must be a function-like object i.e.,
if
i=_{I}j
, the family
\Lambda^{i}
of sets over the index-set
\mu_{0}(i)
is “equal” to the
family
\Lambda^{j}
of sets over the index-set
\mu_{0}(j)
. This equality can be expressed through the
notion of a family map from
\Lambda^{i}
to
\Lambda^{j}
over
\mu_{ij}

(see Definition
3.1.5
). As in the case of the definition of a family of sets we provide the a
priori given transport maps of
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
with certain properties that guarantee the existence
of these family-maps.
As
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
is an impredicative set, to define a family of families of sets, we need to introduce, in
complete analogy to the introduction of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
, the class

\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}

of sets and
impredicative sets
. All notions of assignment routines defined in Chapter
2
are defined
in a similar way when the class
\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}
is used instead of
\mathbb{V}_{0}
. We add the superscript

im
to a symbol in order to denote the version of the corresponding notion that requires the use of
\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}
.

Definition 3.10.1
.

Let
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
and, if
(i,j)\in D(I)
let the
set

T_{ij}(M):=\{(m,n)\in\mu_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\mid\mu_{ij}(m)=_{\mu_{0}(j)}n\}.

A family of families of sets over
I
and
M
, or an
(I,M)
-family of families of sets,
is a pair

(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}:=\big(\Lambda^{0,M},\Lambda^{1,M}\big)
,
where

\Lambda^{0,M}\colon\bigcurlywedge^{\mathrm{im}}\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mu_{0}(i)),\ \ \ \ \Lambda^{0,M}_{i}:=\big(\lambda_{0}^{i},\lambda_{1}^{1}\big);\ \ \ \ i\in I,

\Lambda^{1,M}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\bigcurlywedge_{(m,n)\in T_{ij}(M)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}^{i}(m),\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)\big),

\big(\Lambda^{1,M}_{(i,j)}\big)_{(m,n)}:=\lambda_{mn}^{ij}\colon\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)\to\lambda_{0}^{j}(n);\ \ \ \ (i,j)\in D(I),\ (m,n)\in T_{ij}(M),

such that the transport maps
\lambda^{ij}_{mn}

of
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
, satisfy the following
conditions:

(i)

For every
i\in I
and
(m,m{{}^{\prime}})\in T_{ij}(M)
, we have that
\lambda^{ii}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}:=\lambda^{i}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}
.

(ii)

If
i=_{I}j=_{I}k
, for every
(m,n)\in T_{ij}(M)
and
(n,l)\in T_{jk}(M)
, the following
diagram commutes
\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)
\lambda_{0}^{k}(l)
.
\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)
\lambda^{jk}_{nl}
\lambda^{ij}_{mn}
\ \lambda^{ik}_{ml}

Let
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
be the totality of
(I,M)
-families of families of sets.

For condition (i) above, we have that
\mu_{ii}(m)=m{{}^{\prime}}:\Leftrightarrow m=_{\mu_{(}i)}m{{}^{\prime}}
and for condition (ii),
from the hypotheses
(m,n)\in T_{ij}(M):\Leftrightarrow\mu_{ij}(m)=n
and
(n,l)\in T_{jk}(M):\Leftrightarrow\mu_{jk}(n)=l

we get
(m,l)\in T_{ik}(M):\Leftrightarrow\mu_{ik}(m)=l
, as
\mu_{ik}(m)=\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))=l
. The main
intuition behind this defintion is that if
i=_{I}j
, then
\mu_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\mu_{0}(j)
, hence, if

m\in\mu_{0}(i)
and
n\in\mu_{0}(j)
, there is a transport map
\lambda^{ij}_{mn}
from
\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)

to
\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)
. It is easy to see that if
\Lambda\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J)
, then by taking
I:=\mathbb{1}
and
M

the constant family
J
over
\mathbb{1}
, then
\Lambda
can be viewed as an
(\mathbb{1},M)
-family of families of sets.

Lemma 3.10.2
.

If
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
, for its transport maps
\lambda_{mn}^{ij}
the following hold:

(i)

\lambda^{ii}_{mm}:=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}
.

(ii)

\lambda^{ij}_{mn}\circ\lambda^{ji}_{nm}=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)}
.

(iii)

If
\mu_{ij}(m)=n
, then
\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}=\lambda^{j}_{n\mu_{ij}(m)}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{mn}
\lambda^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m))
\lambda^{j}_{0}(n)
.
\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)
\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)
\lambda^{j}_{n\mu_{ij}(m)}
\lambda^{i}_{mm}
\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}
\lambda^{ij}_{mn}

(iv)

If
m=_{{\mu_{0}(i)}}m{{}^{\prime}}
, then
\lambda^{j}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}=\lambda^{ij}_{m{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\lambda^{i}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}
\lambda^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m))
\lambda^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}}))
.
\lambda^{i}_{0}(m{{}^{\prime}})
\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)
\lambda^{j}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}
\lambda^{i}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}
\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}
\lambda^{ij}_{m{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}

(v)

If
i=_{I}j=_{I}k
, then

\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{ik}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{ik}(m)}=\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}
\lambda^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m))
\lambda^{k}_{0}(\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))
.
\lambda^{k}_{0}(\mu_{ik}(m))
\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)
\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m)}
\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{ik}(m)}
\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}
\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ik}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}

Proof.

(i) By Definition
3.10.1

\lambda^{ii}_{mm}:=\lambda^{i}_{mm}:=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}
.

(ii) By the composition-rule and case (i) we get
\lambda^{ij}_{mn}\circ\lambda^{ji}_{nm}=\lambda^{jj}_{nn}=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)}
.

(iii) By the composition-rule we get

\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}=\lambda^{jj}_{n\mu_{ij}(m)}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{mn}:=\lambda^{j}_{n\mu_{ij}(m)}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{mn}
.

(iv) By Definition
3.10.1
we have that

\displaystyle\lambda^{j}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{jj}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ij}_{m{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\lambda^{ii}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{ij}_{m{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\lambda^{i}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}.

(v) If
l:=\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))
, then by case (iii)

\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{ik}(m)}=\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))\mu_{ik}(m)}\circ\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}
, hence

\displaystyle\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{ik}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{ik}(m)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{ik}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\big[\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))\mu_{ik}(m)}\circ\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\big]

\displaystyle:=\big[\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{ik}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))\mu_{ik}(m)}\big]\circ\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}.\qed

Definition 3.10.3
.

If
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
, and based on Definition
3.1.5
, its transport
family-maps
\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}
are the
family-maps
\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}\colon\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{ij}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\Lambda_{j}^{0,M}
,
defined by the rule

\big[\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}\big]_{m}:=\lambda_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}^{ij};\ \ \ \ m\in\mu_{0}(i),(i,j)\in D(I).

The fact that
\lambda_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}^{ij}\colon\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{ij}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\Lambda_{j}^{0,M}

is shown by the commutativity of the diagram in case (iv) of Lemma
3.10.2
. In analogy to
the transport maps
\lambda_{ij}
of an
I
-family of sets
\Lambda
, the transport family-maps

\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}
witness the equality between
the
\mu_{0}(i)
-family of sets
\Lambda^{0,M}_{i}
and the
\mu_{0}(j)
-family of sets
\Lambda_{j}^{0,M}
.

Definition 3.10.4
.

If
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
,
its exterior union
\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)

is defined by

w\in\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m):\Leftrightarrow\exists_{i\in I}\exists_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\exists_{x\in\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}\big(w:=(i,m,x)\big),

(i,m,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}}(j,n,y):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \mu_{ij}(m)=_{{\mu_{0}(j)}}n\ \&\ \lambda^{ij}_{mn}(x)=_{{\lambda^{j}_{0}(n)}}y.

Remark 3.10.5
.

The equality on
\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)
satisfies the conditions of
an equivalence relation.

Proof.

To show
(i,m,x)=(i,m,x):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}i\ \&\ \mu_{ii}(m)=m\ \&\ \lambda^{ii}_{mm}(x)=x
,
we use Lemma
3.10.2
(i). If
(i,m,x)=(j,n,y):\Leftrightarrow i=j\ \&\ \mu_{ij}(m)=n\ \&\ \lambda^{ij}_{mn}(x)=y,
then
j=i
and
\mu_{ji}(n)=m
, and, using Lemma
3.10.2
(ii),

\lambda^{ji}_{nm}(y)=\lambda^{ji}_{nm}\big(\lambda^{ij}_{mn}(x)\big)=\lambda^{ii}_{mm}(x)=x
i.e.,

(j,n,y)=(i,m,x)
. If
(i,m,x)=(j,n,y)
and
(j,n,xy=(k,l,z):\Leftrightarrow j=k\ \&\ \mu_{jk}(n)=l\ \&\ \lambda^{jk}_{nl}(y)=z,
then
i=k
and
\mu_{ik}(m)=\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))=\mu_{jk}(n)=l
,
and
\lambda^{ik}_{ml}(x)=\lambda^{jk}_{nl}\big(\lambda^{ij}_{mn}(x)\big)=\lambda^{jk}_{nl}(y)=z
i.e.,

(i,m,x)=(k,l,z)
.
∎

Proposition 3.10.6
.

If
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
, then
\Sigma:=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, where

\sigma_{0}(i):=\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m);\ \ \ \ i\in I,

\sigma_{1}(i,j):=\sigma_{ij}\colon\bigg(\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)\bigg)\to\sum_{n\in\mu_{0}(j)}\lambda^{j}_{0}(n);\ \ \ \ (i,j)\in D(I),

\sigma_{ij}(m,x):=\big(\mu_{ij}(m),\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}(x)\big);\ \ \ \ m\in\mu_{0}(i),\ x\in\lambda_{0}^{i}(m).

Proof.

First we show that the operation
\sigma_{ij}

is a function. We suppose that
(m,x)=_{{\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)}}(m{{}^{\prime}},x{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow m=_{{\mu_{0}(i)}}m{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ \lambda^{i}_{mm{{}^{\prime}}}(x)=_{{\lambda^{i}_{0}(m{{}^{\prime}})}}x
,
and we show that

\big(\mu_{ij}(m),\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}(x)\big)=_{{\sum_{n\in\mu_{0}(j)}\lambda^{j}_{0}(n)}}\big(\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}}),\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}(x{{}^{\prime}})\big)\Leftrightarrow

\mu_{ij}(m)=_{{\mu_{0}(j)}}\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})\ \&\ \lambda^{j}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}\big(\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}(x)\big)=_{{\lambda^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}}))}}\lambda^{ij}_{m{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ij}(m{{}^{\prime}})}(x{{}^{\prime}}).

The first conjunct follows from
m=_{{\mu_{0}(i)}}m{{}^{\prime}}
, and the second is
Lemma
3.10.2
(iv). Since

\sigma_{ii}(m,x):=\big(\mu_{ii}(m),\lambda^{ii}_{m\mu_{ii}(m)}(x)\big):=(m,\lambda^{ii}_{mm}(x)):=(m,\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}(x)):=(m,x),

we get
\sigma_{ii}:=\mathrm{id}_{\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)}
. For the commutativity of the diagram
\sum_{n\in\mu_{0}(j)}\lambda^{j}_{0}(n)
\sum_{l\in\mu_{0}(k)}\lambda^{k}_{0}(l)
\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)
\sigma_{jk}
\sigma_{ij}
\ \sigma_{ik}

we have that by definition

\sigma_{ik}(m,x):=\big(\mu_{ik}(m),\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{ik}(m)}(x)\big),
and

\displaystyle\sigma_{jk}\big(\sigma_{ij}(m,x)\big)

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{jk}\big(\mu_{ij}(m),\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}(x)\big)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m)),\ \lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\big(\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}(x)\big)\bigg).

Hence,

\sigma_{ik}(m,x)=_{{\sum_{l\in\mu_{0}(k)}\lambda^{k}_{0}(l)}}\sigma_{jk}\big(\sigma_{ij}(m,x)\big):\Leftrightarrow\mu_{ik}(m)=_{{\mu_{0}(k)}}\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))
and

\lambda^{k}_{\mu_{ik}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\big(\lambda^{ik}_{m\mu_{ik}(m)}(x)\big)=\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{ij}(m)\mu_{jk}(\mu_{ij}(m))}\big(\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}(x)\big).

The first conjunct is immediate to show, and the second is exactly Lemma
3.10.2
(v).
∎

Clearly, for the exterior union of
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
we have that

\sum_{i\in I}\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\sum_{i\in I}\sigma_{0}(i):=\sum_{i\in I}\bigg(\sum_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda^{i}_{0}(m)\bigg).

If
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
and
(M^{i})_{i\in I}
are
(I,M)
-families of families, a map
from
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
to
(M^{i})_{i\in I}
is an appropriate dependent function
(\Psi^{i})_{i\in I}

such that
\Psi^{i}
is a family map from
\Lambda^{i}
to
M^{i}
, for every
i\in I
. Before giving this definition
we show a fact of independent interest.

Proposition 3.10.7
.

Let
(K^{i})_{i\in I}:=(K^{0,M},K^{1,M}),(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}:=(\Lambda^{0,M},\Lambda^{1,M})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
,
and let
i=_{I}j
.

(i)

The operation
\gamma_{ij}:\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0,M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)\rightsquigarrow\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{j}^{0,M},\Lambda_{j}^{0,M}\big)
, defined by the rule

\Psi^{i}\mapsto\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]^{j}
, is a function, where, for every
n\in\mu_{0}(j)
, the
map
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n}^{j}\colon\kappa_{0}^{j}(n)\to\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)
is defined by

\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n}^{j}:=\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}
\kappa^{i}_{0}(\mu_{ji}(n))
\lambda^{i}_{0}(\mu_{ji}(n))
.
\lambda^{j}_{0}(n)
\kappa^{j}_{0}(n)
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n}^{j}
\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}
\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}

(ii)

The pair
\Gamma:=(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, where
\gamma_{0}(i):=\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0,M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)
, for every
i\in I
, and
\gamma_{1}(i,j):=\gamma_{ij}
, for every

(i,j)\in D(I)
.

Proof.

(i) First we show that
\gamma_{ij}
is well-defined i.e.,
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]^{j}\in\textnormal{{Map}}(K^{j},\Lambda^{j})
. If
n,n{{}^{\prime}}\in\mu_{0}(j)
, we show that the following diagram commutes
\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)
\lambda_{0}^{j}(n{{}^{\prime}})
.
\kappa_{0}^{j}(n{{}^{\prime}})
\kappa_{0}^{j}(n)
\lambda^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}
\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n}^{j}
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n{{}^{\prime}}}^{j}

By definition we have that

\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n{{}^{\prime}}}^{j}\circ\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}:=\big[\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})n{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\big]\circ\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}
.
Since
\Psi^{i}
is in
\textnormal{{Map}}(K_{i}^{0,M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M})
, by the commutativity of the following diagram
\lambda_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ji}(n))
\lambda_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}}))
,
\kappa_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}}))
\kappa_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ji}(n))
\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}
\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}

we get
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}=\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}
, and hence

\displaystyle\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}

\displaystyle=\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\big(\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n)}\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\big)\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n)}

\displaystyle=\big(\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\big)\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n)},

\displaystyle\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle:=\big[\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})n{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\big(\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\big)\big]\circ\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\big[\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})n{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\big(\big(\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\big)\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n)}\big)\big]\circ\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle:=\big(\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})n{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)\mu_{ji}(n{{}^{\prime}})}\big)\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\big(\kappa^{ji}_{n{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\big(\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}\big)

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{j}_{nn{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]_{n}^{j}.

If
\Psi^{i}=\Phi^{i}
, we show that
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]^{j}=\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Phi^{i})\big]^{j}
.
As

[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})]_{n}^{j}:=\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}\ \ \ \&\ \ \ [\gamma_{ij}(\Phi^{i})]_{n}^{j}:=\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}\circ\Phi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)},

and since
\Psi^{i}=\Phi^{i}
, we get
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}=\Phi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}
, and hence the
following diagram commutes
\kappa_{0}^{j}(n)
\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)
.
\lambda_{0}^{j}(n)
\kappa_{0}^{j}(n)
\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}\circ\Phi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}
\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(n)n}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(n)}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{n\mu_{ji}(n)}
\kappa^{j}_{nn}
\lambda^{j}_{nn}

(ii) If
m\in\mu_{0}(i)
, then
[\gamma_{ii}(\Psi^{i})]_{m}^{i}:=\lambda^{ii}_{\mu_{ii}(m)m}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ii}(m)}\circ\kappa^{ii}_{m\mu_{ii}(m)}:=\lambda^{ii}_{mm}\circ\Psi^{i}_{m}\circ\kappa^{ii}_{mm}:=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}\circ\Psi^{i}_{m}\circ\mathrm{id}_{\kappa_{0}^{i}(m)}:=\Psi^{i}_{m},

hence
[\gamma_{ii}(\Psi^{i})]_{m}^{i}:=\Psi^{i}_{m}
, and consequently
\big[\gamma_{ii}(\Psi^{i})\big]^{i}:=\Psi^{i}
. For the commutativity of the diagram
\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{j}^{0,M},\Lambda_{j}^{0,M}\big)
\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{k}^{0,M},\Lambda_{k}^{0,M}\big)
\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0,M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)
\gamma_{jk}
\gamma_{ij}
\ \gamma_{ik}

we need to show the equality between the maps

\chi^{k}_{l}:=\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{kj}(l)l}\circ\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]^{j}_{\mu_{kj}(l)}\circ\kappa^{kj}_{l\mu_{kj}(l)}\ \ \ \&\ \ \ \upsilon^{k}_{l}:=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ki}(l)l}\circ\Psi^{j}_{\mu_{ki}(l)}\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ki}(l)}.

By the definition of
\big[\gamma_{ij}(\Psi^{i})\big]^{j}_{\mu_{kj}(l)}
we get

\displaystyle\chi^{k}_{l}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{kj}(l)l}\circ\big(\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))\mu_{kj}(l)}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\kappa^{ji}_{\mu_{kj}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\big)\circ\kappa^{kj}_{l\mu_{kj}(l)}

\displaystyle=\big(\lambda^{jk}_{\mu_{kj}(l)l}\circ\lambda^{ij}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))\mu_{kj}(l)}\big)\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\big(\kappa^{ji}_{\mu_{kj}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\kappa^{kj}_{l\mu_{kj}(l)}\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))l}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}.

By the supposed commutativity of the following diagram
\lambda_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ki}(l))
\lambda_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l)))
,
\kappa_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l)))
\kappa_{0}^{i}(\mu_{ki}(l))
\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ki}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}
\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ki}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ki}(l)}
\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}

\displaystyle\upsilon^{k}_{l}

\displaystyle:=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ki}(l)l}\circ\Psi^{j}_{\mu_{ki}(l)}\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ki}(l)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))l}\circ\big(\lambda^{i}_{\mu_{ki}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\Psi^{j}_{\mu_{ki}(l)}\big)\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ki}(l)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))l}\circ\big(\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ki}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\big)\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ki}(l)}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))l}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\big(\kappa^{i}_{\mu_{ki}(l)\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ki}(l)}\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{ik}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))l}\circ\Psi^{i}_{\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}\circ\kappa^{ki}_{l\mu_{ji}(\mu_{kj}(l))}

\displaystyle=\chi^{k}_{l}.\qed

Definition 3.10.8
.

If
(K^{i})_{i\in I},(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
, a family of families-map
from
(K^{i})_{i\in I}
to
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
, in symbols

\Psi\colon(K^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
,
is a dependent operation

\Psi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0,M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)

such that for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
the following diagram commutes
\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}
\Lambda_{j}^{0,M}
,
K_{j}^{0,M}
K_{i}^{0,M}
\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}
\Phi_{ij}^{K}
\Psi^{i}
\Psi^{j}

where
\Phi_{ij}^{K}
and
\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}
are the transport family-maps of

(K^{i})_{i\in I}
and
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
, respectively, according to
Definition
3.10.3
. If
\Xi\colon(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(N^{i})_{i\in I}
,
the composition
\Xi\circ\Psi\colon(K^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(N^{i})_{i\in I}
is defined, for every
i\in I
, by

(\Xi\circ\Psi)^{i}:=\Xi^{i}\circ\Psi^{i}
K_{i}^{0,M}\
K_{j}^{0,M}
\ \Lambda_{j}^{0,M}
\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\
N_{i}^{0,M}
N_{j}^{0,M}
.
\Phi_{ij}^{K}
\Psi^{j}
\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}
\Psi^{i}
\Xi^{i}
\Xi^{j}
\Phi_{ij}^{N}
(\Xi\circ\Psi)^{i}\
\ (\Xi\circ\Psi)^{j}

The identity family of families-map

\mathrm{Id}_{(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}}
is defined by the
rule
\big[\mathrm{Id}_{(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}}\big]^{i}:=\mathrm{Id}_{\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}}
, for every
i\in I
.
The totality of family of families-maps from
(K^{i})_{i\in I}
to
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
, and
the canonical equality on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
is defined in analogy to Definition
3.1.3
.

If
\Psi\colon(K^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
, the commutativity of the
diagram in Definition
3.10.8
is unfolded as follows. If
i=_{I}j
and
m\in\mu_{0}(i)
, then

\displaystyle\big[\Psi^{j}\circ\Phi_{ij}^{K}\big]_{m}=\big[\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}\circ\Psi^{i}\big]_{m}

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\Psi_{\mu_{ij}(m)}^{j}\circ\big[\Phi_{ij}^{K}\big]_{m}=\big[\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}\big]_{m}\circ\Psi_{m}^{i}

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\Psi_{\mu_{ij}(m)}^{j}\circ\kappa^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}=\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}\circ\Psi_{m}^{i}

i.e., the following diagram commutes
\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)
\lambda^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m))
.
\kappa^{j}_{0}(\mu_{ij}(m))
\kappa^{i}_{0}(m)\
\lambda^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}
\kappa^{ij}_{m\mu_{ij}(m)}
\Psi^{i}_{m}
\Psi^{j}_{\mu_{ij}(m)}

In analogy to Corollary
3.3.4
we have the following.

Corollary 3.10.9
.

If
(K^{i})_{i\in I},(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
and

\Psi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

\Psi\colon(K^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
.

(ii)

\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)
.

Proof.

If
i=_{I}j
, the commutativity of the diagram in the definition of a family of
families-map
\Psi\colon(K^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}
is equivalent to
the membership condition
\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big)

using the above unfolding of the equality
\big[\Psi^{j}\circ\Phi_{ij}^{K}\big]_{m}=\big[\Phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}\circ\Psi^{i}\big]_{m}
.
∎

Definition 3.10.10
.

The totality
\prod_{i\in I}\prod_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)
of dependent functions over
a family of families of set
(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M)
is defined by

\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}\prod_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m):\Leftrightarrow\Theta\colon\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\bigcurlywedge_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)\ \&\ \forall_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\forall_{(m,n)\in T_{ij}(M)}\big(\Theta^{j}_{n}=_{\lambda^{j}_{0}(n)}\lambda^{ij}_{mn}(\Theta^{i}_{m})\big),

\Theta=_{{\prod_{i\in I}\prod_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}}\Phi:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{m\in\mu_{0}(i)}\big(\Theta^{i}_{m}=_{\lambda_{0}^{i}(m)}\Phi^{i}_{m}\big).

The theory of families of families of sets over
(I,M)
within
\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}
can be developed
further along the lines of the
theory of families of sets over
I
within
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.

3.11
Notes

Note 3.11.1
.

The concept of a family of sets indexed by a (discrete) set was asked to be defined
in
[
9
]
, Exercise 2, p. 72, and the required definition, given by Richman,
is included in
[
19
]
, Exercise 2, p. 78, where the discreteness hypothesis is omitted.
The definition has a strong type-theoretic flavour, although, Richman’s motivation had categorical origin,
rather than type-theoretic. In a personal communication regarding this definition, Richman
referred to the definition of a set-indexed family of objects of a category, given in
[
76
]
, p. 18,
as the source of the definition attributed to him in
[
19
]
, p. 78.
Given the categorical flavour of Bishop’s notion of a subset, it might be that Bishop was also
thinking in categorical terms, although Bishop, to our knowledge, neither used a purely categorical language to describe his concepts, nor he used general category theory as a foundational framework for
\mathrm{BISH}
.

Specifically, in
[
76
]
Richman
presented a set
I
as a category with objects its elements and

\mathrm{Hom}_{=_{I}}(i,j):=\{x\in\{0\}\mid i=_{I}j\},

for every
i,j\in I
. If we view
\mathbb{V}_{0}

as a category with objects its elements and

\mathrm{Hom}_{=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}}(X,Y):=\big\{(f,f{{}^{\prime}}):\mathbb{F}(X,Y)\times\mathbb{F}(Y,X)\mid(f,f{{}^{\prime}}):X=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}Y\big\},

for every
X,Y\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
, then
an
I
-family of sets is a functor from the category
I
to the category
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.
Notice that in the definitions of
\mathrm{Hom}_{=_{I}}(i,j)

and of
\mathrm{Hom}_{=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}}(X,Y)
the properties
P(x):=i=_{I}j
and
Q(f,f{{}^{\prime}}):=(f,f{{}^{\prime}}):X=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}Y
are extensional.
In
[
95
]
we reformulated Richman’s definition using the universe

\mathbb{V}_{0}
of sets and the universe
\mathbb{V}_{1}
of triplets
(A,B,f)
, where
A,B\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
and
f\colon A\to B
.
Definition
3.1.1
rests on the notion of dependent operation, in order to be absolutely
faithful to Bishop’s account of sets and functions in
[
9
]
and
[
19
]
.
For the definition of the concept of a family of sets in
\mathrm{ZF}
, or
\mathrm{CZF}
, see
[
82
]
, p. 35, and Note
1.3.4
.

The term “transport map” in Definition
3.1.1
is drawn from MLTT. Actually, Definition
3.1.1

is a “definitional form” of the type-theoretic transport i.e., the existence of the transport map

p_{*}\colon P(x)\to P(y)
, where
p\colon x=_{A}y
and
P\colon A\to\mathcal{U}
is a type-family over
A\colon\mathcal{U}
in
the universe of types
\mathcal{U}
.
In
\mathrm{MLTT}
the existence of
p_{*}
follows from Martin-Löf’s
J
-rule, the induction principle that
accommodates the indentity type-family
=_{A}\colon A\to A\to\mathcal{U}
, for every type
A\colon\mathcal{U}
.
In Definition
3.1.1
we describe in a proof-irrelevant way i.e., using only the fact that
i=_{I}j
and not referring to witnesses of this equality, a structure of transport maps. This structure in
\mathrm{BST}
is defined, and not generated from the equality type family of
\mathrm{MLTT}
.

Note 3.11.2
.

In the categorical setting of Richman (see Note
3.11.1
), a family map
\Psi\in\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,M)
is
a natural transformation from the functor
\Lambda
to the functor
M
. The fact that the most fundamental
concepts of category theory, that of a functor and of a natural transformation, are formulated in a
natural way in
\mathrm{BST}
through the notion of a dependent operation explains why category theory
is so closely connected to
\mathrm{BST}
. For more on the connections between
\mathrm{BST}
, dependent type theory and category theory see section
7.1
.

Note 3.11.3
.

The exterior union, is necessary to the definition of the infinite product of a sequence of sets.
In
[
19
]
, p. 125, the following is noted:

Within the main body of this text, we have only defined the product
of a family of subsets of a given set. However, with the aid of
Problem 2 of Chapter 3 we can define the product of an arbitrary
sequence of sets. Definition (1.7) then applies to such a product
3
3

3

This is the
definition of the countable
product of metric spaces.
.

Note 3.11.4
.

If
\Lambda^{{\mathbb{N}}}
is the sequence of sets defined in Definition
3.1.2
,
the definitional clauses of the corresponding exterior union can be written as follows:

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}=:\{(n,x)\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ \&\ x\in X_{n}\},

(n,x)=_{\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}}(m,y):\Leftrightarrow n=_{{\mathbb{N}}}m\ \&\ x=_{X_{n}}y.

Traditionally, the countable product of this sequence of sets is defined by

\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}:=\bigg\{\phi\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}\mid\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(\phi(n)\in X_{n}\big)\bigg\},

which is a rough writing of the following

\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}:=\bigg\{\phi\colon{\mathbb{N}}\to\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}\mid\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\phi(n))=_{{\mathbb{N}}}n\big)\bigg\}.

In the second writing

\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\phi(n))=_{{\mathbb{N}}}n
implies that
\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\phi(n):=n
,
hence, if
\phi(n):=(m,y)
, then
m=n
and
y\in X_{n}
. When the equality of
I
though, is not like
that of
{\mathbb{N}}
, we cannot solve this problem in a satisfying way. Although Bishop did not consider
products other than countable ones, in more abstract areas of mathematics, like e.g., the general topology
of Bishop spaces, arbitrary products are considered (see
[
88
]
). One could have defined

\Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\Phi\in\mathbb{F}\bigg(I,\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\ \&\ \forall_{i\in I}\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi(i)):=i\big).

This approach has the problem that
the property

Q(\Phi):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}(\Phi(i)):=i\big)

is not necessarily extensional; let
\Phi=_{{\mathbb{F}(I,\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i))}}\Theta
i.e.,

\forall_{i\in I}\big(\Phi(i)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\Theta(i)\big)
, and suppose that

Q(\Phi)
. If we fix some
i\in I
, and
\Phi(i):=(i,x)
and
\Theta(i):=(j,y)
, we only
get that
j=_{I}i
. The use of dependent operations allows us to
define the right analogue to the
\prod
-type of
\mathrm{MLTT}
and being at the same time compatible
with the use of dependent operations by Bishop in
[
9
]
, p. 65.

Note 3.11.5
.

A precise formulation of the definition in
[
19
]
, p. 85,
of the countable product of a sequence
\big(X_{n},\rho_{n}\big)_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
metric spaces, where
\rho_{n}
is bounded by
1
, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, is the following. Let

\Lambda^{{\mathbb{N}}}:=(\lambda_{0}^{{\mathbb{N}}},\lambda_{1}^{{\mathbb{N}}})
be the
{\mathbb{N}}
-family of the sets
(X_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
(see
Definition
3.1.2
). Notice that the dependent operation
\lambda_{1}^{{\mathbb{N}}}
is compatible to
the corresponding metric structures in the sense that each transport map
\lambda^{{\mathbb{N}}}_{nn}:=\mathrm{id}_{X_{n}}
is a
morphism in any category of metric spaces considered. This is an example of a
spectrum of metric spaces

over
\Lambda^{{\mathbb{N}}}
(see also the introduction to section
5.1
). The

countable product metric

\rho_{\infty}

on
\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}
, for every
\Phi,\Theta\in\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}
, is defined by

\rho_{\infty}(\Phi,\Theta):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho_{n}\big(\Phi_{n},\Theta_{n}\big)}{2^{n}}.

Note 3.11.6
.

The equality

\Phi_{j}=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\lambda_{ij}(\Phi_{i})

in Definition
3.3.1
is the proof-irrelevant version of dependent application of a dependent function in
\mathrm{MLTT}

(see also Note
).

Note 3.11.7
.

As it is mentioned in
[
84
]
, the axiom of choice is “freely used in Bishop constructivism”. In
Theorem
3.6.4
we show only the formal version of the type-theoretic axiom choice within
\mathrm{BST}
i.e., the
the distributivity of
\prod
over
\sum
. This term was suggested to us by M. Maietti. In
[
95
]
a
proof of this result is also given, where dependecy is formulated with the help of the universe
\mathbb{V}_{1}

of triplets
(A,B,f)
(see Note
3.11.1
).
As it was first noted to us by E. Palmgren, this distributivity
holds in every locally cartesian closed category. In
[
130
]
it is mentioned that this fact
is attributed to Martin-Löf and his work
[
73
]
. For a proof see
[
2
]
.

Note 3.11.8
.

The notion of an
I
-set of sets is in accordance with Bishop’s predicative spirit, and his need to avoid
the treatment of the universe
\mathbb{V}_{0}
as a set. This notion was not defined by Bishop, only its “internal” version, the notion
of an
I
-set of subsets, was defined similarly by him in
[
9
]
, p. 65. The use of the term “set of subsets” was
a source of misreading of
[
9
]
from the side of Myhill in
[
80
]
(see also Notes
6.6.5

and
6.6.7
).
The definition of the set
\lambda_{0}I
is in the spirit of the definition of the quotient group
G/H
of the group

G
by its normal subgroup
H
, given in
[
76
]
, p. 38.
If
I
is equipped with the equality
=_{I}^{\Lambda}
, then
\Lambda
does not become necessarily an

I
-set of sets. The reason for this is that the transport maps of
\Lambda
are given beforehand, and if we equip
I

with
=_{I}^{\Lambda}
we need to add a transport map
\lambda_{ij}
for every pair
(i,j)
for which
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
and
(i,j)\notin D(I)
, where
D(I)
is understood here as the diagonal
D(I,=_{I})
with respect to the
equality
=_{I}
. So,
\lambda_{1}
has to be extended, and define a new family of sets over
(I,=_{I}^{\Lambda})
,
which is going
to be an
(I,=_{I}^{\Lambda})
-set of sets.

Note 3.11.9
.

A direct family of sets is a useful variation of the notion of a set-indexed family of sets (see Chapter
5
).
A directed set
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
can also be seen as
a category with objects
the elements of
I
, and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\preccurlyeq_{I}}(i,j):=\{x\in\{0\}\mid i\preccurlyeq_{I}j\}.

If the universe
\mathbb{V}_{0}
is seen as a category with objects its elements and

\mathrm{Hom}_{\preccurlyeq_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}}(X,Y):=\mathbb{F}(X,Y),
an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-family of sets is a
functor from the category
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
to this new category
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.

Note 3.11.10
.

A generalisation of the notion of a direct family of sets is that of a preorder family of sets. If
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
is a
preorder (see Definition
8.2.1
), a
covariant preorder family of sets

over
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
is defined as a direct family of sets. One needs though the
property of a directed set to define an interesting equality on the exterior union of the corresponding family.
A
contravariant preorder family of sets
over
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
,
or an
(I,\succcurlyeq_{I})
-family of
sets
, is a pair

M^{\succcurlyeq}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq})
, where if
(j,i)\in D^{\succcurlyeq}(I)
, the transport maps

\mu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq}(j,i)\colon\mu_{0}(j)\to\mu_{0}(i)
behave in a dual way i.e., for every

i,j,k\in I
with
k\succcurlyeq_{I}j\succcurlyeq_{I}i
, the following diagram commutes
\mu_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(k).
\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{jk}^{\succcurlyeq}
\mu_{ij}^{\succcurlyeq}
\ \mu_{ik}^{\succcurlyeq}

If
(I,\preccurlyeq)
is an inverse-directed set (see Definition
8.2.1
) and
M^{\succcurlyeq}
is an

(I,\succcurlyeq_{I})
-contravariant direct family of sets, defined in the obvious way, the

inverse-direct sum

\sum_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mu_{0}(i)

of
M^{\succcurlyeq}
is the totality
\sum_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, equipped with the equality

(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mu_{0}(i)}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\big(i\succcurlyeq_{I}k\ \&\ j\succcurlyeq_{I}k\ \&\ \mu_{ik}^{\succcurlyeq}(x)=_{\mu_{0}(k)}\mu_{jk}^{\succcurlyeq}(y)\big).

The set
\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mu_{0}(i)
is defined in the expected way.
Thinking classically, a topology
T
of
open sets on a set
X
, equipped with the subset order
\subseteq
, is an inverse-directed set, and the notion of
a
presheaf of sets
on
(X,T)
is an example of a
(T,\supseteq)
-contravariant direct family of sets.
In the language of presheaves (see
[
65
]
, p. 72) the transport maps
\mu_{ij}^{\succcurlyeq}
are called

restriction maps
, and a family-map
\Phi\colon\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}\Rightarrow M^{\succcurlyeq}
is called a morphism of presheaves.
It is natural to use also the term
extension map
for the transport map
\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}
of a
covariant (direct) preorder family of sets.
The notion of a family of sets over a partial order is also used in the definition of a Kripke model
for intuitionistic predicate logic. For that see
[
127
]
, p. 85, where the
transport maps
\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}
are called there
transition functions
.

Note 3.11.11
.

If a set-relevant family-map
\Psi\colon\Lambda^{*}\Rightarrow M^{*}
was defined by the stronger condition: for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
, every
p\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)
and
every

q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\mu}(i,j)
the diagram in Definition
3.9.2
commutes, then the expected fact
\mathrm{id}_{\Lambda^{*}}\colon\Lambda^{*}\Rightarrow\Lambda^{*}
implies that
\lambda_{ij}^{p}=\lambda_{ij}^{q}
, for every
p,q\in\varepsilon_{0}^{\lambda}(i,j)
. This property is called proof-irrelevance in Definition
.

Note 3.11.12
.

The theory of families of families of sets over
(I,M)
within
\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}
is the third rung of the
ladder of set-like objects in
\mathbb{V}_{0}^{\mathrm{im}}
. The first three rungs can be described as follows:

X,Y\in\mathbb{V}_{0},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ f\colon X\to Y,

\Lambda,M\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I),\ \ \ \ \Psi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow M\Leftrightarrow\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mu_{0}(i)\big),

(K^{i})_{i\in I},(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,M),\ \ \ \ \Psi\colon(K^{i})_{i\in I}\Rightarrow(\Lambda^{i})_{i\in I}\Leftrightarrow\Psi\in\prod_{i\in I}\textnormal{{Map}}\big(K_{i}^{0,M},\Lambda_{i}^{0,M}\big).

This hierarchy of universes and families can be extended further, if necessary.

Note 3.11.13

(Small categories within
\mathrm{BST}
)
.

As it is mentioned in the introduction to Chapter 9 of
[
126
]
, where category theory is developed within
\mathrm{HoTT}
, categories do not fit well with set-based mathematics. Quit earlier, see e.g, in
[
61
]
, it is mentioned that “type theory is adequate to represent faithfully categorical reasoning”. In
[
61
]
the objects are modelled as types and the Hom-sets as Hom-setoids of arrows, within the Calculus of Inductive Constructions. In
[
87
]
there are elements of such a development of category theory within type theory, where both the algebraic and the hom-definition are given. In
[
86
]
are included interesting remarks on the formulation of category theory in
[
126
]
. For relations between category theory and Explicit Mathematics see
[
64
]
. In this note we briefly explain why small categories fit well with
\mathrm{BST}
.

As we have already explained in Note
3.11.1
, Richman used the notion of a functor to define the fundamental notion of a set-indexed family of sets, as a special case of a set-indexed family of objects in some category
\mathcal{C}
.
Here we do the opposite. The notion of a set-indexed family of sets is fundamental and comes first.
We use the basic theory of set-indexed families of sets to describe the basic notions of category theory within
\mathrm{BST}
.
In what follows we consider the objects of a category to be a set, although that could also be a class. The totality of arrows
is always a set i.e., we could study locally small categories, but here we only present small categories.
A set is not necessarily in the homotopy sense of the book-HoTT (see the corresponding notion
of a strict category in
[
126
]
, section 9.6).
At this point we do not equip
\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
with equality with evidence
(\mathrm{EwE})
that makes
possible the formulation of precategory and category in the sense of the book-HoTT (see section
).
For a general discussion on the relations between categories and sets in
\mathrm{BST}
see section
7.1
.

Definition 3.11.14
.

A
(
small
)
category is a structure
\mathcal{C}:=\big(\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}\big)
, where
\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a set,
\big(\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}})
,

\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}:\bigcurlywedge_{x,y,z\in\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}}\mathbb{F}\big(\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(y,z)\times\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y),\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,z)\big),

\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xyz}:=\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}(x,y,z):\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(y,z)\times\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y)\to\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,z),\ \ \ \ \mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xyz}(\phi,\psi):=\phi\circ\psi,

\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}:\bigcurlywedge_{x\in\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}}\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,x),\ \ \ \ \mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}_{x}:=\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}(x);\ \ \ \ x\in\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}},

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(\mathrm{Cat}_{1})
For every
x,y,z,w\in\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\chi\in\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(z,w),\phi\in\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(y,z)
,

\psi\in\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y)
,

\displaystyle\chi\circ(\phi\circ\psi)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xzw}\big(\chi,\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xyz}(\phi,\psi)\big)

\displaystyle=_{{\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,w)}}\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xyw}\big(\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{yzw}(\chi,\phi),\psi\big)

\displaystyle:=(\chi\circ\phi)\circ\psi.

(\mathrm{Cat}_{2})
For every
x,y\in\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
, and for every
\psi\in\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y)
,

\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}_{y}\circ\psi:=\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xyy}(\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}_{y},\psi)=_{{\mathrm{Mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y)}}\psi\ \ \ \&\ \ \ \psi\circ\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}_{x}:=\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}}_{xxy}(\psi,\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}_{x},\psi)=_{{\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y)}}\psi.

(\mathrm{Cat}_{3})
For every
x,y,z,x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}},z{{}^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
, with
x=_{{\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}}}x{{}^{\prime}}
,

y=_{{\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}}}y{{}^{\prime}}
and
z=_{{\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}}}z{{}^{\prime}}
, for
every
\psi\in\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x,y)
,

\phi\in\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(y,z)
,

\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x,z)(x{{}^{\prime}},z{{}^{\prime}})}(\phi\circ\psi)=_{{\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},z{{}^{\prime}})}}\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{(y,z)(y{{}^{\prime}},z{{}^{\prime}})}(\phi)\circ\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y{{}^{\prime}})}(\psi)

The last two conditions, which reflect a functorial behaviour of the transport maps of
\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{1}
and are not
found in the standard definition of a category, are necessary
compatibility conditions between these transport maps and the
\big(\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}\big)
-structure
of the category

\mathcal{C}
. While in intensional
\mathrm{MLTT}
these conditions follow from the transport, hence the
J
-rule, here we need to include them in our definition.

As a characteristic example of a category in the above sense, we consider the constructive analogue to the category of posets.
Classically, the category of posets has objects the collection of all posets and arrows the monotone functions.
In order to formulate this constructively, we need to generalise Definition
3.11.14
to categories with
objects an abstract totality
\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
. In Definition
3.11.15
we define the category generated
by a spectrum of posets. We can define similarly the category generated by a spectrum of groups, rings, modules etc.
(for the notion of an
S
-spectrum, where
S
is a structure on a set
X
, see the introduction to section
5.1
).

Definition 3.11.16

Proposition 3.11.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}:=\big(\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{0},\mathrm{mor}^{\mathcal{C}}_{1},\mathrm{Comp}^{\mathcal{C}},\mathrm{Id}^{\mathcal{C}}\big)

and

Chapter 4
Families of subsets

We develop the basic theory of set-indexed families of subsets and of the corresponding
family-maps between them. In contrast to set-indexed families of sets, the properties of which
are determined “externally”
through their transport maps, the properties of a set-indexed family
\Lambda(X)
of subsets of a given set
X

are determined “internally” through the embeddings of the subsets of
\Lambda(X)
to
X
. The interior union of

\Lambda(X)
is the internal analogue to the
\sum
-set of a set-indexed family of sets
\Lambda
,
and the intersection of
\Lambda(X)
is the internal analogue to the
\prod
-set of
\Lambda
.
Families of sets over products, sets of subsets, and direct families of subsets are the internal analogue to the
corresponding notions for families of sets. Set-indexed families of partial functions
and set-indexed families of complemented subsets, together with their corresponding family-maps, are studied.

4.1
Set-indexed families of subsets

Roughly speaking, a family of subsets of a set
X
indexed by some set
I
is an assignment routine

\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{P}(X)
that behaves like a function i.e., if
i=_{I}j
, then
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)
. The following definition is a formulation of this rough description that reveals the witnesses of the
equality
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)
. This is done “internally”, through the embeddings of the subsets
into
X
. The equality
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\lambda_{0}(j)
, which in the previous chapter
is defined “externally” through the transport maps, follows, and a family of subsets
is also a family of sets.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
X
and
I
be sets. A family of subsets of

indexed by

,

-family of subsets of

such that the following conditions hold:

cannot be shown to satisfy the expected properties. Clearly, if
\mathcal{N}^{X}_{i}
was defined
by the rule
\mathcal{N}^{X}_{i}\big(\Lambda(X)\big):=x_{0}\in X
, then it cannot be an embedding.

Proposition 4.1.6
.

The operations on families of subsets generate operations on family of subsets-maps.

Proposition 4.1.10
.

Let
\Lambda(X),K(X),M(X),N(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
,
P(Y),Q(Y)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,Y)
, and
f\colon X\to Y
.
Let also
\Phi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow K(X)
,
\Psi\colon M(X)\Rightarrow N(X)
, and
\Xi\colon P(Y)\Rightarrow Q(Y)
.

(i)

\Phi\cap\Psi\colon(\Lambda\cap M)(X)\Rightarrow(K\cap N)(X)
, where, for every
i\in I
and

(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(i)
,

(\Phi\cap\Psi)_{i}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(i)\to k_{0}(i)\cap\nu_{0}(i),\ \ \ \ (\Phi\cap\Psi)_{i}(u,w):=\big(\Phi_{i}(u),\Psi_{i}(w)\big).

(ii)

\Phi\cup\Psi\colon(\Lambda\cup M)(X)\Rightarrow(K\cup N)(X)
, where, for every
i\in I
,

(\Phi\cup\Psi)_{i}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(i)\to k_{0}(i)\cup\nu_{0}(i),

(\Phi\cup\Psi)_{i}(z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Phi_{i}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\lambda_{0}(i)$}\\
\Psi_{i}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\mu_{0}(i)$}\end{array}\right.

(iii)

\Phi\times\Xi\colon(\Lambda\times P)(X\times Y)\Rightarrow(K\times Q)(X\times Y)
, where,
for every
i\in I
and

(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times p_{0}(i)
,

(\Phi\times\Xi)_{i}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\times p_{0}(i)\to k_{0}(i)\times q_{0}(i),\ \ \ \ (\Phi\times\Xi)_{i}(u,w):=\big(\Phi_{i}(u),\Xi_{i}(w)\big).

(iv)

f(\Phi)\colon[f(\Lambda)](Y)\Rightarrow[f(K)](Y)
, where,
for every
i\in I
and
u\in f(\lambda_{0}(i))
,

[f(\Phi)]_{i}\colon f(\lambda_{0}(i))\to f(k_{0}(i)),\ \ \ \ [f(\Phi)]_{i}(u):=\Phi_{i}(u).

(v)

f^{-1}(\Xi)\colon[f^{-1}(P)](X)\Rightarrow[f^{-1}(Q)](X)
, where,
for every
j\in J
and
(x,y)\in f^{-1}(p_{0}(j))
,

[f^{-1}(\Xi)]_{i}\colon f^{-1}(p_{0}(j))\to f^{-1}(q_{0}(j)),\ \ \ \ [f^{-1}(\Xi)]_{j}(x,y):=\big(x,\Xi_{j}(y)\big).

Proof.

It is straightforward to show that all family of subsets-maps above are well-defined.
∎

Definition 4.1.11
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
and
h\colon J\to I
. The triplet

\Lambda(X)\circ h:=(\lambda_{0}\circ h,\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ h,\lambda_{1}\circ h)
, where
\Lambda\circ h:=(\lambda_{0}\circ h,\lambda_{1}\circ h)
is the
h
-subfamily of
\Lambda
, and the dependent operation

\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ h\colon\bigcurlywedge_{j\in J}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(h(j)),X\big)
is defined by

(\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ h)_{j}:=\mathcal{E}^{X}_{h(j)}
, for every
j\in J
, is called the
h
-subfamily of
\Lambda(X)
. If
J:={\mathbb{N}}
, we call
\Lambda(X)\circ h
the
h
-subsequence of
\Lambda(X)
.

It is immediate to show that
\Lambda(X)\circ h\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,X)
, and if
\Lambda(X)\circ h\in\textnormal{{Set}}(J,X)
,
then
h
is an embedding. All notions and results of section
3.4
on
subfamilies of families of sets extend naturally to subfamilies of families of subsets.

4.2
The interior union of a family of subsets

Definition 4.2.1
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})
be an
I
-family of subsets of
X
.
The interior union, or simply the union
of
\Lambda(X)
is the totality
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, which we
denote in this case by
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.
Let the non-dependent assignment routine

e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow X
defined by

(i,x)\mapsto\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)
, for every
(i,x)\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, and let

(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(i,x)=_{X}e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y).

If
\neq_{X}
is an inequality on
X
, let

(i,x)\neq_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)
.
The family
\Lambda(X)
is called a covering of
X
, or

\Lambda(X)
covers
X
, if

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}X.

If
\neq_{I}
is an inequality on
I
, and
\neq_{X}
an inequality on
X
, we say that
\Lambda(X)
is a
family of disjoint subsets of
X

(
with respect to
\neq_{I})
, if

\forall_{i,j\in I}\big(i\neq_{I}j\Rightarrow\lambda_{0}(i)\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}\lambda_{0}(j)\big),

where by Definition
2.8.1

\lambda_{0}(i)\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}\lambda_{0}(j):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{u\in\lambda_{0}(i)}\forall_{w\in\lambda_{0}(j)}\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(w)\big)
.

\Lambda(X)
is called a partition of
X
, if it covers
X
and
it is a family of disjoint subsets of
X
.

Clearly,
=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}
is an equality on

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, which is considered to be a set, and the
operation
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}

is an embedding of
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
into
X
, hence

\big(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)\subseteq X
. The inequality

\neq_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}
is the canonical inequality of the subset

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
of
X
(see Corollary
2.6.3
).
Hence, if
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
is discrete, then
\big(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}},\neq_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big)
is discrete,
and if
\neq_{X}
is tight, then
=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}
is tight.
As the following left diagram commutes,
\Lambda(X)
covers
X
, if and only of the following right
diagram commutes i.e., if and only if
X\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
X
X
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
X
X
.
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\
\ \mathrm{id}_{X}
g
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\
\ \mathrm{id}_{X}

If
(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
, it is not
necessary that
i=_{I}j
, hence it is not necessary that

(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
(as we show in the next proposition, the converse
implication holds). Consequently, the first projection operation
\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}:=\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda}
, where
\Lambda
is the
I
-family of sets induced by
\Lambda(X)
,
is not necessarily a function!
The second projection map on
\Lambda(X)
is defined by

\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda(X)}:=\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda}
.
Notice that
\neq_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}
is an
inequality on
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, without supposing neither an inequality on
I
, nor an inequality
on the sets
\lambda_{0}(i)
’s, as we did in Proposition
3.2.2
(ii).
Moreover,
\neq_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}}}
is tight, if
\neq_{X}
is tight. Cases (ii) and (iii) of the next
proposition are due to M. Zeuner
.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
.

(i)

If
(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
, then

(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
.

(ii)

If
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow X
is an embedding,

\big(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\big(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)
.

(iii)

If
\neq_{I}
is a tight inequality on
X
, and
\Lambda(X)
is a family of disjoint subsets of
X

with respect to
\neq_{I}
, then
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\hookrightarrow X
.

Proof.

(i) If
i=_{I}j
, and since
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}
is a function, we get

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(\lambda_{ij}(x))=\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)
.

(ii) Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)\Leftrightarrow(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
.
We define the operations
\mathrm{id}_{1}\colon\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
and

\mathrm{id}_{2}\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, both
defined by the identity map-rule. That
\mathrm{id}_{1}
is a function, follows from (i).
That
\mathrm{id}_{2}
is a function, follows from the hypothesis on
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}
.

(iii) We suppose that
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(i,x):=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y):=e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(j,y)
and we show that

(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)
. The converse implication follows from (i).
If
\neg(i\neq_{I}j)
, then
\lambda_{0}(i)\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}\lambda_{0}(j)
, hence

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)
, which contradicts our hypothesis. By the tightness of
\neq_{I}
we
get
i=_{I}j
, and it remains to show that
\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(j)}}y
. By
the equalities
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\big(\lambda_{ij}(x)\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(y)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)
, and as
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}

is an embedding, we get
\lambda_{ij}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(j)}}y
.
∎

Remark 4.2.3
.

Let
i_{0}\in I
,
(A,i_{A}^{X})\subseteq X
, and
C^{A}(X):=(\lambda_{0}^{A},\mathcal{E}^{A,X},\lambda_{1}^{A})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
the constant family
A
of subsets of
X
. Then

\bigcup_{i\in I}A:=\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{A}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}A.

Proof.

By definition
(i,a)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}A}}(j,b):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{A,X}_{i}(a)=_{X}\mathcal{E}^{A,X}_{j}(b):\Leftrightarrow i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}i_{A}^{X}(b)\Leftrightarrow a=_{A}b
. Let the operation
\phi\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}A\rightsquigarrow A
, defined by

\phi(i,a):=a
, for every
(i,a)\in\bigcup_{i\in I}A
, and let the operation

\theta\colon A\rightsquigarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}A
, defined by
\theta(a):=(i_{0},a)
, for every

a\in A
. Clearly,
\phi
and
\theta
are functions. The required equality of these subsets
follows from the following equalities:

i_{A}^{X}(a)=_{X}e^{C^{A}(X)}_{{\bigcup}}(i,a):=\mathcal{E}^{A,X}_{i}(a):=i_{A}^{X}(a)
,
and
e^{C^{A}(X)}_{{\bigcup}}(i_{0},a):=\mathcal{E}^{A,X}_{i_{0}}(a):=i_{A}^{X}(a)
.
∎

The interior union of a family of subsets generalises the union of two subsets.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

If
\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}(X)
is the
\mathbb{2}
-family of subsets
A,B
of
X
,

\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}A\cup B.

Proof.

The operation
g\colon\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)\rightsquigarrow A\cup B
, defined by
g(i,x):=\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}(i,x):=x
, for every
(i,x)\in\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)
, is well-defined, and it is an embedding,
since

g(i,x)=_{A\cup B}g(j,y):\Leftrightarrow x=_{A\cup B}y:\Leftrightarrow i^{X}_{A}(x)=_{X}i^{X}_{B}(y):\Leftrightarrow(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y).

The operation
f:A\cup B\rightsquigarrow\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)
, defined by

f(z):=(0,z)
, if
z\in A
, and
f(z):=(1,z)
, if
z\in B
,
is easily seen to be a function. For the commutativity of the following inner diagrams

A\cup B
{\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i)}
X
f
g
i_{A\cup B}^{X}\
\ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}(X)}

we use the equalities
i_{A\cup B}^{X}(g(i,x)):=i_{A\cup B}^{X}(x):=i_{A}^{X}(x):=e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}(X)}(i,x)
and

e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}(X)}f(z)):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}(X)}(0,z)&\mbox{, $z\in A$}\\
\\
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}(X)}(1,z)&\mbox{, $z\in B$}\end{array}\right.:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}i_{A}^{X}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in A$}\\
\\
i_{B}^{X}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in B$}\end{array}\right.:=i_{A\cup B}^{X}(z).\qed

Theorem 4.2.6

(Extension theorem for coverings)
.

Let
X,Y
be sets, and let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
be a covering of

X
. If
f_{i}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\to Y
, for every
i\in I
, such that

{f_{i}}_{|\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)}=_{{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j),Y)}}{f_{j}}_{|\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)},

for every
i,j\in I
, there is a unique

f\colon X\to Y
such that
f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}=_{{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),Y)}}f_{i}
, for every
i\in I
.

Proof.

Let
e\colon X\hookrightarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
such that the following diagram commutes

X
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
X
.
e
\mathrm{id}_{X}\
\ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}

Let the operation

f\colon X\rightsquigarrow Y
defined by

f(x):=f_{\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}(e(x))}\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda(X)}(e(x)\big),

for every
x\in X
. Hence, if
x\in X
, and
e(x):=(i,u)
, for some
i\in I
and
u\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, then

f(x):=f_{i}(u)
. We show that
f
is a function. Recall that

\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j):=\big\{(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times\lambda_{0}(j)\mid\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(w)\big\}
.
If
x,x{{}^{\prime}}\in X
, let
e(x):=(i,u)
and
e(x{{}^{\prime}}):=(j,w)
. If
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
, then

e(x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}e(x{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow(i,u)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,w):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(w):\Leftrightarrow(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j).

By the definition of
f
we have that
f(x):=f_{i}(u)
and
f(x{{}^{\prime}}):=f_{j}(w)
. We show that
f_{i}(u)=_{Y}f_{j}(w)
.
Since
\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)\subseteq\lambda_{0}(i)
and
\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)\subseteq\lambda_{0}(j)
, and as we have explained right before
Proposition
2.6.8
, by Definition
2.6.9
we have that

{f_{i}}_{|\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)}:=f_{i}\circ\textnormal{{pr}}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\ \ \&\ \ {f_{j}}_{|\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)}:=f_{j}\circ\textnormal{{pr}}_{\lambda_{0}(j)}.

Since
(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)
,
by the equality of the restrictions of
f_{i}
and
f_{j}
to
\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)

f_{i}(u):=\big(f_{i}\circ\textnormal{{pr}}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\big)(u,w)=_{Y}\big(f_{j}\circ\textnormal{{pr}}_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\big)(u,w):=f_{j}(w).

Next we show that, if
i\in I
, then
f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}=f_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\colon(\lambda_{0}(i),\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})\subseteq(X,\mathrm{id}_{X})

\lambda_{0}(i)
X
X
,
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\
\ \mathrm{id}_{X}

by Definition
2.6.9
we have that
f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}:=f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
. If
u\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, let

e\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\big):=(j,w)
, for some
j\in I
and
w\in\lambda_{0}(j)
. Hence, by the definition of
f
we get

f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}(u):=f\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\big):=f_{j}(w).

By the commutativity of the first diagram in this proof we get for
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\in X

\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(w):=e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big(e\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\big)\big)=_{X}\mathrm{id}_{X}\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\big):=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)

i.e.,
(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)
. Hence,
f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}(u):=f_{j}(w)=_{Y}f_{i}(u)
. Finally, let

f^{*}\colon X\to Y
such that
f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}^{*}:=f^{*}\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}=_{{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),Y)}}f_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
. If
x\in X
let
e(x):=(i,u)
, for some
i\in I
and
u\in\lambda_{0}(i)
.
By the commutativity of the first diagram, and since
f^{*}
is a function, we get

\displaystyle f^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=_{Y}f^{*}\big(e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(e(x))\big)

\displaystyle:=f^{*}\big(e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(i,u)\big)

\displaystyle:=f^{*}\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\big)

\displaystyle=_{Y}f_{i}(u)

\displaystyle:=f_{\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}(e(x))}\big(\textnormal{{pr}}_{2}^{\Lambda(X)}(e(x)\big)

\displaystyle:=f(x).\qed

Corollary 4.2.7
.

Let

\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
be a partition of

X
. If
f_{i}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\to Y
, for every
i\in I
, there is a unique

f\colon X\to Y
with
f_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}=_{{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),Y)}}f_{i}
, for every
i\in I
.

Proof.

The condition
{f_{i}}_{|\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)}=_{{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j),Y)}}{f_{j}}_{|\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)}
of Theorem
4.2.6
is trivially satisfied using
the logical principle Ex falso quodlibet. If we suppose that
(u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\lambda_{0}(j)
, which is impossible as
\lambda_{0}(i)\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}\lambda_{0}(j)
, the equality

(f_{i}\circ\textnormal{{pr}}_{\lambda_{0}(i)})(u,w)=_{Y}(f_{j}\circ\textnormal{{pr}}_{\lambda_{0}(j)})(u,w)
, where
i,j\in I
, follows immediately.
∎

Proposition 4.2.8
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})
,
M(X):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
,

\Psi:\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow M(X)
, and
(B,i_{B}^{X})\subseteq X
.

(i)

For every
i\in I
the operation

e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}:\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

x\mapsto(i,x)
, is an embedding, and
e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.

(ii)

If
\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq B
, for every
i\in I
, then
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq B
.

(iii)

The operation

\bigcup\Psi:\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

\bigcup\Psi(i,x):=(i,\Psi_{i}(x))
,
is an embedding,
such that for every
i\in I
the following diagram commutes
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcup\Psi
\Psi_{i}
e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}
e_{i}^{M(X)}

Proof.

(i) If
x,x{{}^{\prime}}\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, and since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
is an embedding, we have that

e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}(x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}(x{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(i,x{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x{{}^{\prime}})\Leftrightarrow x=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}x{{}^{\prime}}.

Moreover,
e_{{\bigcup}}^{X}\big(e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}(x)\big):=e_{{\bigcup}}^{X}(i,x):=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)
, hence
e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.

(ii) If
i\in I
and
e_{i}^{B}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq B
, then
i_{B}^{X}(e_{i}^{B}(x))=_{X}\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i}(x)
,
for every
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
. Let the operation
e^{B}\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow B
,
defined by
e^{B}(i,x):=e_{i}^{B}(x)
, for every
(i,x)\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
. The operation
e^{B}
is a function:

\displaystyle(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow i_{B}^{X}(e_{i}^{B}(x))=_{X}i_{B}^{X}(e_{j}^{B}(y))

\displaystyle\Rightarrow e_{i}^{B}(x)=_{X}e_{j}^{B}(y)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow e^{B}(i,x)=_{X}e^{B}(j,y).

Moroever,
i_{B}^{X}\big(e^{B}(i,x)\big):=i_{B}^{X}(e_{i}^{B}(x))=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x):=e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(i,x)
,
hence
e^{B}\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq B
.

(iii) The required commutativity of the diagram is immediate, and
\bigcup\Psi
is an embedding, since
X
\lambda_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\mu_{0}(j)
X
,
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\
\ \mathcal{Z}_{i}^{X}
\Psi_{i}
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\
\ \mathcal{Z}_{j}^{X}
\Psi_{j}

\displaystyle(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(y)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{X}(\Psi_{i}(x))=_{X}\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{j}(\Psi_{j}(y))

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow(i,\Psi_{i}(x))=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(j,\Psi_{j}(y))

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\bigcup\Psi(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}\bigcup\Psi(j,y).\qed

4.3
The intersection of a family of subsets

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
, and
i_{0}\in I
.
The intersection
\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
of
\Lambda(X)
is the
totality defined by

\Phi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):\Leftrightarrow\Phi\in\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})\ \&\ \forall_{i,j\in I}\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(\Phi_{j})\big).

Let
e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}:\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow X
be defined by

e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}(\Phi):=\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Phi_{i_{0}}\big)
, for
every
\Phi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, and

\Phi=_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\Theta:\Leftrightarrow e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}(\Phi)=_{X}e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}(\Theta):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Phi_{i_{0}}\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Theta_{i_{0}}\big),

If
\neq_{X}
is a given inequality on
X
, let

\Phi\neq_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\Theta:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Phi_{i_{0}}\big)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Theta_{i_{0}}\big)
.

Clearly,
=_{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}
is an equality on

\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, which is considered to be a set, and
e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}

is an embedding, hence
\big(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)\subseteq X
. Moreover, the inequality

\neq_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}
is the canonical inequality of the subset

\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
of
X
(see Corollary
2.6.3
).

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
.

(i)

\Phi=_{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}\Theta\Leftrightarrow\Phi=_{\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})}\Theta
.

(ii)

If
\Phi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, then
\Phi\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.

(iii)

If
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
is discrete, the set
\big(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),=_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}},\neq_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big)
is discrete.

Proof.

(i) To show the implication
(\Rightarrow)
, if
i\in I
, then

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Phi_{i_{0}})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Theta_{i_{0}})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Theta_{i}),

and since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
is an embedding,
\Phi_{i}=_{\lambda_{0}(i)}\Theta_{i}
. For the converse implication,
the pointwise equality of
\Phi
and
\Theta
implies that
\Phi_{i_{0}}=_{\lambda_{0}(i_{0})}\Theta_{i_{0}}
, hence

\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Phi_{i_{0}})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Theta_{i_{0}})
.

(ii) If
i=_{I}j
, then

\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\big(\lambda_{ij}(\Phi_{i})\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}(\Phi_{j})
,
and as
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}
is an embedding, we get the required equality
\lambda_{ij}(\Phi_{i})=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}\Phi_{j}
. The proof of (iii) is immediate.
∎

Since the equality of
\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the pointwise equality of
\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})
,
then, as we explained above, the equality of
\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the equality of

\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.

Remark 4.3.3
.

i_{0}\in I

(A,i_{A}^{X})\subseteq X

\bigcap_{i\in I}A:=\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{A}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}A.

Proof.

We proceed similarly to the proof of Remark
4.2.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.3.5
.

Proposition 4.3.6
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}),M_{X}:=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
, let
i_{0}\in I
,

\Psi:\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow M(X)
, and
(B,i_{B}^{X})\subseteq X
.

(i)

The operation

\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}:\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by

\Theta\mapsto\Theta_{i},
is a function, and
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq\lambda_{0}(i)
, for every
i\in I
.

(ii)

If
B\subseteq\lambda_{0}(i)
, for every
i\in I
, then
B\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.

(iii)

The operation

\bigcap\Psi:\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

[\bigcap\Psi(\Theta)]_{i}:=\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i})
, for every
i\in I
, is an embedding,
such that for every
i\in I

the following diagram commutes
\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcap\Psi
\Psi_{i}
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}
\pi_{i}^{M(X)}

Proof.

(i) Since
\Phi=_{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}\Theta\Rightarrow\Phi=_{\mathbb{A}(I,\lambda_{0})}\Theta
, we get

\Phi_{i}=\Theta_{i}
, for every
i\in I
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\big(\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}(\Theta)\big):=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Theta_{i})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Theta_{i_{0}}):=e^{X}_{{\bigcap}}(\Theta)
, we get

\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq\lambda_{0}(i)
.

(ii) If
i\in I
, let
e_{B}^{i}\colon B\subseteq\lambda_{0}(i)
, hence
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(e_{B}^{i}(b))=_{X}i_{B}^{X}(b)
,
for every
b\in B
.
Let the operation
e_{B}\colon B\rightsquigarrow\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by the rule
b\mapsto e_{B}(b)
,
where

[e_{B}(b)]_{i}:=e_{B}^{i}(b)
, for every
b\in B
and
i\in I
. First we show that
e_{B}
is well defined.
If
i,j\in I
, then

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\big([e_{B}(b)]_{i}\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\big([e_{B}(b)]_{j}\big):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(e_{B}^{i}(b))=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(e_{B}^{j}(b))\Leftrightarrow i_{B}^{X}(b)=_{X}i_{B}^{X}(b).

Clearly,
e_{B}
is a function. Moreover,
e_{B}\colon B\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, since,
for every
b\in B
,

e_{{\bigcap}}(e_{B}(b)):=\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big([e_{B}(b)]_{i_{0}}\big):=\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(e_{B}^{i_{0}}(b))=_{X}i_{B}^{X}(b).

(iii) It suffices to show that
\bigcap\Psi
is an embedding.
If
\Phi,\Theta\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, then

\displaystyle\Phi=_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\Theta

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Phi_{i_{0}}\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}\big(\Theta_{i_{0}}\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Psi_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}}))=_{X}\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i_{0}}(\Psi_{i_{0}}(\Theta_{i_{0}}))

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{i_{0}}^{X}\bigg(\bigg[\bigcap\Psi(\Phi)\bigg]_{i_{0}}\bigg)=_{X}\mathcal{Z}_{i_{0}}^{X}\bigg(\bigg[\bigcap\Psi(\Theta)\bigg]_{i_{0}}\bigg)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\bigg(\bigcap\Psi\bigg)(\Phi)=_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}\bigg(\bigcap\Psi\bigg)(\Theta).\qed

The above notions and results can be generalised as follows.

Definition 4.3.7
.

Let

and

be sets, and

.

, a

, for every

\lambda_{0}(i)

.

\ \mathcal{Z}^{Y}_{i}
\Psi_{i}
h

The totality

of family of

is
equipped with the pointwise equality,
and we write
\Psi\colon\Lambda(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}M(Y)
,
if
\Psi\in\textnormal{{Map}}(\Lambda(X),M(X))
.
If

\Xi\colon M(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}N(Z)
, where

, the composition family of subsets-map

\Xi\circ\Psi:\Lambda(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g\circ h}}{{\Longrightarrow}}N(Z)

. In the general case, if

, then
