Proposition 4.3.8
.

Let
X
and
Y
be sets, and
h:X\to Y
. Let also
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
,

M(Y):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{Y},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,Y)
, and
\Psi\colon\Lambda(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}M(Y)
.

(i)

The operation

\bigcup_{h}\Psi:\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

\big(\bigcup_{h}\Psi\big)(i,u):=(i,\Psi_{i}(u))
, for every
(i,u)\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, is
a function,
and for every
i\in I
the following left diagram commutes
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
.
\mu_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcup_{h}\Psi
\Psi_{i}
e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}
e_{i}^{M(Y)}
\bigcap_{h}\Psi
\Psi_{i}
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}
\pi_{i}^{M(Y)}

(ii)

If
i_{0}\in I
, the operation

\bigcap_{h}\Psi:\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)
, defined by

[\bigcap_{h}\Psi(\Theta)]_{i}:=\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i})
, for every
i\in I
, is a function,
such that for every
i\in I
the above right diagram commutes.

Proof.

(i) The commutativity of the diagram is trivial, and we show that
\bigcup_{h}\Psi
is a function:

\displaystyle(i,u)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,w)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}(u)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{j}(w)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow h(\mathcal{E}_{i}(u))=_{Y}h(\mathcal{E}_{j}(w))

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow E_{i}(\Psi_{i}(u))=_{Y}E_{j}(\Psi_{j}(w))

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow(i,\Psi_{i}(u))=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}(j,\Psi_{j}(w))

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\bigg(\bigcup_{h}\Psi\bigg)(i,u)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}\bigg(\bigcup_{h}\Psi\bigg)(j,w).

(ii) The commutativity of the diagram is trivial, and we show that
\bigcap_{h}\Psi
is a function:

\displaystyle\Phi=_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\Theta

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}(\Theta_{i_{0}})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow h\big(\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}})\big)=_{Y}h\big(\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}(\Theta_{i_{0}})\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow E_{i_{0}}\big(\Psi_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}})\big)=_{Y}E_{i_{0}}\big(\Psi_{i_{0}}(\Theta_{i_{0}})\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow E_{i_{0}}\bigg(\bigg[\bigcap_{h}\Psi(\Phi)\bigg]_{i_{0}}\bigg)=_{Y}E_{i_{0}}\bigg(\bigg[\bigcap_{h}\Psi(\Theta)\bigg]_{i_{0}}\bigg)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\bigg(\bigcap_{h}\Psi\bigg)(\Phi)=_{{\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}(i)}}\bigg(\bigcap_{h}\Psi\bigg)(\Theta).\qed

4.4
Families of subsets over products

Proposition 4.4.1
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}),K(X):=(k_{0},\mathcal{H}^{X},k_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
,

M(Y):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{Y},\mu_{1})
, and
N(Y):=(\nu_{0},\mathcal{H}^{Y},\nu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,Y)
.

(i)

(\Lambda\otimes M)(X\times Y):=(\lambda_{0}\otimes\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{Y},\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I\times J,X\times Y)
, where

(\lambda_{0}\otimes\mu_{0})(i,j):=\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j);\ \ \ \ (i,j)\in I\times J,

\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{Y}\big)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\hookrightarrow X\times Y,

\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}\otimes\mathcal{E}^{Y}\big)_{(i,j)}(u,w):=\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i}(u),\mathcal{E}^{Y}_{j}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j),\ (i,j)\in I\times J,

(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\to\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\times\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}}),

(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}(u,w):=\big(\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u),\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j).

(ii)

If
\Phi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow K(X)
and
\Psi\colon M(X)\Rightarrow N(X)
, then
\Phi\otimes\Psi\colon(\Lambda\otimes M)(X\times Y)\Rightarrow(K\otimes N)(X\times Y)
, where, for every
(i,j)\in I\times J
,

(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\to k_{0}(i)\times\nu_{0}(j),

(\Phi\otimes\Psi)_{(i,j)}(u,w):=\big(\Phi_{i}(u),\Psi_{j}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j).

(iii)

The following equality holds

\bigcup_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg).

(iv)

If
i_{0}\in I
and
j_{0}\in J
, the following equality holds

\bigcap_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg).

(v)

If
\Lambda(X)
covers
X
and
M(Y)
covers
Y
, then
(\Lambda\otimes M)(X\times Y)

covers
X\times Y
.

(vi)

Let the inequalities
\neq_{I},\neq_{J},\neq_{X}
and
\neq_{Y}
on
I,J,X
and
Y
, respectively.
If
\Lambda(X)
is a partition of
X
and
M(Y)
is a partition of
Y
, then
(\Lambda\otimes M)(X\times Y)

is a partition of
X\times Y
.

Proof.

The proofs of (i)-(iv) are the internal analogue to the proofs of Proposition
3.5.1
(i)-(iii).

(v) Since
X=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
and
Y=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)
, by case
(iii) and by Proposition
(iv) we have that

X\times Y=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg)=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}\bigcup_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big).

(vi) By Definition
4.2.1
we have that

i\neq_{I}i{{}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i{{}^{\prime}}}^{X}(u{{}^{\prime}});\ \ \ \ i,i{{}^{\prime}}\in I,\ u\in\lambda_{0}(i),\ u{{}^{\prime}}\in\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}}),

j\neq_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{Y}(w)\neq_{Y}\mathcal{E}_{j{{}^{\prime}}}^{Y}(w{{}^{\prime}});\ \ \ \ j,j{{}^{\prime}}\in J,\ w\in\mu_{0}(j),\ w{{}^{\prime}}\in\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}}).

Let
(i,j)\neq_{I\times J}(i{{}^{\prime}},j{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow i\neq_{I}i{{}^{\prime}}\vee j\neq_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}
. If
i\neq_{I}i{{}^{\prime}}
is the case, then

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i{{}^{\prime}}}^{X}(u{{}^{\prime}})
, hence
\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i}(u),\mathcal{E}^{Y}_{j}(w)\big)\neq_{X\times Y}\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i}(u{{}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{E}^{Y}_{j}(w{{}^{\prime}})\big)
. If
j\neq_{J}j{{}^{\prime}}
, we proceed similarly.
∎

Proposition 4.4.2
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}),K(X):=(k_{0},\mathcal{H}^{X},k_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
,

M(X):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1})
, and
N(X):=(\nu_{0},\mathcal{F}^{X},\nu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,X)
.

(i)

(\Lambda\wedge M)(X):=(\lambda_{0}\wedge\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\wedge\mathcal{Z}^{X},\lambda_{1}\wedge\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I\times J,X)
, where

(\lambda_{0}\wedge\mu_{0})(i,j):=\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j);\ \ \ \ (i,j)\in I\times J,

\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}\wedge\mathcal{Z}^{X}\big)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j)\hookrightarrow X,

\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}\wedge\mathcal{Z}^{X}\big)_{(i,j)}(u,w):=\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i}(u);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j),\ (i,j)\in I\times J,

(\lambda_{1}\wedge\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j)\to\lambda_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\cap\mu_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}}),

(\lambda_{1}\otimes\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}(u,w):=\big(\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(u),\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j).

(ii)

(\Lambda\vee M)(X):=(\lambda_{0}\vee\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\vee\mathcal{Z}^{X},\lambda_{1}\vee\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I\times J,X)
, where

(\lambda_{0}\vee\mu_{0})(i,j):=\lambda_{0}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(j);\ \ \ \ (i,j)\in I\times J,

\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}\vee\mathcal{Z}^{X}\big)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(j)\hookrightarrow X,

\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}\vee\mathcal{Z}^{X}\big)_{(i,j)}(z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\lambda_{0}(i)$}\\
\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{Y}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\mu_{0}(j)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ i\in I,\ z\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(i)

(\lambda_{1}\vee\mu_{1})_{(i,j)(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})}(z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lambda_{ii{{}^{\prime}}}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\lambda_{0}(i)$}\\
\mu_{jj{{}^{\prime}}}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\mu_{0}(j)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ \big((i,j),(i{{}^{\prime}}j{{}^{\prime}})\big)\in D(I\times J).

(iii)

If
\Phi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow K(X)
and
\Psi\colon M(X)\Rightarrow N(X)
, then
\Phi\wedge\Psi\colon(\Lambda\wedge M)(X)\Rightarrow(K\wedge N)(X)
, where, for every
(i,j)\in I\times J
,

(\Phi\wedge\Psi)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j)\to k_{0}(i)\cap\nu_{0}(j),

(\Phi\wedge\Psi)_{(i,j)}(u,w):=\big(\Phi_{i}(u),\Psi_{j}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (u,w)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j).

(iv)

If
\Phi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow K(X)
and
\Psi\colon M(X)\Rightarrow N(X)
, then
\Phi\vee\Psi\colon(\Lambda\vee M)(X)\Rightarrow(K\vee N)(X)
, where, for every
(i,j)\in I\times J
,

(\Phi\wedge\Psi)_{(i,j)}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(j)\to k_{0}(i)\cup\nu_{0}(j),

(\Phi\vee\Psi)_{(i,j)}(z):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Phi_{i}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\lambda_{0}(i)$}\\
\Psi_{j}(z)&\mbox{, $z\in\mu_{0}(j)$.}\end{array}\right.

(v)

The following equality holds

\bigcup_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j)\big)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\cap\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg).

(vi)

If
(i_{0},j_{0})\in I\times J
, the following equality holds

\bigcap_{(i,j)\in I\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(j)\big)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg).

(vii)

If
\Lambda(X)
covers
X
and
M(Y)
covers
Y
, then
(\Lambda\wedge M)(X)

covers
X
.

(viii)

Let the inequalities
\neq_{I},\neq_{J},\neq_{X}
and
\neq_{Y}
on
I,J,X
and
Y
, respectively.
If
\Lambda(X)
is a partition of
X
and
M(Y)
is a partition of
Y
, then
(\Lambda\wedge M)(X)

is a partition of
X
.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Proposition
4.4.1
.
∎

Let
M(Y):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{Y},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,Y)
,
(A,i_{A}^{X})\subseteq X
,
(B,i_{B}^{Y})\subseteq Y
, and let

\Lambda^{A}(X):=(\lambda_{0}^{A},\mathcal{E}^{A,X},\lambda_{1}^{X})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},X)
the constant family

of

X
, and
\Lambda^{B}:=(\lambda_{0}^{B},\mathcal{E}^{B,Y},\lambda_{1}^{B})
the constant family

of subsets of
Y
. By

and
4.4.2
we have that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j\in J}(A\times\mu_{0}(j))

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}A\times\mu_{0}(j)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}^{A}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{1}}\lambda_{0}^{A}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}A\times\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg),

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j\in J}(A\times\mu_{0}(j))

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}A\times\mu_{0}(j)

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}^{A}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\big)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{1}}\lambda_{0}^{A}(i)\bigg)\times\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(X\times Y)}A\times\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg),

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j\in J}(B\cap\mu_{0}(j))

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}B\cap\mu_{0}(j)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}^{B}(i)\cap\mu_{0}(j)\big)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{1}}\lambda_{0}^{B}(i)\bigg)\cap\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}B\cap\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg),

\displaystyle\bigcap_{j\in J}(B\cup\mu_{0}(j))

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}B\cup\mu_{0}(j)

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{1}\times J}\big(\lambda_{0}^{B}(i)\cup\mu_{0}(j)\big)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{1}}\lambda_{0}^{B}(i)\bigg)\cup\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}B\cup\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}(j)\bigg).

Definition 4.4.3
.

Let
X,Y,Z\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
x_{0}\in X,y_{0}\in Y
, and
R(Z):=(\rho_{0},\mathcal{E}^{Z}\rho_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X\times Y,Z)
.

(i)

If
x\in X
, the
x
-component of
R
is
the triplet

, where the assignment routines

are
as in Definition

\mathcal{E}^{x,Z}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\rho_{0}^{x}(y),Z\big)

, the

, where the assignment routines
\rho_{0}^{y},\rho_{1}^{y}
are

, and the dependent operation
\mathcal{E}^{y,Z}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\rho_{0}^{y}(x),Z\big)

\mathcal{E}^{y,Z}_{x}:=\mathcal{E}_{(x,y)}^{Z}
, for every
x\in Y
.

(iii)

Let
\bigcup^{1}R:=(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0},\big(\bigcup^{1}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z},\bigcup^{1}\rho_{1})
, where

\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0}:X\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,

\bigcup^{1}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x{{}^{\prime}})\bigg),\big(\bigcup^{1}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),Z\big)\ \mbox{are defined by}

\bigg(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0}\bigg)(x):=\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{x}(y):=\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ x\in X,

\bigg(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)(x,x{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y);\ \ \ \ (x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X),

\bigg(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(y,u):=\big(y,\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(u)\big);\ \ \ \ (y,u)\in\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y),

\big(\bigcup^{1}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}_{x}(y,u):=\mathcal{E}_{(x,y)}^{Z}(u);\ \ \ \ x\in X,\ (y,u)\in\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(iv)
Let
\bigcup^{2}R:=(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0},\big(\bigcup^{2}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z},\bigcup^{2}\rho_{1})
, where

\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0}:Y\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,

\bigcup^{2}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(x{{}^{\prime}})\bigg),\big(\bigcup^{2}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),Z\big)\ \mbox{are defined by}

\bigg(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0}\bigg)(y):=\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{y}(x):=\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ y\in Y,

\bigg(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)(y,y{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y);\ \ \ \ (y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y),

\bigg(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}(x,w):=\big(x,\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (x,w)\in\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y),

\big(\bigcup^{2}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}_{y}(x,w):=\mathcal{E}_{(x,y)}^{Z}(w);\ \ \ \ y\in Z,\ (x,w)\in\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(v)

Let
\bigcap^{1}R:=(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0},\big(\bigcap^{1}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z},\bigcap^{1}\rho_{1})
, where

\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0}:X\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,

\bigcap^{1}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x{{}^{\prime}})\bigg),\big(\bigcap^{1}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),Z\big)\ \mbox{are defined by}

\bigg(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0}\bigg)(x):=\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{x}(y):=\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ x\in X,

\bigg(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)(x,x{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}},y);\ \ \ \ (x,x{{}^{\prime}})\in D(X),

\bigg[\bigg(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{xx{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi)\bigg]_{y}:=\rho_{(x,y)(x{{}^{\prime}},y)}(\Phi_{y});\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y),

\big(\bigcap^{1}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}_{x}(\Phi):=\mathcal{E}_{(x,y_{0})}^{Z}(\Phi_{y_{0}});\ \ \ \Phi\in\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(vi)

Let
\bigcap^{2}R:=(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0},\big(\bigcap^{2}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z},\bigcap^{2}\rho_{1})
, where

\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0}:X\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,

\bigcap^{2}\rho_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y)}\mathbb{F}\bigg(\big(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y{{}^{\prime}})\bigg),\big(\bigcap^{2}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Z}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),Z\big)\ \mbox{are defined by}

\bigg(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0}\bigg)(y):=\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{y}(x):=\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y);\ \ \ \ y\in Y,

\bigg(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)(y,y{{}^{\prime}}):=\bigg(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y)\to\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y{{}^{\prime}});\ \ \ \ (y,y{{}^{\prime}})\in D(Y),

\bigg[\bigg(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{1}\bigg)_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi)\bigg]_{x}:=\rho_{(x,y)(x,y{{}^{\prime}})}(\Phi_{x});\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y),

\big(\bigcap^{2}\mathcal{E}\big)^{Z}_{y}(\Phi):=\mathcal{E}_{(x_{0},y)}^{Z}(\Phi_{x_{0}});\ \ \ \Phi\in\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

Clearly,

R^{y}(Z),\bigcup^{1}R(Z),\bigcap^{1}R(Z)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X,Z)
and

R^{x}(Z),\bigcup^{2}R(Z),\bigcap^{2}R(Z)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(Y,Z)
.

Proposition 4.4.4
.

Let
X,Y,Z\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
R(Z):=(\rho_{0},\mathcal{E}^{Z}\rho_{1}),S(Z):=(\sigma_{0},\mathcal{A}^{Z},\sigma_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X\times Y,Z)
, and

\Phi\colon R(Z)\Rightarrow S(Z)
.

(i)

Let
\Phi^{x}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\rho_{0}^{x}(y),\sigma_{0}^{x}(y)\big)
, where

\Phi^{x}_{y}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\colon\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\to\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)
.

(ii)

Let
\Phi^{y}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(R^{y}(x),S^{y}(x)\big)
, where

\Phi^{y}_{x}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\colon\rho_{0}^{y}(x)\to\sigma^{y}_{0}(x)
.

(iii)

Let
\bigcup^{1}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcup^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\bigcup^{1}\sigma_{0}\big)(x)\big)
, where, for every
x\in X
, we define

\bigg(\bigcup^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}\colon\sum_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{y}(x)\to\bigcup_{y\in Y}\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)

\bigg(\bigcup^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(y,u):=\big(y,\Phi_{(x,y)}(u)\big);\ \ \ \ (y,u)\in\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(iv)

Let
\bigcup^{2}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcup^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\bigcup^{2}\sigma_{0}\big)(y)\big)
, where, for every
y\in Y
, we define

\bigg(\bigcup^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}\colon\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\to\bigcup_{x\in X}\sigma^{y}_{0}(x)

\bigg(\bigcup^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}(x,w):=\big(x,\Phi_{(x,y)}(w)\big);\ \ \ \ (x,w)\in\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho(x,y).

(v)

Let
\bigcap^{1}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{x\in X}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcap^{1}\rho_{0}\big)(x),\big(\bigcap^{1}\sigma_{0}\big)(x)\big)
, where, for every
x\in X
, we define

\bigg(\bigcap^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}\colon\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}^{y}(x)\to\bigcap_{y\in Y}\sigma^{x}_{0}(y)

\bigg[\bigg(\bigcap^{1}\Phi\bigg)_{x}(\Theta)\bigg]_{y}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\big(\Theta_{y}))\big);\ \ \ \ \Theta\in\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y).

(vi)

Let
\bigcap^{2}\Phi\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\mathbb{F}\big(\big(\bigcap^{2}\rho_{0}\big)(y),\big(\bigcap^{2}\sigma_{0}\big)(y)\big)
, where, for every
y\in Y
, we define

\bigg(\bigcap^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}\colon\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}^{x}(y)\to\bigcap_{x\in X}\sigma^{y}_{0}(x)

\bigg[\bigg(\bigcap^{2}\Phi\bigg)_{y}(\Theta)\bigg]_{x}:=\Phi_{(x,y)}\big(\Theta_{x})\big);\ \ \ \ \Theta\in\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

Then we have that
\Phi^{x}\colon R^{x}(Z)\Rightarrow S^{x}(Z)
and
\Phi^{y}\colon R^{y}(Z)\Rightarrow S^{y}(Z)
and

\bigcup^{1}\Phi\colon\big(\bigcup^{1}R\big)(Z)\Rightarrow\big(\bigcup^{1}S\big)(Z)
and

\bigcup^{2}\Phi\colon\big(\bigcup^{2}R\big)(Z)\Rightarrow\big(\bigcup^{2}S\big)(Z)
and

\bigcap^{1}\Phi\colon\big(\bigcap^{1}R\big)(Z)\Rightarrow\big(\bigcap^{1}S\big)(Z)
and

\bigcap^{2}\Phi\colon\big(\bigcap^{2}R\big)(Z)\Rightarrow\big(\bigcap^{2}S\big)(Z)
.

Proof.

We proceed similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.5.3
.
∎

Proposition 4.4.5
.

If
R:=(\rho_{0},\rho_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(X\times Y,Z)
, the following equalities hold.

\bigcup_{x\in X}\bigcup_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)=_{\mathcal{P}(Z)}\bigcup_{y\in Y}\bigcup_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y),

\bigcap_{x\in X}\bigcap_{y\in Y}\rho_{0}(x,y)=_{\mathcal{P}(Z)}\bigcap_{y\in Y}\bigcap_{x\in X}\rho_{0}(x,y).

Proof.

The proof is straightforward.
∎

4.5
The semi-distributivity of
\bigcap
over
\bigcup

Section
4.4
is the “internal” analogue to section
3.5
,
as the presentation of the families of subsets over products follows the presentation of the families
of sets over products.
The distributivity of
\bigcap
over
\bigcup
though, cannot be approached as the distributivity of

\Sigma
over
\prod
, as the crucial Lemma
3.6.1
depends on the fact that the operation
\textnormal{{pr}}_{1}^{R^{x}}
is a
function, something which is not the case, as we have already explained in section
4.2
,
when the totality of the exterior union is equipped with the equality of the
interior union.

Definition 4.5.1
.

If
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
and
h\colon J\to I
, the composition family
of
\Lambda(X)

with
h
is the triplet
\Lambda(X)\circ h:=(\lambda_{0}\circ h,\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ h,\lambda_{1}\circ h)
, where

\lambda_{0}\circ h\colon J\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and
\lambda_{1}\circ h\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(j,j{{}^{\prime}})\in D(J)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(h(j)),\lambda_{0}(h(j{{}^{\prime}}))\big)
are given in Definition
3.1.6
(
iii
)
, and
the dependent operation
\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ h\colon\bigcurlywedge_{j\in J}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(h(j)),X\big)
is defined by

(\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ h)_{j}:=\mathcal{E}^{X}_{h(j)}
, for every
j\in J
.

Clearly,
\Lambda(X)\circ h\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,X)
. To formulate the distributivity of

\bigcap
over
\bigcup
in the language of
\mathrm{BST}
we need to introduce a family of subsets
P(I)

of the index-set
I
of a given family of subsets of a set
X
.
Throughout this section let the following data:

(a)

\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
.

(b)

(K,=_{K},\neq_{K})
is a set, and
k_{0}\in K
.

(c)

P(I):=(p_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{I},p_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(K,I)
.

(d)

\Lambda(X)\circ\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}:=(\lambda_{0}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k},\mathcal{E}^{X}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k},\lambda_{1}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(p_{0}(k),X)
, for every
k\in K
.

(e)

T:=\bigcap_{k\in K}p_{0}(k)
.

Proposition 4.5.2
.

N(K):=(\nu_{0},\mathcal{N}^{X},\nu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(K,X)
, where
\nu_{0}\colon K\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
is defined by

\nu_{0}(k):=\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k)}(\lambda_{0}\circ\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k})(j):=\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k)}\lambda_{0}\big(\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(j)\big);\ \ \ \ k\in K,

and
\mathcal{N}^{X}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{k\in K}\mathbb{F}(\nu_{0}(k),X)
,

\nu_{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(k,k{{}^{\prime}})\in D(K)}\mathbb{F}\big(\nu_{0}(k),\nu_{0}(k{{}^{\prime}})\big)
are defined by

\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k}\colon\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k)}\lambda_{0}\big(Z^{I}_{k}(j)\big)\bigg)\hookrightarrow X,\ \ \mathcal{N}^{X}_{k}(j,u):=\mathcal{E}^{X}_{\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(j)}(u);\ \ \ \ j\in p_{0}(k),\ u\in\lambda_{0}\big(Z^{I}_{k}(j)\big),

\nu_{1}(k,k{{}^{\prime}}):=\nu_{kk{{}^{\prime}}}\colon\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k)}\lambda_{0}\big(Z^{I}_{k}(j)\big)\to\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k{{}^{\prime}})}\lambda_{0}\big(Z^{I}_{k{{}^{\prime}}}(j)\big),

\nu_{kk{{}^{\prime}}}(j,u):=\big(p_{kk{{}^{\prime}}}(j),\lambda_{Z^{I}_{k}(j)Z^{I}_{k{{}^{\prime}}}(p_{kk{{}^{\prime}}}(j))}(u)\big);\ \ \ \ j\in p_{0}(k),\ u\in\lambda_{0}\big(Z^{I}_{k}(j)\big).

Proof.

The operation
\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k}
is an embedding, since by Definition
4.5.1

(j,u)=_{{\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k)}\lambda_{0}(Z^{I}_{k}(j))}}(j{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{X}_{\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(j)}(u)=_{X}\mathcal{E}^{X}_{\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(j{{}^{\prime}})}(u{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k}(j,u)=_{X}\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k}(j{{}^{\prime}},u{{}^{\prime}}).

Let
k=_{K}k{{}^{\prime}}
,
j\in p_{0}(k)
and
u\in\lambda_{0}\big(Z^{I}_{k}(j)\big)
. By the commutativity of the left inner diagrams

Proposition 4.5.3
.

Proposition 4.5.5

(Semi-distributivity of
\bigcap
over
\bigcup
)
.

\big(V,e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Xi(X)}\big)\subseteq\big(W,e_{{\bigcap}}^{N(X)}\big)
.

Proof.

Let the operation
\theta\colon V\rightsquigarrow W
, defined by

\theta(\tau,\Phi):=A^{(\tau,\phi)},\ \ \ \ (\tau,\Phi)\in V,

A^{(\tau,\Phi)}_{k}:=(\tau_{k},\Phi_{k});\ \ \ \ k\in K.

By definition
\tau_{k}\in p_{0}(k)
and
\Phi_{k}\in\lambda_{0}\big(\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(\tau_{k})\big)
. We show that
\theta
is well-defined i.e.,
A^{(\tau,\Phi)}\in\bigcap_{k\in K}\nu_{0}(k)
. If
k,l\in K
, by the above unfolding of
A\in W
we need to show that

\mathcal{E}^{X}_{\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(\tau_{k})}(\Phi_{k})=_{X}\mathcal{E}^{X}_{\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(\tau_{l})}(\Phi_{l}),

which follows immediately from the unfolding of the membership
\Phi\in\bigcap_{k\in K}\lambda_{0}\big(\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(\tau_{k})\big)
. If

(\tau,\Phi)=_{{\bigcup_{\tau\in T}\xi_{0}(\tau)}}(\tau{{}^{\prime}},\Phi{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{X}_{\tau}(\Phi)=_{X}\mathcal{H}^{X}_{\tau{{}^{\prime}}}(\Phi{{}^{\prime}}):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{k_{0}}^{I}(\tau_{k_{0}})}^{X}(\Phi_{k_{0}})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{k_{0}}^{I}(\tau_{k_{0}})}^{X}(\Phi{{}^{\prime}}_{k_{0}}),

A^{(\tau,\Phi)}=_{{\bigcap_{k\in K}\nu_{0}(k)}}A^{(\tau{{}^{\prime}},\Phi{{}^{\prime}})}:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k_{0}}(A_{k_{0}}^{(\tau,\Phi)})=_{X}\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k_{0}}(A_{k_{0}}^{(\tau{{}^{\prime}},\Phi{{}^{\prime}})})

:\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k_{0}}(A_{k_{0}}^{(\tau_{k},\Phi_{k})})=_{X}\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k_{0}}(A_{k_{0}}^{(\tau{{}^{\prime}}_{k},\Phi{{}^{\prime}}_{k})}):\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{k_{0}}^{I}(\tau_{k_{0}})}^{X}(\Phi_{k_{0}})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{k_{0}}^{I}(\tau_{k_{0}})}^{X}(\Phi{{}^{\prime}}_{k_{0}}),

hence
\theta
is a function. The commutativity of the following diagram is shown by the equalities
\bigcup_{\tau\in T}\bigg[\bigcap_{k\in K}\lambda_{0}\big(\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(\tau_{k})\big)\bigg]
\bigcap_{k\in K}\bigg[\bigcup_{j\in p_{0}(k)}\lambda_{0}\big(\mathcal{Z}^{I}_{k}(j)\big)\bigg]
X
\theta
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Xi(X)}\ \
\ e_{{\bigcap}}^{N(X)}

e_{{\bigcap}}^{N(X)}\big(\theta(\tau,\Phi)\big):=\mathcal{N}^{X}_{k_{0}}\big(\tau_{k_{0}},\Phi_{k_{0}}):=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{Z}_{k_{0}}^{I}(\tau_{k_{0}})}^{X}(\Phi_{k_{0}}):=e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Xi(X)}(\tau,\Phi).\qed

For the converse inclusion see Note
4.11.6
.

4.6
Sets of subsets

Definition 4.6.1
.

If
I,X\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
, a set of subsets of
X
indexed by
I
, or an
I
-set
of subsets of
X
, is triplet
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)

such that the following condition is satisfied:

Q(\Lambda(X)):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i,j\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)\Rightarrow i=_{I}j\big).

Let
\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
be their totality, equipped with the canonical equality on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
.

Remark 4.6.2
.

If
\Lambda(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
and
M(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
such that
\Lambda(X)=_{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)}M(X)
, then

M(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
.

Proof.

Let
\Phi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow M(X)
and
\Psi\colon M(X)\Rightarrow\Lambda(X)
such that

(\Phi,\Psi)\colon\Lambda(X)=_{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)}M(X)
. Let also
(f,g)\colon\mu_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\mu_{0}(j)
. It
suffices to show that
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)
.

\mu_{0}(i)
\mu_{0}(j)
X
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
f
g
\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i}\
\ \mathcal{Z}^{X}_{j}
\Phi_{i}
\Psi_{i}
\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i}\ \
\Psi_{j}
\Phi_{j}
\ \mathcal{E}^{X}_{j}

If we define
f{{}^{\prime}}:=\Psi_{j}\circ f\circ\Phi_{i}
and
g{{}^{\prime}}:=\Psi_{i}\circ g\circ\Phi_{j}
, it is straightforward to
show that
(f{{}^{\prime}},g{{}^{\prime}})\colon\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)
, hence
i=_{I}j
.
∎

By the previous remark
Q(\Lambda(X))
is an extensional property on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
. Since
\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
is defined by
separation on
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
, and since we see no objection to consider
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
to be a set, we also see no objection
to consider
\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
to be a set.

Definition 4.6.3
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
. Let the equality

=_{I}^{\Lambda(X)}
on
I

given by
i=_{I}^{\Lambda(X)}j:\Leftrightarrow\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)
, for every
i,j\in I
.
The set
\lambda_{0}I(X)
of subsets of
X

generated by
\Lambda(X)
is
the totality
I
equipped with the equality
=_{I}^{\Lambda(X)}
. We write
\lambda_{0}(i)\in\lambda_{0}I(X)
,
instead of
i\in I
, when
I
is equipped with the equality
=_{I}^{\Lambda(X)}
.
The operation
\lambda_{0}^{*}:I\rightsquigarrow I
from
(I,=_{I})
to
(I,=_{I}^{\Lambda(X)})
is
defined as in Definition
3.7.3
.

Clearly,
\lambda_{0}^{*}
is a function.
All results in section
3.7
are shown similarly for sets of subsets, and for convenience
we include them here without proof.

Proposition 4.6.4
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
, and let
Y
be a set.
If
f\colon I\to Y
, there is a unique function
\lambda_{0}f\colon\lambda_{0}I(X)\to Y
such that the
following diagram commutes
\lambda_{0}I(X)

Y.
\ \lambda_{0}f
\lambda_{0}
f

and

such that
the corresponding diagram commutes, then
f
is a function and
f^{*}
is equal to the function from

\lambda_{0}I(X)
to

generated by
f
.

.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)

Definition 4.6.6
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
, and let

If

Corollary 4.6.7
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
, and let
Y
be a set.

(i)

The operation
\Phi\colon\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{F}(I,Y)
, defined by

\Phi(f^{*}):=f^{*}\circ\lambda_{0}
, for every
f^{*}\in\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)
, is an embedding.

(ii)

If
\Lambda(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
, then
\Phi
is a surjection, the operation

\Theta\colon\mathbb{F}(I,Y)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)
, defined by

\Theta(f):=\lambda_{0}f
, for every
f\in\mathbb{F}(I,Y)
, is an embedding, and

(\Theta,\Phi)\colon(\mathbb{F}(I,Y)=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)
.

Proof.

(i) By definition of the corresponding equalities we have that

\displaystyle f^{*}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I(X),Y)}g^{*}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(f^{*}(\lambda_{0}(i))=_{Y}g^{*}(\lambda_{0}(i))\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big((f^{*}\circ\lambda_{0})(i))=_{Y}(g^{*}\circ\lambda_{0})(i))\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow f^{*}\circ\lambda_{0}=_{\mathbb{F}(I,Y)}g^{*}\circ\lambda_{0}.

(ii) If
f\in\mathbb{F}(I,Y)
, then by Proposition
4.6.4
there is unique
\lambda_{0}f\in\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)
such that
\Phi(\lambda_{0}f):=\lambda_{0}f\circ\lambda_{0}=_{\mathbb{F}(I,Y)}f
.
By definition of the corresponding equalities we have that

\displaystyle f=_{\mathbb{F}(I,Y)}g

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(f(i))=_{Y}g(i)\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}f(\lambda_{0}(i))=_{Y}\lambda_{0}g(\lambda_{0}(i)\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\lambda_{0}f=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,Y)}\lambda_{0}g.

Moreover, we have that

(\Theta\circ\Phi)(f^{*}):=\Theta(f^{*}\circ\lambda_{0}):=\lambda_{0}(f^{*}\circ\lambda_{0})=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I(X),Y)}f^{*}
, and

(\Phi\circ\Theta)(f):=\Phi(\lambda_{0}f):=(\lambda_{0}f)\circ\lambda_{0}=_{\mathbb{F}(I,Y)}f
.
∎

.

Let

Remark 4.6.9
.

Let the set
\big(X,=_{X},\neq_{X}^{{\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}}\big)
, and

\Delta^{1}(X):=\big(\delta_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\delta_{1}^{1}\big)
, where the non-dependent assignment routine

\delta_{0}^{1}\colon\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
is defined by the rule
f\mapsto\delta_{0}^{1}(f)
, for every

f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})

(
see Definition
2.8.3
)
, and the dependent operations

\mathcal{E}^{1,X}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{f\in DF(X,\mathbb{2})}\mathbb{F}(\delta_{0}^{1}(f),X)
and

\delta_{1}^{1}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(f,g)\in D(\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}))}\mathbb{F}(\delta_{0}^{1}(f),\delta_{0}^{1}(g))
are defined, respectively, by

\mathcal{E}^{1,X}_{f}\colon\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\hookrightarrow X\ \ \ \ x\mapsto x;\ \ \ \ x\in\delta_{0}^{1}(f),

\delta_{1}^{1}(f,g):=\delta_{fg}^{1}\colon\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\to\delta_{0}^{1}(g)\ \ \ \ x\mapsto x;\ \ \ \ x\in\delta_{0}^{1}(f).

If
\Delta^{0}(X):=\big(\delta_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\delta_{1}^{0}\big)
, where

\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
-sets of detachable subsets of

.

Proof.

We give the proof only for
\Delta^{1}(X)
. It is easy to show that
\Delta^{1}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}),X)
.
Let

i.e., there are

\delta_{0}^{1}(f)
\delta_{0}^{1}(g)
X
.
e
k
\mathcal{E}^{1,X}_{f}\
\ \mathcal{E}^{1,X}_{g}

.

. Since

, we get

, and since
x=_{X}k(x)
, we get
g(x)=_{\mathbb{2}}1
. If
f(x)=_{\mathbb{2}}0
, we use proceed similarly.
∎

Clearly,
\delta_{0}^{1}(\overline{1})=X
,

\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap\delta_{0}^{1}(g)=\delta_{0}^{1}(f\cdot g)
, and
\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{1}(g)=\delta_{0}^{1}(f+g-f\cdot g)
.

Corollary 4.6.13
.

Let
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
and

M(Y):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{Y}\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,Y)
. The operation
\Theta\colon\mathbb{F}(I,J)\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I,\mu_{0}J)
, defined by
f\mapsto f^{*}
, for every
f\in\mathbb{F}(I,J)
,
is a function. If
M(Y)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(J,Y)
, then
\Theta
is an embedding, and a surjection.

Proof.

By definition of the corresponding equalities we have that

\displaystyle f=_{\mathbb{F}(I,J)}g

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(f(i)=_{J}g(i)\big)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\mu_{0}(f(i))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\mu_{0}(g(i))\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(f^{*}(\lambda_{0}(i))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}g^{*}(\lambda_{0}(i))\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow f^{*}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}I(X),\mu_{0}J(Y))}g^{*}.

If
M(Y)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(J,Y)
, the above implication is also an equivalence,
hence
\Theta
is an embedding. By Proposition
4.6.5
we have that
\Theta
is a surjection.
∎

Definition 4.6.15
.

An
I
-family of sets
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
is a family of

is contractible, for every

.
A modulus of centres of contraction for
\Lambda
is a
dependent operation
\textnormal{{centre}}^{\Lambda}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, with

\textnormal{{centre}}^{\Lambda}_{i}
a centre of contraction for
\lambda_{0}(i)
, for every
i\in I
.

In Proposition

we saw that if
(f,g)\colon X=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}Y
,
the set
\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(y)
is contractible with
\textnormal{{centre}}^{f}_{y}:=g(y)
, for every
y\in Y
i.e., the dependent operation

\textnormal{{centre}}^{f}
is a modulus of centres of contractions for the family
\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(X)
. Next follows a kind of inverse to
Proposition
2.4.1
.

Proposition 4.6.16
.

Let the sets
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
,
(Y,=_{Y},\neq_{Y})
, and
f\colon X\to Y
. If

\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(X):=\big(\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}_{0},\mathcal{E}^{\textnormal{{fib}},X},\textnormal{{fib}}_{1}^{f}\big)

X
with

\textnormal{{centre}}^{\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(X)}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{y\in Y}\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(y)

\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(X)
,
there is
g\in\mathbb{F}(Y,X)
with
(f,g)\colon X=_{\mathbb{V}_{0}}Y
.

Proof.

Let the operation
g\colon Y\rightsquigarrow X
, defined by
g(y):=\textnormal{{centre}}^{\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(X)}(y)
, for every
y\in Y
.
Since
g(y)\in\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(y)
, we have that
f(g(y))=_{Y}y
. Since
g(y)
is a centre of contraction for
\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(y)
, we
have that
\forall_{x\in X}\big(f(x)=_{Y}y\Rightarrow x=_{X}g(y)\big)
. First we show that the operation
g
is a function.
For that, let

, and we show that

. Since the map
\textnormal{{fib}}_{yy{{}^{\prime}}}^{1}
in

, we get

. Since
g(y)
is a centre of contraction for
\textnormal{{fib}}^{f}(y)
, we get

.
It remains to show that if

. By the definition of

. As

, we get

.
∎

4.7
Families of equivalence classes

In this section we extend results on sets of subsets to families of equivalence classes.
Although a family of equivalence classes is not, in general, a set of subsets,
we can define functions on them, if we use appropriate functions on their index-set.

Definition 4.7.1
.

If
X
is a set and
R_{X}(x,x{{}^{\prime}})
is an extensional property on
X\times X
that
satisfies the conditions of an equivalence relation, we call the pair
(X,R_{X})

an equivalence structure. If
(Y,S_{Y})
is an equivalence structure,
a function
f\colon X\to Y
is an equivalence preserving function, or an
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-function, if

\forall_{x,x{{}^{\prime}}\in X}\big(R(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow S(f(x),f(x{{}^{\prime}}))\big).

If, for every
x,x{{}^{\prime}}\in X
, the converse implication holds, we say that

f
is an
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-embedding.
Let
\mathbb{F}(R_{X},S_{Y})
be the set of
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-functions
2
2

2

By the extensionality of
S_{Y}

the property of being an
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-function is extensional on
\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
.
.

.

are the corresponding families of equivalence classes, there is a unique

f^{*}\colon\rho_{0}X(X)\to\sigma_{0}Y(Y)
such that the following diagram commutes
\rho_{0}X(X)
\sigma_{0}Y(Y).
Y
X
f^{*}
f
\rho_{0}^{*}
\sigma_{0}^{*}

If
f\colon X\to Y
and
f^{*}:\rho_{0}X(X)\to\sigma_{0}Y(Y)
such that the above diagram commutes,
then
f
is an
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-function and
f^{*}
is equal to the function from
\rho_{0}X(X)
to
\sigma_{0}Y(Y)

generated by
f
.

Proof.

The assignment routine
f^{*}
from
\rho_{0}X(X)
to
\sigma_{0}Y(Y)
defined by

f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x)):=\sigma_{0}(f(x))
, for every
\rho_{0}(x)\in\rho_{0}X(X)

is extensional, since for every
x,x{{}^{\prime}}\in X
we have that

\rho_{0}(x)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow R_{X}(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow S_{Y}(f(x),f(x{{}^{\prime}}))
, hence

\sigma_{0}(f(x))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\sigma_{0}(f(x{{}^{\prime}})):\Leftrightarrow f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}}))
.
The uniqueness of
f^{*}
is immediate. For the converse, if
x,x{{}^{\prime}}\in X
, then by the transitivity
of
=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}
we have that

R_{X}(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow\rho_{0}(x)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}}))\Rightarrow\sigma_{0}(f(x))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\sigma_{0}(f(x{{}^{\prime}}))
, hence
S_{Y}(f(x),f(x{{}^{\prime}}))
.
The proof that
f^{*}
is equal to the function from
\rho_{0}X(X)
to
\sigma_{0}Y(Y)
generated by
f
is immediate.
∎

The previous is the constructive analogue to a standard classical fact (see
[
45
]
, p. 17).
A function
f^{*}\colon\rho_{0}X(X)\to\sigma_{0}Y(Y)

does not generate a function from
X
to
Y
.

Proposition 4.7.7
.

Let
(X,R_{X})
and
(Y,S_{Y})
be equivalence structures and
R(X),S(Y)
the families of their
equivalence classes. If
f^{*}:\rho_{0}X(X)\to\sigma_{0}Y(Y)
, there is

f\colon X\rightsquigarrow Y
,
which is
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-preserving and
(=_{X},S_{Y})
-preserving, such that the following diagram commutes
\rho_{0}X(X)
\sigma_{0}Y(Y).
Y
X
f^{*}
f
\rho^{*}
\sigma^{*}

Proof.

If
x\in X
, then
f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x)):=\sigma_{0}(y)
, for some
y\in Y
. We define the routine
f(x):=y
i.e.,
the output of
f^{*}
determines the output of
f
. Since

R(x,x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow\rho_{0}(x)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}})\Rightarrow f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x))=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}f^{*}(\rho_{0}(x{{}^{\prime}}))
,
hence
\sigma_{0}(y)=_{\mathcal{P}(Y)}\sigma_{0}(y{{}^{\prime}})
and
S_{Y}(y,y{{}^{\prime}})
, we get
S_{Y}(f(x),f(x{{}^{\prime}}))
, and
the operation
f
is
(R_{X},S_{Y})
-preserving. Although we cannot show that
f
is a function, we can show
that it is
(=_{X},S)
-preserving, since
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow R_{X}(x,x{{}^{\prime}})
, and we work as above.
∎

If

, then

\mathcal{P}_{j}^{Y}\big(\lambda_{ij}(u)\big)=_{Y}\mathcal{P}^{Y}(u)=_{Y}\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{Y}(w)=_{Y}\mathcal{Z}_{j}^{Y}\big(\mu_{ij}(w)\big)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Z}^{Y}{\ {\odot}\ }\mathcal{E}^{X}\big)_{j}\big((\mu_{1}{\ {\odot}\ }\lambda_{1})_{ij}(u,w)\big)

For the other two inner diagrams we proceed similarly.

The basic properties of the composition of partial functions extend to equalities for the corresponding
families of partial functions. E.g., we get

N(Z,W){\ {\odot}\ }\big[M(Y,Z){\ {\odot}\ }\Lambda(X,Y)\big]=_{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X,W)}\big[N(Z,W){\ {\odot}\ }M(Y,Z)\big]{\ {\odot}\ }\Lambda(X,Y).

Suppose that
\Lambda(X,Y):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},\mathcal{P}^{Y})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X,Y)

and
M(X,Y):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1},\mathcal{Q}^{Y})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X,Y)
. We can define in the expected way the following
families of partial functions:

(\Lambda\cap_{l}M)(X,Y):=\big(\lambda_{0}\cap_{l}\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\cap_{l}\mathcal{Z}^{X},\lambda_{1}\cap_{l}\mu_{1},(\mathcal{P}\cap_{l}\mathcal{Q})^{Y}\big),

(\Lambda\cap_{r}M)(X,Y):=\big(\lambda_{0}\cap_{r}\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\cap_{r}\mathcal{Z}^{X},\lambda_{1}\cap_{r}\mu_{1},(\mathcal{P}\cap_{r}\mathcal{Q})^{Y}\big),

(\Lambda\cup M)(X,Y):=\big(\lambda_{0}\cup\mu_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{X},\lambda_{1}\cup\mu_{1},(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{Q})^{Y}\big).

The basic properties of the intersections and union of partial functions extend to equalities for the corresponding
families of partial functions. E.g., we get

(\Lambda\cup M)(X,Y)=_{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X,Y)}(M\cup\Lambda)(X,Y).

Various notions and results on families of subsets extend to families of partial functions.

4.9
Families of complemented subsets

Definition 4.9.1
.

Let the sets
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
and
(I,=_{I})
.
A family of complemented subsets
of
X
indexed by
I
, or an
I
-family of complemented subsets of
X
,
is a structure

\bm{\Lambda}(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0})
, such that

\Lambda^{1}(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
and

\Lambda^{0}(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)

i.e., for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
, the following inner diagrams commute
X
\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}^{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{0,X}
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{0,X}\ \

\lambda_{ji}^{1}
\lambda_{ij}^{0}
\lambda_{ji}^{0}

such that

\forall_{i\in I}\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1}(i),\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)\big)\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)\big)

If
\bm{M}(X):=(\mu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\mu_{1}^{1},\mu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\mu_{1}^{0})
,

\bm{N}(X):=(\nu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{H}^{1,X},\nu_{1}^{1},\nu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{H}^{0,X},\nu_{1}^{0})

from

\bm{\Psi}:=(\Psi^{1},\Psi^{0})
, where

\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
\mu_{0}^{0}(i)
.

\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{1,X}
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{1,X}
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{0,X}
\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{0,X}\ \ \
\Psi_{i}^{1}
\Psi_{i}^{0}

The totality
\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\bm{\Lambda}(X),\bm{M}(X))
of the

from
\bm{\Lambda}(X)
to
\bm{M}(X)
is equipped with the pointwise equality.
If
\bm{\Psi}\colon\bm{\Lambda}(X)\Rightarrow\bm{M}(X)
and if

and

.
Moreover,
\mathrm{Id}_{\bm{\Lambda}(X)}:=(\mathrm{Id}_{\Lambda^{1}(X)},\mathrm{Id}_{\Lambda^{0}(X)})
, and the totality
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
of families of complemented subsets of

over

is equipped with the equality

\bm{\Lambda}(X)=_{{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})}}\bm{M}(X)
if and only if

\exists_{\bm{\Psi}\in\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\bm{\Lambda}(X),\bm{M}(X))}\exists_{\bm{\Xi}\in\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\bm{M}(X),\bm{\Lambda}(X))}\big(\bm{\Psi}\circ\bm{\Xi}=\mathrm{Id}_{\bm{M}(X)}\ \&\ \bm{\Xi}\circ\bm{\Psi}=\mathrm{Id}_{\bm{\Lambda}(X)}\big).

As in the case of
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
, we see no reason not to consider
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
a set.

Clearly, the obviously defined set
\textnormal{{PrfEql}}_{0}(\bm{\Lambda}(X),\bm{M}(X))
is a subsingleton. A family
\bm{\Lambda}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
is in
\textnormal{{Set}}(I,\bm{X})
, if
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}\bm{\Lambda}_{0}(j)\Rightarrow i=_{I}j
, for every

. Trivially, if

, or if
\Lambda^{0}(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X)
, then
\bm{\Lambda}(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,\bm{X})
. Clearly, if
\bm{\Lambda}(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,\bm{X})
and
\bm{M}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
such that
\bm{M}(X)=_{{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})}}\bm{\Lambda}(X)
, then
\bm{M}(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,\bm{X})
.
The operations between complemented subsets induce new families of complemented subsets and family-maps between them. If

\bm{\Lambda}(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0}\big)
and

\bm{M}(X):=\big(\mu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\mu_{1}^{1},\mu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\mu_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
, let the following new elements of
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
:

(-\bm{\Lambda})(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0},\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1}\big),

(\bm{\Lambda}\cap\bm{M})(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1}\cap\mu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1}\cap\mu_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0}\cup\mu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0}\cup\mu_{1}^{0}\big),

(\bm{\Lambda}\cup\bm{M})(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1}\cup\mu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1}\cup\mu_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0}\cap\mu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0}\cap\mu_{1}^{0}\big),

(\bm{\Lambda}-\bm{M})(X):=[\bm{\Lambda}\cap(-\bm{M})](X).

If
\bm{N}(Y):=(\nu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{H}^{1,Y},\nu_{1}^{1},\nu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{H}^{0,Y},\nu_{1}^{0})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{Y})
and
f\colon X\to Y
, then using
