4.1.9
we define

f^{-1}(\bm{N})(X):=\big(f^{-1}(\nu_{0}^{1}),f^{-1}\big(\mathcal{H}^{1,Y}\big)^{X},f^{-1}(\nu_{1}^{1}),f^{-1}(\nu_{0}^{0}),f^{-1}\big(\mathcal{H}^{0,Y}\big)^{X},f^{-1}(\nu_{1}^{0})\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X}),

f(\bm{\Lambda})(Y):=\big(f^{-1}(\lambda_{0}^{1}),f\big(\mathcal{E}^{1,X}\big)^{Y},f(\lambda_{1}^{1}),f(\lambda_{0}^{0}),f\big(\mathcal{E}^{0,X}\big)^{Y},f(\lambda_{1}^{0})\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{Y}).

Properties between complemented subsets induce equalities between their families e.g.,

\big[f^{-1}\big[(\bm{N}\cup\bm{K})(Y)\big]\big](X)=_{\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})}\big[f^{-1}(\bm{N})(Y)\cup f^{-1}(\bm{K})(Y)\big](X).

Using definitions from section
4.1
, if
\bm{\Phi}\colon\bm{\Lambda}(X)\Rightarrow\bm{M}(X)
, let

-\bm{\Phi}\colon(-\bm{\Lambda})(X)\Rightarrow(-\bm{M})(X)

-\bm{\Phi}:=(\Phi^{0},\Phi^{1});\ \ \ \ \bm{\Phi}:=(\Phi^{1},\Phi^{0}).

If
\bm{\Psi}\colon\bm{P}(X)\Rightarrow\bm{Q}(X)
, then
\bm{\Phi}\cap\bm{\Psi}\colon(\bm{\Lambda}\cap\bm{P})(X)\Rightarrow(\bm{M}\cap\bm{R})(X)
, where

\bm{\Phi}\cap\bm{\Psi}:=(\Phi^{1}\cap\Psi^{1},\Phi^{0}\cup^{0}),

and
\bm{\Phi}\cup\bm{\Psi}\colon(\bm{\Lambda}\cup\bm{P})(X)\Rightarrow(\bm{M}\cup\bm{R})(X)
, where

\bm{\Phi}\cup\bm{\Psi}:=(\Phi^{1}\cup\Psi^{1},\Phi^{0}\cap^{0}),

and
\bm{\Phi}-\bm{\Psi}\colon(\bm{\Lambda}-\bm{P})(X)\Rightarrow(\bm{M}-\bm{R})(X)
, where

\bm{\Phi}-\bm{\Psi}:=\bm{\Phi}\cap(-\bm{\Psi})
. If
\bm{S}(Y)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,Y)
,

(\bm{\Lambda}\times\bm{S})(X\times Y):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1}\times s_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X}\times\mathcal{S}^{1,Y},\lambda_{1}^{1}\times s_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0}\times s_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X}\times\mathcal{S}^{0,Y},\lambda_{0}^{0}\times s_{0}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I\times J,\bm{X}\times\bm{Y}),

(\bm{\lambda}_{0}\times\bm{s}_{0})(i,j):=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\times\bm{s}_{0}(i).

If
\bm{\Xi}\colon\bm{S}(Y)\Rightarrow\bm{T}(Y)
, then
\bm{\Phi}\times\bm{\Xi}\colon(\bm{\Lambda}\times\bm{S})(X\times Y)\Rightarrow(\bm{M}\times\bm{T})(X\times Y)
, where

\bm{\Phi}\times\bm{\Xi}:=\big(\Phi^{1}\times\Xi^{1},\Phi^{0}\times\Xi^{0}\big).

Due to the above families of complemented subsets the following proposition is well-formulated.

Proposition 4.9.2
.

Let
\bm{\Lambda}(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
,
i_{0}\in I
, and let

\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i):=\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{1}(i),\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)\bigg)\ \ \ \ \&\ \ \ \ \bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i):=\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{1}(i),\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)\bigg).

(i)

\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i),\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
.

(ii)

-\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigcap_{i\in I}\big(-\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)
.

(iii)

-\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigcup_{i\in I}\big(-\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)
.

(iv)

If
i\in I
, then
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
.

(v)

If

\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
, for some
i\in I
, then

\bm{A}\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
.

(vi)

If
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\subseteq\bm{A}
, for every
i\in I
, then
\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\subseteq\bm{A}
.

(vii)

If
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\supseteq\bm{A}
, for every
i\in I
, then
\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\supseteq\bm{A}
.

(viii)

If
\bm{M}(X):=\big(\mu_{0}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\mu_{1}^{1},\mu_{0}^{0},\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\mu_{1}^{0})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{Y})
and
f\colon X\to Y
,
then

f^{-1}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\mu}_{0}(i)\bigg)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigcup_{i\in I}f^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}_{0}(i)\big)\ \ \ \ \&\ \ \ \ f^{-1}\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\mu}_{0}(i)\bigg)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigcap_{i\in I}f^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}_{0}(i)\big).

Proof.

(i) We show the first membership only. If
(i,x)\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)

and
\Phi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
, then
e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}(i,x):=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)
and
e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}(\Phi):=\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Phi_{i_{0}})
.
Since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i})=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Phi_{i_{0}})
and
\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
,
we have that
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i})
, and by the extensionality of
\neq_{X}
we get

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Phi_{i_{0}})
.

(ii) and (iii) are straightforward to show. For (iv) we need to show that
\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)
and
\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)\subseteq\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
,
which follow from Propositions
4.2.8
(ii) and
4.3.6
(ii), respectively.
Case (v) follows from (iv) and the transitivity of
\bm{A}\subseteq\bm{B}
.

(vi) If
\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)\subseteq A^{1}
, for every
i\in I
, then
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)\subseteq A^{1}
, and if
A^{0}\subseteq\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
, for every
i\in I
, then
A^{0}\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)
.
Case (vii) is shown similarly.

(viii) We show the first equality only. By Propositions
4.2.5
and
4.3.5

we have that

\displaystyle f^{-1}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\mu}_{0}(i)\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(f^{-1}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}^{1}(i)\bigg),f^{-1}\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}^{0}(i)\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}f^{-1}\big(\mu_{0}^{1}(i)\big),\bigcap_{i\in I}f^{-1}\big(\mu_{0}^{0}(i)\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigg(f^{-1}\big(\mu_{0}^{1}(i)\big),f^{-1}\big(\mu_{0}^{0}(i)\big)\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{i\in I}f^{-1}\big(\bm{\mu}_{0}(i)\big).\qed

Let
\bm{\Lambda}(X),\bm{M}(X)
and
\Psi\colon\bm{\Lambda}(X)\Rightarrow\bm{M}(X)
. Since
\Psi^{1}\colon\Lambda^{1}(X)\Rightarrow M^{1}(X)
and
\Psi^{0}\colon\Lambda^{0}(X)\Rightarrow M^{0}(X)
, the following maps between
complemented subsets (see Definition
2.8.2
) are defined

\bigcup\bm{\Psi}:=\big(\bigcup\Psi^{1},\bigcap\Psi^{0}\big)\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\to\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\mu}_{0}(i),

\bigcap\bm{\Psi}:=\big(\bigcap\Psi^{0},\bigcup\Psi^{1}\big)\colon\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\to\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\mu}_{0}(i),\ \ \ \ \mbox{where}

\bigcup\Psi^{1}\colon\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)\to\bigcup_{i\in I}\mu_{0}^{1}(i)\ \ \ \&\ \ \ \bigcap\Psi^{0}\colon\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}^{0}(i)\to\bigcap_{i\in I}\mu_{0}^{0}(i)

are defined according to Proposition
4.2.8
(ii) and
4.3.6
(ii).

Proposition 4.9.3
.

Let
\bm{A}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X),\bm{B}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(Y)
,
\bm{\Lambda}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
and
\bm{M}(Y)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(J,\bm{Y})
. The following properties hold:

\bm{A}\times\bigcup_{j\in J}\bm{\mu}_{0}(j)=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X\times Y)}\bigcup_{j\in J}(\bm{A}\times\bm{\mu}_{0}(j)),

\bm{A}\times\bigcap_{j\in J}\bm{\mu}_{0}(j)=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X\times Y)}\bigcap_{j\in J}(\bm{A}\times\bm{\mu}_{0}(j)),

\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bm{B}=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X\times Y)}\bigcup_{i\in I}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\times\bm{B}),

\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\bigg)\times\bm{B}=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X\times Y)}\bigcap_{i\in I}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\times\bm{B}).

Proof.

We show the first equality, and for the rest we proceed similarly. By the equalities shown after
Propositions
4.4.2

we have that

\displaystyle\bm{A}\times\bigcup_{j\in J}\bm{\mu}_{0}(j)

\displaystyle:=\bigg(A^{1}\times\bigcup_{j\in J}\mu_{0}^{1}(j),(A^{0}\times Y)\cup\bigg[X\times\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}\mu_{0}^{0}(j)\bigg)\bigg]\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}(A^{1}\times\mu_{0}^{1}(j)),(A^{0}\times Y)\cup\bigg(\bigcap_{j\in J}(X\times\mu_{0}^{0}(j))\bigg)\bigg)

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X\times Y)}\bigg(\bigcup_{j\in J}(A^{1}\times\mu_{0}^{1}(j)),\bigcap_{j\in J}\big[(A^{0}\times Y)\cup(X\times\mu_{0}^{0}(j))\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{j\in J}(\bm{A}\times\bm{\mu}_{0}(j)).\qed

4.10
Direct families of subsets

Definition 4.10.1
.

Let
(I,\preccurlyeq)
be a directed set, and
X\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
. A
(
covariant
)
direct family of subsets
of
X
indexed by
I
, or
an
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-family of subsets of
X
, is a triplet

\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
, where

\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
\mathcal{E}^{X}
is a modulus of embeddings for
\lambda_{0}

(
see
Definition
4.1.1
)

\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\lambda_{0}(j)\big),\ \ \ \lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}(i,j):=\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq},\ \ \ (i,j)\in\ D^{\preccurlyeq}(I),

a modulus of covariant transport maps for
\lambda_{0}
, such that

\lambda_{ii}:=\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}
, for every
i\in I
, and, for every
(i,j)\in\ D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)
,
the following left diagram
commutes
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
X
.
X
\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}
\mathcal{E}_{i}\
\ \mathcal{E}_{j}
\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}
\mathcal{E}_{i}\
\ \mathcal{E}_{j}

A contravariant
(I,\succcurlyeq)
-family of subsets of
X
is defined dually i.e.,

\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in\preccurlyeq(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\lambda_{0}(j),\lambda_{0}(i)\big),\ \ \ \lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq}(i,j):=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq},\ \ \ (i,j)\in\ D^{\preccurlyeq}(I),

is a modulus of contravariant transport maps for
\lambda_{0}
,

such that for every
(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)
, the above right diagram commutes.

Proposition 4.10.2
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
a directed set,
\lambda_{0}:I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
,
\mathcal{E}^{X}
a modulus
of embeddings for
\lambda_{0}
, and
\lambda_{1}
a modulus of transport maps for
\lambda_{0}
. The
following are equivalent.

(i)

\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
is an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-family of subsets of
X
.

(ii)

\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
and
\mathcal{E}^{X}\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}\Rightarrow C^{\preccurlyeq,X}
,
where
C^{\preccurlyeq,X}
is the constant
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-family
X

(
see Definition
3.8.1
)
.

Proof.

We proceed exactly as in the proof of Proposition
4.1.2
.
∎

If
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
is an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-family of subsets of
X
, and
if
i\preccurlyeq_{I}j
, then
\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}_{ij}\colon\lambda_{0}(i)\subseteq\lambda_{0}(j)
i.e.,
\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}

is a
modulus of subset-witnesses
for
\lambda_{0}
.

Definition 4.10.3
.

If
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})andM^{\preccurlyeq}(X):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})

are
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-families of subsets of
X
, a
direct family of subsets-map

\Psi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)

from
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)
to
M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)
is a family of subsets-map
\Phi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow M(X)
.
Their set
\textnormal{{Map}}_{(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X),M^{\preccurlyeq}(X))
is the set

\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda(X),M(X))
. The composition of direct family of subsets-maps, and the totality

\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\preccurlyeq_{I},X)
of
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-families of subsets of
X
are defined as the composition of
family of subsets-maps, and as the totality
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
, respectively. The
totality
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\succcurlyeq,X)
of contravariant direct families of subsets
of
X
over
(I,lt_{I})
and the corresponding family-maps are defined similarly.

Proposition 4.10.4
.

Let
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}),M(X):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\preccurlyeq_{I},X)
.

(i)

If
\Psi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)
, then
\Psi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}
.

(ii)

If
\Psi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)
and
\Phi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)
, then

\Phi=_{\textnormal{{Map}}_{(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X),M^{\preccurlyeq}(X))}\Psi
.

Proof.

We proceed exactly as in the proof of Proposition
4.1.6

∎

The interior union and intersection of
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(M))
, are defined as for
an
I
-family of subsets
\Lambda(X)
. As in the case of
\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
and
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, the equality of
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
does not imply the externally defined equality of

\sum_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
, only the converse is true i.e.,

(i,x)=_{{\sum_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y)\Rightarrow(i,x)=_{{\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}}(j,y),

as, if there is some
k\in I
such that
i\preccurlyeq_{I}k,j\preccurlyeq_{I}k
, and
\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(k)}\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}(y)
, then by the equalities
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathcal{E}_{k}\circ\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}
and

\mathcal{E}_{j}=\mathcal{E}_{k}\circ\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}
we get

\mathcal{E}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{E}_{k}\big(\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}(x)\big)=\mathcal{E}_{k}\big(\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}(y)\big)=\mathcal{E}_{j}(y)
.

4.11
Notes

Note 4.11.1
.

The definition of a family of subsets given by Bishop in
[
9
]
, p. 65, was the rough description we gave at
the beginning of this chapter. Our definition
4.1.1
highlights the witnessing data of the
rough description, and it is in complete analogy to Richman’s definition of a set-indexed family of sets,
included later by Bishop and Bridges in
[
19
]
, p. 78.
In
[
19
]
, p. 80, and in
[
9
]
, p. 65, an alternative definition of a family
of subsets of
X
indexed by
I
is given, as a subset
\Lambda
of
X\times I
. The fact
that
(x,i)\in\Lambda
can be interpreted as
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)

This definition though, which was never used by Bishop, does not reveal the witnessing data for the
equality
\lambda_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}(X)}\lambda_{0}(j)
, if
i=_{I}j
, and it is not possible to connect with
the notion of a family of sets.
The definition of a set of subsets is given by Bishop in
[
9
]
, p. 65, and
it is repeated in
[
19
]
, p. 69. The example of the set of detachable subsets of a set is given in
[
9
]
,
p. 65, where the term
free
subsets
is used instead,
and it is repeated in
[
19
]
, p. 70.

Note 4.11.2
.

There are many examples of families of subsets in the literature of Bishop-style
constructive mathematics. In topology a neighborhood space (in
[
19
]
, p. 75, the reference
to the indices is omitted for simplicity) is a pair
(X,N)
, where

X
is a set and
N
is a family
\nu
of subsets of
X
indexed by some set
I
such that

\forall_{i,j\in I}\forall_{x\in X}\big(x\in\nu(i)\cap\nu(j)\Rightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\big(x\in\nu(k)\subseteq\nu(i)\cap\nu(j)\big)\big).

The covering property is not mentioned there.
If
(X,F)
is a Bishop space (see
[
19
]
, chapter 3, and
[
88
]
),
the neighborhood structure
N_{F}
on
X
generated by the Bishop topology
F
on
X
is the
family
U
of subsets of
X
indexed by
F
that assigns to every element
f\in F
the set

U(f):=\{x\in X\mid f(x)>0\}.

If
f=g
, then
U(f)=U(g)
, while the converse is not true (take e.g.,
f=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}

and
g=2\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}
, where
X={\mathbb{R}}
and
F={\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
). In real analysis
sequences of bounded intervals of
{\mathbb{R}}
are considered in
[
19
]
Problem 1, p. 292.
In the theory of normed linear spaces a sequence of
bounded, located, open, convex sets is constructed in the proof of the separation theorem (see
[
19
]
,
pp. 336–340). A family
N_{t}^{*}
, for every
t>0
, of subsets of the unit sphere of the dual space

X^{*}
of a separable normed space
X
occurs in the proof of Theorem (6.8) in
[
19
]
, p. 354.
In constructive algebra families of ideals and families of submodules of an
R
-module are studied
(see
[
76
]
, p. 44, and p. 53, respectively).

Note 4.11.3
.

In
[
19
]
, p. 69, the interior union
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is defined as the totality

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):=\big\{x\in X\mid\exists_{i\in I}\big(x\in\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\big\}.

Using our notation though, in
[
19
]
, pp. 69–70 it is written that

\ldots
to construct an element
u
of

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
we first
construct an element
i
of
I
, and then construct an element
x
of
\lambda_{0}(i)
.

Clearly, what is meant by the totality
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)

is what is written in Definition
4.2.1
. The intersection of an
I
-family
\lambda
of subsets of
X
is roughly
defined in
[
9
]
, p. 70, as

\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):=\big\{x\in X\mid\forall_{i\in I}\big(x\in\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\big\},

while the more precise definition that follows this simplified notation is different,
and it is based on the undefined in
[
9
]
and
[
19
]

notion of a dependent operation over
\lambda
, hence it is not that precise. Moreover, the definition of

\prod_{i\in I}^{{X}}\lambda_{0}(i)
, given in
[
19
]
, p. 70, as the set

\bigg\{f:I\to\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\mid\forall_{i\in I}\big(f(i)\in\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\bigg\}

is not compatible with the precise definition of
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, and
it is not included in
[
9
]
.

Note 4.11.4
.

One could have defined an
I
-family of disjoint subsets of
X
with respect to given
inequalities
\neq_{I}
and
\neq_{X}
(Definition
4.2.1
) by

\forall_{i,j\in I}\big(i\neq_{I}j\Rightarrow\neg\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\between\lambda_{0}(j)\big)\big),\ \ \ \ \mbox{or}

\forall_{i,j\in I}\big(\lambda_{0}(i)\between\lambda_{0}(j)\Rightarrow i=_{I}j\big).

The first definition is negativistic, while the second, which avoids
\neq_{I}
, is too strong.

Note 4.11.5
.

The classical proof of the extension theorem of coverings (Theorem
4.2.6
)
is based on the definition of the interior union as the set

\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i):=\big\{x\in X\mid\exists_{i\in I}\big(x\in\lambda_{0}(i)\big)\big\}
.
As a result, the required function
f\colon X\to Y
is defined as follows: If
x\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
,
there is
i\in I
such that

x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
. Then, one defines
f(x)=f_{i}(x)
, and shows that the value
f(x)
does not depend on the choice
of
i
(see
[
45
]
, p. 13). The use of choice is avoided in our proof, because of the embedding
e\colon X\hookrightarrow\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
.
Theorem
4.2.6
is related to the notion of a
sheaf of sets
.
The sheaf-property added to the notion of a presheaf
is exactly the main condition of
Theorem
4.2.6
, where the covering of
X
is an open covering i.e., a covering of
open subsets (see
[
53
]
).

Note 4.11.6
.

If
P(I)
is a partition of
I
, such that
p_{0}(k)\neq\emptyset
, for every
k\in K
, and if

T:=\prod_{k\in K}p_{0}(k),

then the converse inclusion to the semi-distributivity of
\bigcap
over
\bigcup
(Proposition
4.5.5
) holds classically, and the distributivity of
\bigcap
over
\bigcup
holds classically. The converse inclusion to the semi-distributivity of
\bigcap
over
\bigcup
is equivalent to the axiom of choice (see
[
45
]
, p. 25). It is expected that this converse inclusion is constructively provable only if non-trivial data are added to the hypotheses.

Note 4.11.7
.

In the hypothesis of Proposition
4.6.16
we need to suppose the existence of a modulus of centres of
contraction to avoid choice in the definition of function
g
. Proposition
4.6.16
is our translation
of Theorem 4.4.3 of book-HoTT into
\mathrm{BST}
. In the formulation of Theorem 4.4.3 of
[
126
]
no modulus of centres of
contraction is mentioned, as the type-theoretic axiom of choice is provable in
\mathrm{MLTT}
.

Note 4.11.8
.

As an equivalence structure
(X,R_{X})
is the analogue to the set
(X,=_{X})
, one can equip
(X,R_{X})

with an extensional relation
I_{X}
on
X\times Y
satisfying the properties of an inequality.
In this way the structure
(X,R_{X},I_{X})
becomes the equivalence relation-analogue to the set
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
.

Note 4.11.9
.

Examples of families of partial functions are found in the predicative reconstruction of the Bishop-Cheng measure
theory in
[
131
]
and
[
104
]
.

Note 4.11.10
.

There are many examples of families of complemented subsets in the literature of Bishop-style
constructive mathematics. In the theory of normed linear spaces, sequences of complemented subsets
occur in the formulation
of the constructive version of Lebesgue’s decomposition of measures (see
[
19
]
, pp. 329–331), and in the
formulation of the constructive Radon-Nikodym theorem (see
[
19
]
, pp. 333–334).
In the integration theory of
[
19
]
, the sequences of integrable sets in an
integrable space
X
(see
[
19
]
, pp. 234–235) are families of subsets
of
X
indexed by
{\mathbb{N}}
. Sequences of measurable sets are considered in
[
19
]
, pp. 269–271.
Moreover, a measure space (see
[
19
]
, p. 282) is defined as a triplet
(X,M,\mu)
, where

M
is a set of complemented sets in an inhabited set
X
. In the definition of complete measure
space in
[
19
]
, pp. 288–289, the notion of a sequence of elements of
M
is also used.

Note 4.11.11
.

In the measure theory developed in
[
9
]
certain families
\mathcal{F}
(and subfamilies
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{F}
)
of complemented subsets of some set
X

are considered in the definition of a measure space (see
[
9
]
, p. 183).
For the definition of a measure space found in
[
9
]
, p. 183, Myhill writes
in
[
80
]
, p. 351, the following:

The only one of the classical set-existence axioms (not counting choice) which is
missing
3
3

3

He means from his system
\mathrm{CST}
.
is power set. Certainly there is no hint of this axiom
in Bishop’s book (except for
\mathcal{F}
on p. 183,
surely a slip
4
4

4

Our emphasis.
),
or for that matter anywhere in Brouwer’s writings prior to 1974.

In our view, Myhill is wrong to believe first, that the use of family of
\mathcal{F}
requires the powerset axiom,
and, second, that its use from Bishop is surely a slip.
The notion of family of subsets does not imply the use of the powerset as a set, since a family of subsets
is a certain assignment routine from
I
to
\mathbb{V}_{0}
that behaves like a function, without being one.
Moreover, it is not a slip, as it is repeatedly
used by Bishop in the new measure theory, also found in
[
19
]
, and by practicioners of Bishop-style constructive
mathematics, like Bridges and Richman.
It is not a coincidence that the notion of family of subsets is not a fundamental function-like object in Myhill’s system
\mathrm{CST}
.

Note 4.11.12
.

In
[
9
]
, p. 68, the following properties of complemented subsets are mentioned

(\bm{A}\cup-\bm{A})\cap\bigg(\bm{A}\cup\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\bigg)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}(\bm{A}\cup-\bm{A})\cap\bigg[\bigcap_{i\in I}\big(\bm{A}\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)\bigg],

(\bm{A}\cap-\bm{A})\cup\bigg(\bm{A}\cap\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\bigg)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}(\bm{A}\cap-\bm{A})\cup\bigg[\bigcup_{i\in I}\big(\bm{A}\cap\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)\bigg].

These equalities are the constructive analogue of the classical properties

\bm{A}\cup\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigcap_{i\in I}\big(\bm{A}\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big),

\bm{A}\cap\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}}\bigcup_{i\in I}\big(\bm{A}\cap\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big).

Note 4.11.13
.

In
[
18
]
, pp. 16–17, and in
[
19
]
, p. 73,
the join and meet of a countable family of complemented subsets are defined by

\bigvee_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(n):=\bigg(\bigg[\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(\lambda_{0}^{1}(n)\cup\lambda_{0}^{0}(n)\big)\bigg]\cap\bigg[\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{0}^{1}(n)\bigg],\ \ \bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{0}^{0}(n)\bigg),

\bigwedge_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(n):=\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{0}^{1}(n),\ \ \bigg[\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\big(\lambda_{0}^{1}(n)\cup\lambda_{0}^{0}(n)\big)\bigg]\cap\bigg[\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{0}^{0}(n)\bigg]\bigg).

These definitions can be generalised to arbitrary families of complemented subsets and properties
similar to the ones shown for
\bigcup_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
and
\bigcap_{i\in I}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)
hold.

Note 4.11.14
.

Set-relevant families of subsets over some set
I
, and set-relevant direct families of subsets over
some directed set
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
can be studied in a way similar to set-relevant families of sets over
I

and set-relevant direct families of sets over
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
in section
3.9
. As a
consequence, a theory of generalised direct spectra of subspaces can be developed. Families of families
of subsets of
X
can also be studied, in analogy to families of families of sets (see Section
3.10
).
As
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
is in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
, the families of families of subsets of
X
are defined in
\mathbb{V}_{0}
.

Chapter 5
Families of sets and spectra of Bishop spaces

We connect various notions and results from the theory of families of sets and subsets to the
theory of Bishop spaces, a function-theoretic approach to constructive topology. Associating in
an appropriate way to each set
\lambda_{0}(i)
of an
I
-family of sets
\Lambda
a Bishop
topology
F_{i}
a spectrum
S(\Lambda)
of Bishop spaces is generated. The
\sum
-set and the
\prod
-set
of a spectrum
S(\Lambda)
are equipped with canonical Bishop topologies.
A direct spectrum of Bishop spaces is a family of Bishop spaces associated to a direct family of sets.
The direct and inverse limits of direct spectra of Bishop spaces are studied. Direct spectra of Bishop
subspaces are also examined.
For all notions and facts on Bishop spaces mentioned in this chapter we refer to section
8.1

of the Appendix. Many Bishop topologies are defined inductively within the extension
\mathrm{BISH}^{*}
of
\mathrm{BISH}

with inductive definitions with rules of countably many premises. For all notions and facts on directed
sets mentioned in this chapter we refer to section
8.2
of the Appendix.

5.1
Spectra of Bishop spaces

Roughly speaking, if
S
is a structure on some set, an
S
-spectrum
is
an
I
-family of sets
\Lambda
such that each set
\lambda_{0}(i)
is equipped with a structure
S_{i}
, which is
compatible with the transport maps
\lambda_{ij}
of
\Lambda
.
Accordingly, a spectrum of Bishop spaces is an
I
-family of sets
\Lambda
such that each set
\lambda_{0}(i)

is equipped with a Bishop topology, which is compatible with the transport maps of
\Lambda
. As expected,
in the case of a spectrum of Bishop spaces this compatibility condition is that the transport
maps
\lambda_{ij}
are Bishop morphisms i.e.
\lambda_{ij}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})
. It is natural to associate to

\Lambda
an
I
-family of sets
\Phi:=(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda})
such that
\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\phi_{0}^{\Lambda}(i)\big)
is the Bishop space corresponding to
i\in I
.
If
i=_{I}j
, and if we put no restriction to the definition of
\phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}:F_{i}\to F_{j}
,
we need to add extra data in the definition of a map between spectra of
Bishop spaces. Since the map
\lambda_{ji}^{*}:F_{i}\to F_{j}
, where
\lambda_{ji}^{*}
is the element
of
\mathbb{F}(F_{i},F_{j})
induced by the Bishop morphism
\Lambda_{ji}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{i})
, is
generated by the data of
\Lambda
, it is natural to define
\phi_{ij}:=\lambda_{ji}^{*}
.
In this way proofs of properties of maps between spectra of Bishop spaces become easier. If
X

is a set, we use the notation
\mathbb{F}(X):=\mathbb{F}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
, and every subset of
\mathbb{F}(X)
considered
in this chapter is an extensional subset of it.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\Lambda:=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1})
,
M:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
.
A family of Bishop topologies associated to
\Lambda
is a pair
\Phi^{\Lambda}:=\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda}\big)
, where

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda}\colon I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and
\phi^{\Lambda}\colon\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\phi_{0}^{\lambda}(i),\phi_{0}^{\Lambda}(j)\big)
, such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda}(i):=F_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i))
, and
\mathcal{F}_{i}:=(\lambda_{0}(i),F_{i})

is a Bishop space, for every
i\in I
.

(ii)

\lambda_{ij}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})
, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
.

(iii)
\phi_{1}^{\Lambda}(i,j):=\lambda_{ji}^{*}
, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
,
where, if
f\in F_{i}
, the induced map
\lambda_{ji}^{*}\colon F_{i}\to F_{j}
from
\lambda_{ji}
is defined by

\lambda_{ji}^{*}(f):=f\circ\lambda_{ji}
, for every
f\in F_{i}
.

We call the structure
S(\Lambda):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda})

is called a
spectrum of Bishop spaces
over
I
, or an

I
-
spectrum
with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop isomorphisms

(\lambda_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
.

If
S(M):=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\phi_{0}^{M},\phi_{1}^{M})
is an
I
-spectrum with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and
Bishop isomorphisms
(\mu_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
,
a
spectrum-map

\Psi
from
S(\Lambda)
to
S(M)
, in
symbols
\Psi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(M)
, is a family-map

\Psi\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow M
. The totality of spectrum-maps from
S(\Lambda)
to
S(M)
is denoted by

\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(S(\Lambda),S(M))
and it is equipped with the equality of
\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,M)
.
A spectrum-map
\Phi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(M)
is called
continuous
, if

\Psi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i})
, for every
i\in I
, and we denote by
\textnormal{{Cont}}_{I}(S(\Lambda),S(M))
their totality, which is equipped with the equality of
\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,M)
. The totality
\textnormal{{Spec}}(I)
of
I
-spectra of Bishop spaces
is equipped with the equality
S(\Lambda)=_{{\textnormal{{Spec}}(I)}}S(M)
if and only if there exist
continuous spectrum-maps
\Phi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(M)
and
\Psi\colon S(M)\Rightarrow S(\Lambda)
such
that
\Phi\circ\Psi=_{{\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(M,M)}}\mathrm{Id}_{M}
and
\Psi\circ\Phi=_{{\textnormal{{Map}}_{I}(\Lambda,\Lambda)}}\mathrm{Id}_{\Lambda}
.

As the identity map
\mathrm{id}_{X}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
, where
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
is a Bishop space, the identity family-map

\mathrm{id}_{\Lambda}\colon\Lambda\Rightarrow\Lambda
is a continuous spectrum-map from
S(\Lambda)
to
S(\Lambda)
.
As the composition of Bishop morphism is a Bishop morphism, if
\Phi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(M)
and
\Xi\colon S(M)\Rightarrow S(N)
are continuous spectrum-maps, then
\Xi\circ\Phi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(N)
is a continuous spectrum-map.

Definition 5.1.2
.

The structure
S(\mathbb{2}):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}},\lambda_{1}^{\mathbb{2}},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}\big)
, where
\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}:=(\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}},\lambda_{1}^{\mathbb{2}})
is the
\mathbb{2}
-family of

X
and
Y
, and
\Phi^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}:=\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}\big)
is the

\mathbb{2}
-family of the sets
F
and
G
,
\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}(0):=F
is a topology on
X
, and

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}(1):=G
is a topology on
Y
, is the
\mathbb{2}
-spectrum of
\mathcal{F}
and

\mathcal{G}
.

Since
\mathrm{id}_{X}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})
,
\mathrm{id}_{Y}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G})
,
\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}(0,0):=\mathrm{id}_{X}^{*}
with
\mathrm{id}_{X}^{*}:=\mathrm{id}_{F}
, and similarly,
\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}(1,1):=\mathrm{id}_{Y}^{*}
with
\mathrm{id}_{Y}^{*}:=\mathrm{id}_{G}
, we conclude that
S(\mathbb{2})
is a
\mathbb{2}
-spectrum with Bishop
spaces
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}
and Bishop isomorphisms
\mathrm{id}_{X},\mathrm{id}_{Y}
.

Remark 5.1.3
.

Let
S(\Lambda):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda})
be an
I
-spectrum
with Bishop spaces
\mathcal{F}_{i}
and Bishop isomorphisms
\lambda_{ij}
,

S(M):=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\phi_{0}^{M},\phi_{1}^{M})
an
I
-spectrum with Bishop spaces
\mathcal{G}_{i}
and Bishop
isomorphisms
\mu_{ij}
, and
\Psi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(M)
. Then
\Phi^{\Lambda}:=\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I)
, and if
\Psi
is continuous, then, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
, the
following diagram commutes
F_{i}
F_{j}
.
G_{j}
{{G_{i}}}
\lambda_{ji}^{*}
\mu_{ji}^{*}
\Psi_{i}^{*}
\Psi_{j}^{*}

Proof.

If
i\in I
, then
\phi_{ii}^{\Lambda}(f):=f\circ\lambda_{ii}:=f\circ\mathrm{id}_{\lambda_{0}(i)}:=f
.
If
i=_{I}j=_{I}k
and
f\in F_{i}
, then
F_{j}
F_{k}
F_{i}
\lambda_{kj}^{*}
\lambda_{ji}^{*}
\ \lambda_{ki}^{*}

\lambda_{kj}^{*}\big(\lambda_{ji}^{*}(f)\big):=\lambda_{kj}^{*}(f\circ\lambda_{ji}):=(f\circ\lambda_{ji})\circ\lambda_{kj}=f\circ(\lambda_{ji}\circ\lambda_{kj})=f\circ\lambda_{ki}:=\lambda_{ki}^{*}(f)
. By the definition of a continuous spectrum-map we have that if
(j,i)\in D(I)
, then

\Psi_{j}^{*}(\mu_{ji}^{*}(g)):=\Psi_{j}^{*}(g\circ\mu_{ji}):=(g\circ\mu_{ji})\circ\Psi_{j}=g\circ(\mu_{ji}\circ\Psi_{j})=g\circ(\Psi_{i}\circ\lambda_{ji})

=(g\circ\Psi_{i})\circ\lambda_{ji}:=\lambda_{ji}^{*}(g\circ\Psi_{i}):=\lambda_{ji}^{*}(\Psi_{i}^{*}(g)).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \qed

5.2
The topology on the
\sum
- and the
\prod
-set of a spectrum

Remark 5.2.1
.

Let
S(\Lambda):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I)

with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop isomorphisms
(\lambda_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
.
If
\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}F_{i}
,
the following operation is a function

f_{\Theta}:\bigg(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)\bigg)\rightsquigarrow{\mathbb{R}},\ \ \ \ f_{\Theta}(i,x):=\Theta_{i}(x);\ \ \ \ (i,x)\in\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i).

Proof.

If
(i,x)=_{\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)}(j,y):\Leftrightarrow i=_{I}j\ \&\ \lambda_{ij}(x)=_{\lambda_{0}(j)}y
,
by the definition of
\prod_{i\in I}F_{i}
we have that
\Theta_{i}=_{F_{i}}\phi_{ji}^{\Lambda}(\Theta_{j}):=\lambda_{ij}^{*}(\Theta_{j}):=\Theta_{j}\circ\lambda_{ij}
, hence

f_{\Theta}(i,x):=\Theta_{i}(x)=_{{\mathbb{R}}}[\Theta_{j}\circ\lambda_{ij}](x)=_{{\mathbb{R}}}\Theta_{j}(y):=f_{\Theta}(j,y).

∎

Definition 5.2.2
.

Let
S(\Lambda):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I)

with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop isomorphisms
(\lambda_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
.
The sum Bishop space of
S(\Lambda)

is the pair

\sum_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\bigg(\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\int_{i\in I}F_{i}\bigg),\ \ \ \ \mbox{where}\ \ \int_{i\in I}F_{i}:=\bigvee_{\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}F_{i}}f_{\Theta},

and the dependent product Bishop space of
S(\Lambda)
is the pair

\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\bigg(\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\oint_{i\in I}F_{i}\bigg),\ \ \ \ \mbox{where}\ \ \oint_{i\in I}F_{i}:=\bigvee_{i\in I}^{f\in F_{i}}\big(f\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}\big),

and
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}
is the projection function defined in
Proposition
3.3.5
(
i
)
.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

Let
S(\Lambda):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I)
with Bishop spaces

(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop isomorphisms
(\lambda_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
,

S(M):=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\phi_{0}^{M},\phi_{1}^{M})\in Spec(I)

with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop isomorphisms
(\mu_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
, and
\Psi\colon S(\Lambda)\Rightarrow S(M)
.

(i)

If
i\in I
, then
e_{i}^{\Lambda}\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\mathcal{F}_{i},\sum_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}\big)
.

(ii)

If
\Psi
is continuous, then

\Sigma\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\sum_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\sum_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i}\big)
.

(iii)

If
\Psi
is continuous, then

\Pi\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i}\big)
.

Proof.

(i) By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms it suffices to show that

\forall_{\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}F_{i}}\big(f_{\Theta}\circ e_{i}^{\Lambda}\in F_{i}\big)
. If

x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, then

\big(f_{\Theta}\circ e_{i}^{\Lambda}\big)(x):=f_{\Theta}(i,x):=\Theta_{i}(x),
and

f_{\Theta}\circ e_{i}^{\Lambda}:=\Theta_{i}\in F_{i}
.

(ii) By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms it suffices to show that

\forall_{\Theta{{}^{\prime}}\in\prod_{i\in I}G_{i}}\big(f_{\Theta{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\Sigma\Psi\in\int_{i\in I}F_{i}\big).

If
i\in I
and
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, we have that

\big(f_{\Theta{{}^{\prime}}}\circ\Sigma\Psi\big)(i,x):=f_{\Theta{{}^{\prime}}}\big(i,\Psi_{i}(x)\big):=\Theta_{i}{{}^{\prime}}\big(\Psi_{i}(x)\big):=f_{\Theta}(i,x),

where
\Theta:\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}F_{i}
is defined by
\Theta_{i}:=\Theta_{i}{{}^{\prime}}\circ\Psi_{i}
,
for every
i\in I
. By the continuity of
\Psi
we get
\Theta_{i}\in F_{i}
. We show that
\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}F_{i}
. If
i=_{I}j
, by the commutativity of the diagram of Remark
5.1.3
we get

\phi_{ij}^{\Lambda}(\Theta_{i}):=\lambda_{ji}^{*}(\Theta_{i}):=\Theta_{i}\circ\lambda_{ji}:=(\Theta_{i}{{}^{\prime}}\circ\Psi_{i})\circ\lambda_{ji}:=(\Theta_{i}{{}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{ji})\circ\Psi_{j}=\Theta_{j}{{}^{\prime}}\circ\Psi_{j}:=\Theta_{j}
.

(iii) By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms it suffices to show that

\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{g\in G_{i}}\big((g\circ\pi_{i}^{M})\circ\prod\Psi\in\oint_{i\in I}F_{i}\big)
.
If
\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, then

\big[(g\circ\pi_{i}^{M})\circ\Pi\Psi\big](\Theta):=g(\Psi_{i}(\Theta_{i})):=\big[(g\circ\Psi_{i})\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}\big](\Theta),

hence
(g\circ\pi_{i}^{M})\circ\Pi\Psi=(g\circ\Psi_{i})\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}
. By the
continuity of
\Psi
we have that
\Psi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i})
, hence
g\circ\Psi_{i}\in F_{i}
, and

(g\circ\Psi_{i})\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda}\in\oint_{i\in I}F_{i}
.
∎

If
S(\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}})
is the spectrum of the Bishop spaces
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
,
its sum Bishop space

\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G}:=\bigg(\sum_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\lambda_{0}^{\mathbb{2}}(i),\int_{i\in\mathbb{2}}\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\mathbb{2}}}(i)\bigg):=(X+Y,F+G)

is called the
coproduct
of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.
By definition of the sum Bishop topology

F+G:=\bigvee_{f\in F,g\in G}f\oplus g,

(f\oplus g)(w):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}f(x)&\mbox{, $\exists_{x\in X}\big(w:=(0,x)\big)$}\\
g(y)&\mbox{, $\exists_{y\in Y}\big(w:=(1,y)\big)$}\end{array}\right.;\ \ \ \ f\in F,\ g\in G.

The coproduct Bishop space is the coproduct in the category of Bishop spaces.

Proposition 5.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
and
\mathcal{G}:=(Y,G)
be Bishop spaces.

(i)

The function
i_{X}:X\to X+Y
, defined by
x\mapsto(0,x)
, for every
x\in X
,
is in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G})
.

(ii)

The function
i_{Y}:Y\to X+Y
, defined by
y\mapsto(1,y)
, for every
y\in Y
,
is in
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G})
.

(iii)

If
\mathcal{H}:=(Z,H)
is a Bishop space,
\phi_{X}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{H})
and
\phi_{Y}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, there is a unique
\phi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
such that the following
inner diagrams commute
X
X+Y
Z
Y.
\phi
\phi_{X}\
i_{X}
i_{Y}
\ \phi_{Y}

Proof.

(i) By definition
i_{X}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}+\mathcal{G})
if and only if

\forall_{f\in F}\forall_{g\in G}\big((f\oplus g)\circ i_{X}\in F\big)
. It is
immediate to see that
(f\oplus g)\circ i_{X}:=f\in F
. Case (ii) is shown similarly.

(iii) We define
\phi:X+Y\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

ϕ
(w) := {

ϕ
_X(x)

,
\exists_{x\in X}\big(w:=(0,x)\big)

ϕ
_Y(y)

,
\exists_{y\in y}\big(w:=(1,y)\big)
,

and since
\phi\circ i_{X}:=\phi_{X}
and
\phi\circ i_{Y}:=\phi_{Y}
, the diagrams commute.
If
h\in H
, then
(h ∘

.
The uniqueness of

is immediate to show.
∎

Proposition 5.2.5
.

If
F
is a topology on
X
,
G
is a topology on
Y
,
F_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{F}(X,{\mathbb{R}})
,
and
G_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{F}(Y,{\mathbb{R}})
are inhabited, then

\bigg(\bigvee F_{0}\bigg)+G=\bigvee_{f_{0}\in F_{0},g\in G}f_{0}\oplus g:=F_{0}+G,

F+\bigg(\bigvee G_{0}\bigg)=\bigvee_{f\in F,g_{0}\in G_{0}}f\oplus g_{0}:=F+G_{0}.

Proof.

We prove only the first equality, and the proof of the second is similar. Clearly,

F_{0}+G\subseteq\big(\bigvee F_{0}\big)+G.

Since
\big(\bigvee F_{0}\big)+G:=\bigvee_{f\in\bigvee F_{0},g\in G}f\oplus g,

and since
F_{0}+G
is a topology, for the converse inclusion
it suffices to show inductively
that
\forall_{f\in\bigvee F_{0}}P(f)
, where

P(f):\Leftrightarrow\big(\forall_{g\in G}\big(f\oplus g\in F_{0}+G\big)\big).

If
f_{0}\in F_{0}
, then
P(f_{0})
follows immediately. If
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
g\in G
, we show
that
\overline{a}^{X}\oplus g\in F_{0}+G
.
Since
\frac{g}{3}-\overline{a}^{Y}\in G
, by the inductive hypothesis on
f_{0}\in F_{0}
, we
get
f_{0}\oplus\big(\frac{g}{3}-\overline{a}^{Y}\big)\in F_{0}+G
. Since
(*) (f_1 ⊕g_1) + (f_2 ⊕g_2) = (f_1 + f_2) ⊕(g_1 + g_2),
and since
\overline{a}^{X}\oplus\overline{a}^{Y}=\overline{a}^{X+Y}\in F_{0}+G
, by
(*)
we get

\big(f_{0}+\overline{a}^{X}\big)\oplus\frac{g}{3}=\big(f_{0}\oplus\big(\frac{g}{3}-\overline{a}^{Y}\big)\big)+\big(\overline{a}^{X}\oplus\overline{a}^{Y}\big)\in F_{0}+G
.
Since by the inductive hypothesis
\big(f_{0}\oplus-\frac{2g}{3}\big)\in F_{0}+G
, and since
-(f\oplus g)=(-f)\oplus(-g)
, we also get
\big(-f_{0}\oplus\frac{2g}{3}\big)\in F_{0}+G
, hence by
(*)

a
⊕g =
[
(f_0 +
a
^X) ⊕

g

3

]
+
[(
-f_0
⊕

2g

3

)]

∈F_0 + G.
Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in\bigvee F_{0}
such that
P(f_{1})
and
P(f_{2})
. If
g\in G
, by these hypotheses
we get
f_{1}\oplus\frac{g}{2}\in F_{0}+G
and
f_{2}\oplus\frac{g}{2}\in F_{0}+G
. Hence by
(*)

(f_1 + f_2) ⊕g =
(
f_1 ⊕

g

2

)
+
(
f_2 ⊕

g

2

)
∈F_0 + G.
If
\phi\in{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
and
f\in\bigvee F_{0}
such that
P(f)
, we show
P(\phi\circ f)
. If
g\in G
, then
(**)
ϕ
∘(f ⊕g) := (
ϕ
∘f) ⊕(
ϕ
∘g).
By
P(f)
we get
f\oplus\overline{0}^{Y}\in F_{0}+G
,
and since
\phi\circ\overline{0}^{Y}=\overline{\phi(0)}^{Y}\in F_{0}+G
,
by
(**)

(
ϕ
∘f) ⊕
ϕ
(0)
^Y =
(
ϕ
∘f
)
⊕
(
ϕ
∘
0
^Y
)
=
ϕ
∘
(
f ⊕
0
^Y
)
∈F_0 + G.
By the case of constant functions
\overline{0}^{X}\oplus\big(g-\overline{\phi(0)}^{Y}\big)\in F_{0}+G
, hence by
(*)

(
ϕ
∘f) ⊕g =
[
(
ϕ
∘f) ⊕

(0)
^Y
]
+
[
0
^X
⊕
(
