Theorem 5.6.5
.

Let
S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}})

be a contravariant direct spectrum over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
,
(J,e,\textnormal{{cof}}_{J})
a cofinal subset of
I
, and
S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}\circ e):=\big((\lambda_{0}\circ e,\lambda_{1}\circ e,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}\circ e},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}\circ e}\big)

the relative spectrum of
S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})
to
J
. Then

\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{j}\simeq\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}.

Proof.

If
\Theta\in\prod_{j\in J}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(j)
, then, if
j\preccurlyeq j{{}^{\prime}}
, we have that

\Theta_{j}=\lambda_{j{{}^{\prime}}j}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{j{{}^{\prime}}}\big):=\lambda_{e(j{{}^{\prime}})e(j)}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{j{{}^{\prime}}}\big)
.
If
i\in I
, then
\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)\in J
and
\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\in\lambda_{0}(e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)))
. Since

i\preccurlyeq e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))
, we define the operation

\phi:\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(j)\rightsquigarrow\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)
, by the rule
\Theta\mapsto\phi(\Theta)
, for every
\Theta\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(j)
,
where

[\phi(\Theta)]_{i}:=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)\in\lambda_{0}(i);\ \ \ \ i\in I.

First we show that
\phi
is well-defined i.e.,
\phi(\Theta)\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
i.e.,
for every
i,i{{}^{\prime}}\in I
,

i\preccurlyeq i{{}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow[\phi(\Theta)]_{i}=\lambda_{i{{}^{\prime}}i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big([\phi(\Theta)]_{i{{}^{\prime}}}\big)
.
Working as in the proof of Lemma
5.5.11
(ii), we get

\displaystyle\lambda_{i{{}^{\prime}}i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big([\phi(\Theta)]_{i{{}^{\prime}}}\big)

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{i{{}^{\prime}}i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}}))i{{}^{\prime}}}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}})}\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\big[\lambda_{i{{}^{\prime}}i}^{\succcurlyeq}\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}}))i{{}^{\prime}}}^{\succcurlyeq}\big]\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}})}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq: eq1})}}{{=}}\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}}))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}})}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq: eq2})}}{{=}}\big[\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}}))e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))}^{\succcurlyeq}\big]\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}})}\big)

\displaystyle:=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}}))e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i{{}^{\prime}})}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)

\displaystyle:=[\phi(\Theta)]_{i}.

To show that
\phi
is a function we consider the following equivalences:

\displaystyle\phi(\Theta)=_{{\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\phi(H)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\big[\phi(\Theta)\big]_{i}=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big[\phi(H)\big]_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)\big),\ \ \ (1)

\Theta=_{{\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(j)}}H:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{j\in J}\big(\Theta_{j}=_{{\lambda_{0}(e(j))}}H_{j}\big)\ \ \ \ \ (2).

To show that (1)
\Rightarrow
(2) we use the fact that
e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(j))=j
, and since
j\preccurlyeq j
, by the
extensionality of
\preccurlyeq
we get
j\preccurlyeq e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(j))
. Since

\Theta_{j}=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)
, and

H_{j}=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)
, we get (2).
By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms

\phi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{j},\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{f\in F_{i}}\big((f\circ\pi_{i}^{S^{\succcurlyeq}})\circ\phi\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}F_{j}\big)
.
If
\Theta\in\prod_{j\in J}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(j)
, we have that

\displaystyle[(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S^{(}\Lambda{\succcurlyeq})})\circ\phi](\Theta)

\displaystyle:=f\big(\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}(\phi(\Theta))\big)

\displaystyle:=f\big(\big[\phi(\Theta)\big]_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:=f\big(\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(f\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big)\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big)

\displaystyle:=\big[\big(f\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big)\circ\pi_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})\circ e}\big](\Theta),

hence

\big(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)\circ\phi:=\big(f\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big)\circ\pi_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})\circ e}\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}F_{j}
,
as by definition
\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))},\mathcal{F}_{i})
, and hence
\lambda_{0}(e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)))
\lambda_{0}(i)
{\mathbb{R}}
\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}
f
f\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\ \

f\circ\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\in F_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))}:=F_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}
.
Let the operation
\theta\colon\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(j)
,
defined by the rule
H\mapsto\theta(H):=H^{J}
, for every

H\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
, where

H_{j}^{J}:=H_{e(j)}\in\lambda_{0}(e(j))
, for every
j\in J
. We show that
H^{J}\in\prod_{j\in J}\lambda_{0}(j)
.
If
j\preccurlyeq j{{}^{\prime}}
, then

H_{j}^{J}=\lambda_{e(j{{}^{\prime}})e(j)}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H_{j{{}^{\prime}}}^{J}\big):\Leftrightarrow H_{e(j)}=\lambda_{e(j{{}^{\prime}})e(j)}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H_{e(j{{}^{\prime}})}\big),

which holds by the hypothesis
H\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
. Moreover,
we have that
\phi(H^{J})=H:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\big[\phi(H^{J})\big]_{i}=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}H_{i}\big)
.
If
i\in I
, and since
i\preccurlyeq e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))
, we have that

\big[\phi(H^{J})\big]_{i}:=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H^{J}_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i)}\big):=\lambda_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))i}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(i))}\big)=H_{i}.

It is immediate to show that
\theta
is a function. Moreover,
\theta(\phi(\Theta)=\Theta
, as if
j\in J
, then

\phi(\Theta)_{j}^{J}:=\phi(\Theta)_{e(j)}:=\lambda^{\succcurlyeq}_{e(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(e(j))e(j)}\big(\Theta_{\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(e(j))}\big)=\Theta_{j},

as by hypothesis
\Theta_{j}=\lambda_{e(j{{}^{\prime}})e(j)}^{\succcurlyeq}(\Theta_{j{{}^{\prime}}})
, with
j\preccurlyeq j{{}^{\prime}}
, and by
(\textnormal{{Cof}}_{1})
we
have that

j=_{J}(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(e(j))
, hence by the extensionality of
\preccurlyeq
we get
j\preccurlyeq(\textnormal{{cof}}_{J}(e(j))
. Finally,

\theta\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{j}\big)\Leftrightarrow\forall_{j\in J}\forall_{f\in F_{j}}\big(\big(f\circ\pi_{j}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)\circ\theta\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}F_{i}\big)
, which follows from the equalities

\displaystyle\big[\big(f\circ\pi_{j}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)\circ\theta\big](H)

\displaystyle:=\big(f\circ\pi_{j}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)(H^{J})

\displaystyle:=f\big(H^{J}_{j}\big)

\displaystyle:=f\big(H_{e(j)}\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(f\circ\pi_{e(j)}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)(H).\qed

Proposition 5.6.6
.

If
(I,\preccurlyeq),(J,\preccurlyeq)
are directed sets,

with Bishop spaces

and Bishop morphisms

,

is a contravariant direct

with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{G}_{j})_{j\in J}
and Bishop morphisms

, there is a function

\times:\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)\times\prod_{j\in J}^{\succcurlyeq}\mu_{0}(j)\to\prod_{(i,j)\in I\times J}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)\times\mu_{0}(j)\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}\times\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{G}_{j},\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\times\mathcal{G}_{j})\big).

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Proposition
5.5.15
.
∎

5.7
Duality between direct and inverse limits of spectra

Proposition 5.7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F),\mathcal{G}:=(Y,G)
and
\mathcal{H}:=(Z,H)
be Bishop spaces, and let

\lambda\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}),\mu\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
. We define the mappings

\lambda^{+}\colon\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})\to\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}),\ \ \ \ \lambda^{+}(\phi):=\phi\circ\lambda;\ \ \ \ \phi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F}),

\mu^{-}\colon\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{H})\to\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}),\ \ \ \ \mu^{-}(\theta):=\mu\circ\theta;\ \ \ \ \theta\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{H}),

Y
Z
X
Z
Y
.
\lambda
\phi
{\phi\circ\lambda}
{\mu\circ\theta}\
\theta
\mu

{}^{+}\colon\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})\to\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}),\ \ \ \ \lambda\mapsto\lambda^{+};\ \ \ \ \lambda\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}),

{}^{-}\colon\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})\to\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}),\ \ \ \ \mu\mapsto\mu^{-};\ \ \ \ \mu\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}).

Then
{}^{+}\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H},(\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F})\to(\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F})\big)
and

{}^{-}\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G},(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{H})\to(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G})\big)
.

Proof.

By definition and the
\bigvee
-lifting of the exponential topology we have that

\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}:=\bigg(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}),\bigvee_{y\in Y}^{h\in H}\phi_{y,h}\bigg),\ \ \ \ \mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F}:=\bigg(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F}),\bigvee_{z\in Z}^{f\in F}\phi_{z,f}\bigg),

\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}:=\bigg(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}),\bigvee_{y\in Y}^{f\in F}\phi_{y,f}\bigg),

(\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F})\to(\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}):=\bigg(\mathrm{Mor}((\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})\to\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F})),\bigvee_{\varphi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})}^{e\in G\to F}\phi_{\varphi,e}\bigg),

\bigvee_{\varphi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})}^{e\in G\to F}\phi_{\varphi,e}=\bigvee_{\varphi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})}^{y\in Y,f\in F}\phi_{\varphi,\phi_{y,f}}.

By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms we have that

{}^{+}\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H},(\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F})\to(\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F})\big)\Leftrightarrow\forall_{\varphi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})}\forall_{y\in Y}\forall_{f\in F}\big(\phi_{\varphi,\phi_{y,f}}\circ\ ^{+}\in G\to H\big).

If
\lambda\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
, we have that

\phi_{\varphi,\phi_{y,f}}\circ\ ^{+}](\lambda):=\phi_{\varphi,\phi_{y,f}}(\lambda^{+}):=(\phi_{y,f}\circ\lambda^{+})(\varphi):=(\phi_{y,f}(\varphi\circ\lambda):=[f\circ(\varphi\circ\lambda)](y):=[(f\circ\varphi)\circ\lambda](y):=\phi_{y,f\circ\varphi}(\lambda)

i.e.,
\phi_{\varphi,\phi_{y,f}}\circ\ ^{+}:=\phi_{y,f\circ\varphi}\in G\to H
,
since
\varphi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{F})
and hence
f\circ\varphi\in H
. For the mapping
-
we work similarly.
∎

Next we see how with the use of the exponential Bishop topology we can get a contravatiant spectrum
from a covariant one, and vice versa.

Proposition 5.7.2
.

(A)

Let
S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I,\preccurlyeq)
and
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
a Bishop space.

(i)

If
S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{M^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{M^{\succcurlyeq}})
, where

M^{\succcurlyeq}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq})

is a contravariant direct family of sets over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with
\mu_{0}(i):=\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})
and

\mu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq}(i,j):=\big(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathcal{F}),\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}),(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{+}\big),

and if
\phi_{0}^{M^{\succcurlyeq}}(i):=F_{i}\to F
and
\phi_{1}^{M^{\succcurlyeq}}(i,j):=\big(F_{i}\to F,F_{j}\to F,[(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{+}]^{*}\big)
, then
S^{\preccurlyeq}\to\mathcal{F}
is a contravariant
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-spectrum
with Bishop spaces
(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}))_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

\big((\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{+}\big)_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{F}\to S^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{N^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{N^{\preccurlyeq}})
, where

N^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})
is a direct family of sets over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with
\nu_{0}(i):=\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})
and

\nu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}(i,j):=\big(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i}),\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{j}),(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{-}\big),

and if
\phi_{0}^{N^{\preccurlyeq}}(i):=F\to F_{i}
and
\phi_{1}^{N^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j):=\big(F\to F_{j},F\to F_{i},[(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{-}]^{*}\big)
, then
\mathcal{F}\to S^{\preccurlyeq}
is a covariant
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-spectrum with
Bishop spaces
(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i}))_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

\big((\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{-}\big)_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
.

(B)

Let
S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}})

be a contravariant
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-spectrum, and
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
a Bishop space.

(i)

If
S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}})
, where

M^{\preccurlyeq}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})

is a direct family of sets over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with
\mu_{0}(i):=\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})
and

\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq}(i,j):=\big(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}),\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathcal{F}),(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq})^{+}\big),

and if
\phi_{0}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}}(i):=F_{i}\to F
and
\phi_{1}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,j):=\big(F_{j}\to F,F_{i}\to F,[(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq})^{+}]^{*}\big)
, then
S^{\succcurlyeq}\to\mathcal{F}
is an
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-spectrum with
Bishop spaces
(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}))_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

\big((\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq})^{+}\big)_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
.

(ii)

If
\mathcal{F}\to S(N^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{N^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{N^{\succcurlyeq}})
, where

N^{\succcurlyeq}:=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq})

is a contravariant direct family of sets over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with
\nu_{0}(i):=\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})
and

\nu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq}(i,j):=\big(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{j}),\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i}),(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq})^{-}\big),

and if
\phi_{0}^{N^{\succcurlyeq}}(i):=F\to F_{i}
and
\phi_{1}^{N^{\succcurlyeq}}(i,j):=\big(F\to F_{i},F\to F_{j},[(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq})^{-}]^{*}\big)
, then
\mathcal{F}\to S^{\preccurlyeq}
is a contravariant
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-spectrum with
Bishop spaces
(\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

\big((\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})^{-}\big)_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
.

Proof.

We prove only the case (A)(i) and for the other cases we work similarly.
It suffices to show that if
i\preccurlyeq j\preccurlyeq k
, then the following diagram commutes
\mathrm{Mor}(F_{j},\mathcal{F})
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{k},\mathcal{F}).
\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})
\big(\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}
\big(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}
\ \ \ \big(\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}

If
\phi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{k},\mathcal{F})
, then

\big(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}\big[\big(\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}(\phi)\big]:=\big(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}[\phi\circ\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}]:=(\phi\circ\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq})\circ\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq}:=\phi\circ(\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}\circ\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})=\phi\circ\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}:=\big(\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}(\phi)
.
∎

Similarly to the
\bigvee
-lifting of the product topology, if

S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}})
a contravariant direct spectrum over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with Bishop spaces

\big(F_{i}=\bigvee F_{0i}\big)_{i\in I}
, then

\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}F_{i}=\bigvee_{i\in I}^{f\in F_{0i}}\big(f\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}}\big).

Theorem 5.7.3

(Duality principle)
.

Let
S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I,\preccurlyeq)
with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}

and Bishop morphisms
(\lambda_{ij}^{\preccurlyeq})_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
. If
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
is a Bishop space and

S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{M^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{M^{\succcurlyeq}})
is the contravariant
direct spectrum over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
defined in Proposition
5.7.2

(A)(i),

then

\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F})\simeq[(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\mathcal{F}].

Proof.

First we determine the topologies involved in the required Bishop isomorphism.
By definition and by the above remark on the
\bigvee
-lifting of the
\prod^{\succcurlyeq}
-topology we have that

\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}):=\bigg(\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mu_{0}(i),\bigvee_{i\in I}^{g\in F_{i}\to F}g\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}}\bigg),

F_{i}\to F:=\bigvee_{x\in\lambda_{0}(i)}^{f\in F}\phi_{x,f},

\bigvee_{i\in I}^{g\in F_{i}\to F}g\circ\pi_{i}^{S^{\preccurlyeq}\to\mathcal{F}}=\bigvee_{i\in I}^{x\in\lambda_{0}(i),f\in F}\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}},

\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\bigg(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i),\bigvee_{{\Theta\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}F_{i}}}\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}f_{\Theta}\bigg),

\big(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}\big)\to\mathcal{F}:=\bigg(\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}),\bigvee_{{\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\in\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)}}^{f\in F}\phi_{{\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)},f}\bigg),

\phi_{{\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)},f}(h):=(f\circ h)\big(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\big)

{\mathbb{R}}
.
X
\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)
f\in F
f\circ h\ \ \
{h\in\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})}

If
H\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})
, let the operation
\theta(H):\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)\rightsquigarrow X
, defined by

\theta(H)\big(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\big):=H_{i}(x);\ \ \ \ \textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\in\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i).

We show that
\theta(H)
is a function. If

\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)=_{{\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(j,y)\Leftrightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\big(i,j\preccurlyeq k\ \&\ \lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(k)}}\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}(y)\big),

we show that

\theta(H)\big(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\big):=H_{i}(x)=_{X}H_{j}(y)=:\theta(H)\big(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(j,y)\big)
.
By the equalities

H_{i}=\big(\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}(H_{k})=H_{k}\circ\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}
and

H_{j}=\big(\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)^{+}(H_{k})=H_{k}\circ\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}
we get

H_{i}(x)=\big(H_{k}\circ\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)(x):=H_{k}\big(\lambda_{ik}^{\preccurlyeq}(x)\big)=_{X}H_{k}\big(\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}(y)\big):=\big(H_{k}\circ\lambda_{jk}^{\preccurlyeq}\big)(y):=H_{j}(y).

Next we show that

\theta(H)\in\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(f\circ\theta(H)\in\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}F_{i}\big)
.
If
f\in F
, then the dependent assignment routine

\Theta:\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}F_{i}
, defined by
\Theta_{i}:=f\circ H_{i}
, for every
i\in I
{\mathbb{R}}
X
\lambda_{0}(i)
f\in F
f\circ H_{i}\ \ \
{H_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})}

is in
\prod_{i\in I}^{\preccurlyeq}F_{i}
i.e., if
i\preccurlyeq j
, then

, since

.
Hence
f\circ\theta(H):=\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}f_{\Theta}\in\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}F_{i}
, since

[f\circ\theta(H)]\big(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\big):=f\big(H_{i}(x)\big):=(f\circ H_{i})(x):=f_{\Theta}(i,x):=\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}f_{\Theta}\big(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\big).

Consequently, the operation
\theta\colon\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})\rightsquigarrow\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})
, defined by the rule
H\mapsto\theta(H)
,
is well-defined. Next we show that
\theta
is an embedding.

\displaystyle\theta(H)=\theta(K)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{{\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)\in\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big(\theta(H)(\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x))=\theta(K)(\omega_{S^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x))\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(H_{i}(x)=_{X}K_{i}(x)\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow H=K.

Next we show that
\theta\in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}),(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\mathcal{F}\big)

By the equalities

we get
\phi_{{\textnormal{{eql}}_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}}(i,x)},f}\circ\theta=\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}}
. Let

, where

, for every
i\in I

.

, since

With respect to the possible dual to the previous theorem i.e., the isomorphism

\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F})\simeq[(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\mathcal{F}]
, what we can

Proposition 5.7.4
.

Let

S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}})
be a contravariant direct spectrum over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with Bishop spaces

(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\lambda_{ji}^{\succ})_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
. If
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
is a Bishop space and

S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})\to\mathcal{F}:=(\mu_{0},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}})
is the
(I,\preccurlyeq)
-directed
spectrum defined in Proposition
5.7.2

(B)(i),

there is a function

\ \widehat{}\ \colon\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}[\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})]\to\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})

such that the following hold:

(i)

\ \widehat{}\ \in\mathrm{Mor}\big(\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}_{i}\to\mathcal{F}),(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\to\mathcal{F}\big)
.

(ii)

If for every
j\in J
and every
y\in\lambda_{0}(j)
there is

\Theta_{y}\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)

such that
\Theta_{y}(j)=_{{\lambda_{0}(j)}}y
,
then
\ \widehat{}\
is an embedding of
\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}[\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})]
into

\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

We proceed similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.7.3
.
∎

Theorem 5.7.5
.

Let
S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}})
be a contravariant direct spectrum over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with Bishop spaces

(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\lambda_{ji}^{\prec})_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
.
If
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
is a Bishop space and

\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}):=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{{}^{\succcurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{N^{\succcurlyeq}})

\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i})\simeq[\mathcal{F}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}].

First we determine the topologies involved in the required Bishop isomorphism:

\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i}):=\bigg(\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i}),\bigvee_{i\in I}^{g\in F\to F_{i}}g\circ\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\bigg),

, then

\lambda_{0}(i)
.
\lambda_{0}(j)
X
\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}
H_{i}\
H_{j}

Let the operation

, defined by

x\mapsto[e(H)](x)
, where
\big[[e(H)](x)\big]_{i}:=H_{i}(x)
, for every
i\in I
.
First we show that
[e(H)](x)\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
. If
i\preccurlyeq j
, then

\big[[e(H)](x)\big]_{i}:=H_{i}(x)=\big(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\circ H_{j}\big)(x):=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(H_{j}(x)\big):=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\big[[e(H)](x)\big]_{j}\big)
.
Next we show that
e(H)
is a function. If
x=_{X}x{{}^{\prime}}
, then

\forall_{i\in I}\big(H_{i}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}H_{i}(x{{}^{\prime}})\big):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(\big[[e(H)](x)\big]_{i}=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big[[e(H)](x{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{i}\big):\Leftrightarrow[e(H)](x)=_{{\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}}[e(H)](x{{}^{\prime}})
.
By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms

e(H)\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{f\in F_{i}}\big(\big(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)\circ e(H)\in F\big).

Since

\displaystyle e(H)=e(K)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\big([e(H)](x)=_{{\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}}[e(K)](x)\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\forall_{i\in I}\big(H_{i}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}K_{i}(x)\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{x\in X}\big(H_{i}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}K_{i}(x)\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\big(H_{i}=_{{\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})}}K_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow H=_{{\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})}}K.

By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms we show that

e\in\mathrm{Mor}(\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i}),\mathcal{F}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{f\in F_{i}}\big(\phi_{x,f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}}\circ e\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(F\to F_{i})\big)

\displaystyle\big(\phi_{x,f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}}\circ e\big)(H)

\displaystyle:=\phi_{x,f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}}\big(e(H)\big)

\displaystyle:=\big[(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})})\circ e(H)\big](x)

\displaystyle:=(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})})\big([e(H)](x)\big)

\displaystyle:=f\big([e(H)(x)]_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:=f\big(H_{i}(x)\big)

\displaystyle:=(f\circ H_{i})(x)

\displaystyle:=\phi_{x,f}\big(H_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:=\big[\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big](H)

we get
\phi_{x,f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}}\circ e:=\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\in\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(F\to F_{i})
. Let

\phi\colon\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\rightsquigarrow\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})

First we show that
H^{\mu}_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i}):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F_{i}}\big(f\circ H^{\mu}_{i}\in F\big)
. If
f\in F_{i}
, and
x\in X
, then

[f\circ H^{\mu}_{i}(x):=f\big(H^{\mu}_{i}\big):=f\big([\mu(x)]_{i}\big):=\big[\big(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S^{(}\Lambda{\succcurlyeq})}\big)\circ\mu\big](x)

i.e.,
f\circ H^{\mu}_{i}:=\big(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big)\circ\mu\in F
,
as
\mu\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})
.
Since
\mu(x)\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)
,

[\mu(x)]_{i}=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\big([\mu(x)]_{j}\big),

for every
i,j\in I
such that
i\preccurlyeq j
. To show that
H_{\mu}\in\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})
,
let
i\preccurlyeq j
. Then

\displaystyle H^{\mu}_{i}=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\circ H^{\mu}_{j}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\big(H^{\mu}_{i}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big[\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\circ H^{\mu}_{j}\big](x)\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\big([\mu(x)]_{i}=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}\big[\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\big([\mu(x)]_{j}\big)\big),

which holds by the previous remark on
\mu(x)
. It is immediate to show that
\phi
is a function.
To show that

\phi\in\mathrm{Mor}\big([\mathcal{F}\to\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}],\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i})\big)
,
we show that

\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{f\in F_{i}}\forall_{x\in\lambda_{0}(i)}\bigg(\big[\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big]\circ\phi\in\bigvee_{x\in X,i\in I}^{f\in F_{i}}\phi_{x,f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}}\bigg),

\displaystyle\big[\big[\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big]\circ\phi\big](\mu)

\displaystyle:=\big[\phi_{x,f}\circ\pi_{i}^{\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\big](H^{\mu})

\displaystyle:=\phi_{x,f}\big(H^{\mu}_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:=(f\circ H_{i}^{\mu})(x)

\displaystyle:=f\big(\mu(x)_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:=(f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}\circ\mu)(x)

\displaystyle:=\big[\phi_{x,f\circ\pi_{i}^{S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq})}}\big](\mu).

Moreover,
\phi(e(H)):=H
, as
H^{e(H)}_{i}(x):=[e(H)(x)]_{i}:=H_{i}(x)
, and
e(\phi(\mu))=\mu
, as

\displaystyle e(H^{\mu})=\mu

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\big([e(H^{\mu})](x)=_{{\prod_{i\in I}^{\succcurlyeq}\lambda_{0}(i)}}\mu(x)\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\forall_{i\in I}\big(H^{\mu}_{i}(x)=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}[\mu(x)]_{i}\big)

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in X}\forall_{i\in I}\big([\mu(x)]_{i}=_{{\lambda_{0}(i)}}[\mu(x)]_{i}\big).\qed

With respect to the possible dual to the previous theorem i.e., the isomorphism

\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i})\simeq(\mathcal{F}\to\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})
,
what we can show is the following proposition.

Proposition 5.7.6
.

Let
S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I)
with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}

and Bishop morphisms
(\lambda_{ij}^{\prec})_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
. If
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
is a Bishop space and

\mathcal{F}\to S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}):=(\nu_{0},\nu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{N^{\preccurlyeq}},\phi_{1}^{N^{\preccurlyeq}})
is the

(I,\preccurlyeq)
-direct spectrum defined in Proposition
5.7.2

(A)(ii),

there is a map
\ \widehat{}\ :\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}[\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{i})]\to\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})

with
\ \widehat{}\ \in\mathrm{Mor}\big((\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}(\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}_{i}),\mathcal{F}\to\underset{\to}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}\big)
.

Proof.

We proceed similarly to the proof of Theorem
5.7.5
.
∎

5.8
Spectra of Bishop subspaces

Definition 5.8.1
.

If
\Lambda(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X)
,
a family of Bishop subspaces of the Bishop space
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
associated to
\Lambda(X)
is a pair
\Phi^{\Lambda(X)}:=\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)
, where

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)}\colon I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and
\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)}(i),\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)}(j)\big)
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)}(i):=F_{i}:=F_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}:=\bigvee_{f\in F}f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
,
for every
i\in I
.

(ii)

\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}(i,j):=\lambda_{ji}^{*}
, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
.

We call the structure

S_{F}(\Lambda(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)})

a spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
over
I
, or an
I
-spectrum
of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
. If
S_{F}(M(X)):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{M(X)},\phi_{1}^{M(X)})

is an
I
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
, a subspaces spectrum-map
\Psi
from

S_{F}(\Lambda(X))
to
S_{F}(M(X))
, in symbols

\Psi\colon S_{F}(\Lambda(X))\Rightarrow S_{F}(M(X))
, is a family of subsets-map
\Psi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow M(X)
.
If
F
is clear from the context, we may omit the symbol
F
as a subscript in the above notations.

The topology
F_{i}
on
\lambda_{0}(i)
is the relative Bishop topology of
F
to
\lambda_{0}(i)
, and
it is the least topology that makes the embedding
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
a Bishop morphism from
\mathcal{F}_{i}
to
\mathcal{F}
.
In contrast to the external framework of a spectrum of Bishop spaces, we can prove that the transport
maps
\lambda_{ij}
of a spectrum of subspaces
S_{F}(\Lambda(X))
are always Bishop morphisms.
The extensionality of a Bishop topology
F
on a set
X
as a subset of
\mathbb{F}(X)
is crucial to the next proof.

Remark 5.8.2
.

Let
S_{F}(\Lambda(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)})
be an

I
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
, and
S_{F}(M(X)):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{M(X)},\phi_{1}^{M(X)})

an
I
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
.

(i)

S(\Lambda):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)})
is
an
I
-spectrum with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop isomorphisms
(\lambda_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D(I)}
.

(ii)

If
\Psi\colon S(\Lambda(X))\Rightarrow S(M(X))
, then
\Psi
is continuous i.e.,
\Psi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i})
,
for every
i\in I
.

Proof.

(i) It suffices to show that
\lambda_{ij}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})
, for every
(i,j)\in D(I)
. By the

\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms we have that

\lambda_{ij}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{j})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big((f\circ\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X})\circ\lambda_{ij}\in F_{i}\big):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(f\circ(\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\circ\lambda_{ij})\in F_{i}\big)
.
If we fix some
f\in F
, and as
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\circ\lambda_{ij}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),X)}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
, we get

f\circ(\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\circ\lambda_{ij})=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i))}f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
. Since
f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\in F_{i}

by the extensionality of
F_{i}
we get
f\circ(\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}\circ\lambda_{ij})\in F_{i}
.

(ii) By the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms we have that

\Psi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big((f\circ\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i})\circ\Psi_{i}\in F_{i}\big):\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(f\circ(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i}\circ\Psi_{i})\in F_{i}\big)
.
Since
\Psi\colon\Lambda(X)\Rightarrow M(X)
, we get
\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{X}\circ\Psi_{i}=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i),X)}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
, and
hence
f\circ(\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{X}\circ\Psi_{i})=_{\mathbb{F}(\lambda_{0}(i))}f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
, for every
i\in I
and
f\in F
.
By the definition of
F_{i}
we have that
f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\in F_{i}
, and hence by the extensionality of
F_{i}

we conclude that
f\circ(\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{X}\circ\Psi_{i})\in F_{i}
.
∎

Definition 5.8.3
.

Let
\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,X)
be the totality of spectra of subspaces of the Bishop space
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
over
I
,
equipped with the equality of
\textnormal{{Spec}}(I,X)
.

Definition 5.8.4
.

Let
S_{F}(\Lambda(X)):=\big(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)\in\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,X)
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
.
The canonical Bishop topology on the interior union
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the relative topology of
F
to it i.e.,

\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\bigg(\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\bigcup_{i\in I}F_{i}\bigg),

\bigcup_{i\in I}F_{i}:=\bigvee_{f\in F}f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)},

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)(i,x):=f(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(x));\ \ \ \ (i,x)\in\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i).

The canonical Bishop topology on

\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
is the relative topology of
\mathcal{F}
to it i.e.,

\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\bigcap_{i\in I}F_{i}\bigg),

\bigcap_{i\in I}F_{i}:=\bigvee_{f\in F}f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)},

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)(\Phi):=f(\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\Phi_{i_{0}}));\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i).

Next follows the continuous-analogue to Proposition
4.3.6
, using
repeatedly the
\bigvee
-lifting of morphisms and the extensionality of a Bishop topology.

Proposition 5.8.5
.

Let
S(\Lambda(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,X)

with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
,

S(M(X)):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{M(X)},\phi_{1}^{M(X)})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,X)

with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{Z}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
,
and
\Psi\colon S(\Lambda(X))\Rightarrow S(M(X))
.

(i)

e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i})
, for every
i\in I
.

(ii)

\bigcup\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i})
.

(iii)

\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})
, for every
i\in I
.

(iv)

\bigcap\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i})
.

Proof.

(i)
e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)\circ e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\in F_{i}\big)
. If
f\in F
, then

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)\circ e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}:=f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\in F_{i}
,
since, for every
x\in\lambda_{0}(i)
, we have that

\big[\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)\circ e_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\big](x):=\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)(i,x):=\big(f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})(x)
.

(ii)
\bigcup\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcup\Psi\in\bigcup_{i\in I}F_{i}\big)
, and

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcup\Psi=f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\bigcup_{i\in I}F_{i}
, as

\big[\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcup\Psi\big](i,x):=\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{M(X)}\big)\big((i,\Psi_{i}(x))\big):=f\big(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i}(\Psi_{i}(x))\big)

=\big((f\circ\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i})\circ\Psi_{i}\big)(x)=(f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})(x)\\
:=\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)(i,x).

(iii)
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big((f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X})\in\bigcap_{i\in I}F_{i}\big)
, and

(f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}=f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\bigcap_{i\in I}F_{i}
,
as

\big[(f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})\circ\pi_{i}^{\Lambda(X)}\big](\Phi):=f(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i}))=f(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}})):=\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}\big)(\Phi)
.

(iv)
\bigcap\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcap\Psi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}F_{i}\big)
, and

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcap\Psi=f\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}\in\bigcup_{i\in I}F_{i}
, as

\big[\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcap\Psi\big](\Phi):=\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)\big(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i_{0}}(\Psi_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}}))\big)=\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}})\big):=f\big(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i_{0}}(\Phi_{i_{0}})\big):=\big(\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)(\Phi).

∎

The notions mentioned in the next proposition were defined in Proposition
4.3.8
.

Proposition 5.8.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
,
(\mathcal{G}:=(Y,G)
be Bishop spaces,
h:X\to Y\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
,

S(\Lambda(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},F;\phi_{0}^{\Lambda(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda(X)})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,X)

with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{E}^{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
,

S(M(Y)):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{Y},\mu_{1},G,\phi_{0}^{M(Y)},\phi_{1}^{M(Y)})\in\textnormal{{Spec}}_{G}(I,Y)

with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{Z}^{Y}_{i})_{i\in I}
,
and
\Psi:\Lambda(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h\ }}{{\Rightarrow}}M(Y)
.

(i)

\Psi
is continuous i.e.,
\Psi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i})
, for every
i\in I
.

(ii)

\bigcup_{h}\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i})
.

(iii)

\bigcap_{h}\Psi\in\mathrm{Mor}(\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{F}_{i},\bigcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{G}_{i})
.

Proof.

(i)
\Psi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mathcal{G}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{g\in G}\big((g\circ E_{i})\circ\Psi_{i}\in F_{i}
,
and if
g\in G
, then

(g\circ\mathcal{Z}^{Y}_{i})\circ\Psi_{i}:=g\circ(\mathcal{Z}^{Y}_{i}\circ\Psi_{i})=g\circ(h\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}):=(g\circ h)\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\in F_{i}
, as
h\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
, and hence
g\circ h\in F
.

(ii) and (iii) Working as in the proof of the Proposition
5.8.5
(ii) and (iv),
we get

\big(g\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcup_{h}\Psi=(g\circ h)\circ e_{{\bigcup}}^{\Lambda(X)}
and

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{M(X)}\big)\circ\bigcap_{h}\Psi=(g\circ h)\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X)}
,
for every
g\in G
.
∎

5.9
Direct spectra of Bishop subspaces

Definition 5.9.1
.

If
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\preccurlyeq,X)
,
a family of Bishop subspaces of the Bishop space
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)

associated to
\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)

is a pair
\Phi^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}:=\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}\big)
, where

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}\colon I\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{V}_{0}
and
\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}:\bigcurlywedge_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}\mathbb{F}\big(\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}(j),\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}(i)\big)
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}(i):=F_{i}:=F_{|\lambda_{0}(i)}:=\bigvee_{f\in F}f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
,
for every
i\in I
.

(ii)

\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)}(i,j):=\big(\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}_{ij}\big)^{*}
, for every
(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)
.

We call the structure

S_{F}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)})

a
(
covariant
)
direct spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
over
I
, or
an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and
Bishop morphisms

(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
. If
S_{F}(M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},F,\phi_{0}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)})

is an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and
Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
, a subspaces direct spectrum-map

\Psi
from
S_{F}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X))
to
S_{F}(M^{\preccurlyeq}(X))
, in symbols

\Psi\colon S_{F}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X))\Rightarrow S_{F}(M^{\preccurlyeq}(X))
, is a direct family of
subsets-map
\Psi\colon\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)\Rightarrow M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)

(
see Definition
4.10.3
)
.
If
F
is clear from the context, we may omit he symbol
F
as a subscript in the above notations.
A contravariant direct spectrum
S_{F}(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)})
of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
over
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})

and a subspaces contravariant direct
spectrum-map
are defined similarly.

Remark 5.9.2
.

Let
S_{F}(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)})
be an

(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}

and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
, and
S_{F}(M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)):=(\mu_{0},\mathcal{Z}^{X},\mu^{\preccurlyeq}_{1},F,\phi_{0}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{M^{\preccurlyeq}(X)})

an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-spectrum of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop
morphisms
(\mathcal{Z}^{X}_{i})_{i\in I}
.

(i)

S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}):=(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}^{\preccurlyeq},\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X)})
is
an
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
-spectrum with Bishop spaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\lambda^{\preccurlyeq}_{ij})_{(i,j)\in D^{\preccurlyeq}(I)}
.

(ii)

If
\Psi\colon S(\Lambda^{\preccurlyeq}(X))\Rightarrow S(M^{\preccurlyeq}(X))
, then
\Psi
is continuous.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Remark
5.8.2
.
∎

Definition 5.9.3
.

Let
\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,\preccurlyeq,X)
be the totality of covariant direct spectra of subspaces of the
Bishop space
\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
and let

\textnormal{{Spec}}_{F}(I,\succcurlyeq,X)
be the totality of contravariant direct spectra of subspaces of
\mathcal{F}
over
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I}
,
equipped with the equality of
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\preccurlyeq,X)
and
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\succcurlyeq,X)
, respectively.

Definition 5.9.4
.

If

S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)})
is contravariant direct spectrum of subspaces of the Bishop space

\mathcal{F}:=(X,F)
over
(I,\preccurlyeq)
with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms

(\mathcal{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
, its inverse limit is the following
Bishop space

\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)):=\underset{\leftarrow_{X}}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\bigg(\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\bigvee_{f\in F}f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\bigg).

Next we show the universal property of the inverse limit for
\underset{\leftarrow_{X}}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}

Proposition 5.9.5
.

If

S(\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)):=\big(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E},\lambda_{1}^{\succcurlyeq},F,\phi_{0}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)},\phi_{1}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\big)\in\textnormal{{Spec}}(I,\succcurlyeq,X)

with Bishop subspaces
(\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
and Bishop morphisms
(\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X})_{i\in I}
,
its inverse limit
\underset{\leftarrow}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i}
satisfies the universal property of inverse limits
i.e., if
i\preccurlyeq_{j}
, the following left diagram commutes
\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(i)
\lambda_{0}(j),
Y
\ \ \pi_{j}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\
\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}
\ \varpi_{j}
\varpi_{i}\
\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}

and for every Bishop space
\mathcal{G}:=(Y,G)
and a family
(\varpi_{i})_{i\in I}
, where

\varpi_{i}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}_{i})
, for every
i\in I
, such that the above right diagram commutes, there is a
unique Bishop morphism
h:Y\to\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
such that the following diagrams commute
Y
\lambda_{0}(j)
,
\lambda_{0}(i)
\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
\ \ \varpi_{j}
\varpi_{i}\
\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\ \ \ \ \ \
\ \ \pi_{j}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}
\pi_{i}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\
\ \ \ h

Proof.

For the commutativity of the first diagram, we have that if
\Phi\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, then

\pi_{i}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}(\Phi):=\Phi_{i}
, and
\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\pi_{j}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}}(\Phi)\big):=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}(\Phi_{j})
, and since
\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\circ\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}
, we have that

\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(\Phi_{j})=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\big(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}(\Phi_{j})\big)
, hence by the definition of

\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
we get
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\Phi_{i})=\mathcal{E}_{j}^{X}(\Phi_{j})=\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\big(\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}(\Phi_{j})\big)
, and since
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
is an embedding we get
\Phi_{i}=\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}(\Phi_{j})=:\lambda_{ji}^{\succcurlyeq}\big(\pi_{j}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}(\Phi)\big)
.
Let a Bishop space
\mathcal{G}:=(Y,G)
and a family of Bishop morphisms
(\varpi_{i})_{i\in I}
, where

\varpi_{i}:Y\to\lambda_{0}(i)
, for every
i\in I
, such that the above right diagram commutes. Let also the
operation
h:Y\rightsquigarrow\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, defined by the rule
y\mapsto h(y)
, where

h(y):\bigcurlywedge_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i),\ \ \ \ h(y)_{i}:=\varpi_{i}(y);\ \ \ \ i\in I.

To show that
h(y)\in\bigcap_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
we need to show that

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\big(h(y)_{i}\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i{{}^{\prime}}}^{X}\big(h(y)_{i{{}^{\prime}}}\big)\Leftrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\varpi_{i}(y))=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i{{}^{\prime}}}^{X}(\varpi_{i{{}^{\prime}}}(y)),

for every
i,i{{}^{\prime}}\in I
. Since
(I,\preccurlyeq_{I})
is directed, there is
k\in I
such that
i\preccurlyeq_{I}k
and
i{{}^{\prime}}\preccurlyeq_{I}k
,
hence

\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(\varpi_{i}(y))=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}\big(\lambda_{ki}^{\succcurlyeq}(\varpi_{k}(y))\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{k}^{X}\big(\varpi_{k}(y)\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i{{}^{\prime}}}^{X}\big(\lambda_{ki{{}^{\prime}}}^{\succcurlyeq}(\varpi_{k}(y))\big)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i{{}^{\prime}}}^{X}(\varpi_{i{{}^{\prime}}}(y)).

It is immediate to show that
h
is a function. Finally, we show that

h\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\underset{\leftarrow_{X}}{\mathrm{Lim}}\mathcal{F}_{i})\Leftrightarrow\forall_{f\in F}\big(\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\big)\circ h\in G\big).

If
y\in Y
, then

\big[\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\big)\circ h\big](y):=f\big(\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X}(\varpi_{i_{0}}(y))\big):=[(f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X})\circ\varpi_{i_{0}}](y),

hence

\big(f\circ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda^{\succcurlyeq}(X)}\big)\circ h:=(f\circ\mathcal{E}_{i_{0}}^{X})\circ\varpi_{i_{0}}\in G
, as by our hypothesis
\varpi_{i_{0}}\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{F}_{i_{0}})
.
∎

5.10
Notes

Note 5.10.1
.

The theory of Bishop spaces, that was only sketched by Bishop in
[
9
]
, and revived
by Bridges in
[
26
]
, and Ishihara in
[
63
]
, was developed by the author in
[
88
]
-
[
96
]

and
[
98
]
-
[
101
]
.
Since inductive definitions with rules of countably many premises are used, for the study of Bishop spaces
we work within
\mathrm{BST}^{*}
, which is
\mathrm{BST}
extended
with such inductive definitions. A formal system for
\mathrm{BISH}
extended with such definitions
is Myhill’s formal system
\mathrm{CST}^{*}

with dependent choice, where
\mathrm{CST}^{*}
is Myhill’s extension of his formal system of constructive set theory

\mathrm{CST}
with inductive definitions (see
[
80
]
). A variation of
\mathrm{CST}^{*}
is Aczel’s system CZF together
with a very weak version of Aczel’s regular extension axiom
(REA),
to accommodate these inductive definitions (see
[
1
]
).

Note 5.10.2
.

In contrast to topological spaces, in the theory of Bishop spaces continuity of functions is
an a priori notion, while the concept of an open set comes a posteriori, through the neighbourhood
space induced by a Bishop topology. The theory of Bishop spaces can be seen as an abstract and
constructive approach to the theory of the ring
C(X)
of continuous functions of a topological
space
(X,\mathcal{T})
(see
[
52
]
for a classical treatment of this subject).

Note 5.10.3
.

The results on the direct and inverse limits of direct spectra of Bishop spaces are the constructive
analogue to the classical theory of direct and inverse limits of (spectra of) topological spaces, as
this is developed e.g., in the Appendix of
[
45
]
. As in the case of the classic textbook of Dugundji,
we avoid here possible, purely categorical arguments in our proofs. One of the advantages of working with a proof-relevant
definition of a cofinal subset is that the proof of the cofinality theorem
5.5.12
is choice-free.

Note 5.10.4
.

The notion of a spectrum of Bishop spaces can be generalised by considering a family of Bishop spaces
associated to a set-relevant family of sets over some set
I
. In this case, all transport maps
\lambda_{ij}^{m}

are taken to be Bishop morphisms. The direct versions of set-relevant spectra of Bishop spaces can be defined,
and their theory can be developed in complete analogy to the theory of direct spectra of Bishop spaces,
as in the case of generalised direct spectra of topological spaces (see
[
45
]
, p. 426).

Note 5.10.5
.

The formulation of the universal properties of the various limits of spectra of Bishop spaces included
here is impredicative, as it requires quantification over the class of Bishop spaces. A predicative
formulation of a universal property can be given, if one is restricted to a given set-indexed family of Bishop spaces.

Note 5.10.6
.

The study of the direct limit of a spectrum of Bishop subspaces is postponed for future work. The natural candidate
\bigcup_{i\in I}\lambda_{0}(i)
, equipped with the relative topology, “almost” satisfies the universal property of the direct limit.

Chapter 6
Families of subsets in measure theory

We study the Borel and Baire sets within Bishop spaces as a constructive counterpart to the study
of Borel and Baire algebras within topological spaces. As we use the inductively defined least Bishop
topology, and as the Borel and Baire sets over a family of
F
-complemented subsets are defined inductively,
we work within the extension
\mathrm{BISH}^{*}
of
\mathrm{BISH}
with inductive definitions with rules of countably many premises.
In contrast to the classical theory, we show that the Borel and the Baire sets of a Bishop space coincide.
Our reformulation within
\mathrm{BST}
of the Bishop-Cheng definition of a measure space and of an integration space,
based on the notions of families of complemented subsets and of families of partial functions, facilitates
a predicative reconstruction of the originally impredicative Bishop-Cheng measure theory.

6.1
The Borel sets of a Bishop space

The Borel sets of a topological space
(X,\mathcal{T})
is the least set of subsets of
X
that includes the
open (or, equivalently the closed) sets in
X
and it is closed under countable unions, countable
intersections and relative complements. The Borel sets of a Bishop space
(X,F)
is the least set
of complemented subsets of
X
that includes the
basic

F
-complemented subsets of
X
that
are generated by
F
, and it is closed under countable unions and countable intersections. As
the Borel sets of
(X,F)
are complemented subsets, it is not a coincidence that their closure under
complements is provable.

In the next two sections

F
denotes a Bishop topology on a set
X
and
G
a Bishop topology
on a set
Y
. For simplicity, we denote the constant function on
X
with value
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
also by
a
,
and we may write equalities between elements of
\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)
and equalities between elements of
F

without denoting the corresponding subscripts.

Definition 6.1.1
.

If
a,b\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
a\neq_{{\mathbb{R}}}b:\Leftrightarrow|a-b|>0\Leftrightarrow a>b\vee b<a
.
For simplicity we may write
a\neq b
, instead of
a\neq_{{\mathbb{R}}}b
. The inequality
x\neq_{X}^{F}y
on
X

generated by
F
is defined by

x\neq^{F}_{X}y:\Leftrightarrow\exists_{f\in F}\big(f\colon x\neq_{X}^{F}y\big),\ \ \ \ \mbox{where}\ \ \ \ f\colon x\neq_{X}^{F}y:\Leftrightarrow f(x)\neq_{{\mathbb{R}}}f(y).

A complemented subset
\bm{A}
of
X
with respect to
\neq_{X}^{F}
is called an
F
-complemented subset of

X
, and their totality is denoted by
\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)
. An

F
-complemented subset
\bm{A}
of
X
is uniformly
F
-complemented, if

\exists_{f\in F}\big(f\colon A^{1}\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}A^{0}\big),\ \ \ \ \mbox{where}\ \ \ \ f\colon A^{1}\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}A^{0}:\Leftrightarrow\forall_{x\in A^{1}}\forall_{y\in A^{0}}\big(f\colon x\neq_{X}^{F}y\big),

and
\bm{A}
is strongly
F
-complemented,
if there is
f\in F
such that
f\colon A^{1}\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}A^{0}
,
f(x)=1
, for every
x\in A^{1}
, and
f(y)=0
,
for every
y\in A^{0}
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

If
h\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
and
\bm{A}\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{G}}(Y)
, then

h^{-1}(\bm{A})\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in h^{-1}(A^{1})
and
y\in h^{-1}(A^{0})
i.e.,
h(x)\in A^{1}
and
h(y)\in A^{0}
. Let
g\in G

such that
g(h(x))\neq g(h(y))
. Hence,
g\circ h\in F
and
(g\circ h)(x)\neq(g\circ h)(y)
.
∎

Definition 6.1.3
.

We denote by
\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,F,\bm{X})
and
\textnormal{{Set}}(I,F,\bm{X})
the sets of families
and sets of
F
-complemented subsets of
X
, respectively. Let
\bm{O}_{F}(X):=\big(o_{0}^{1,F},\mathcal{O}^{1,X},o_{1}^{1,F},o_{0}^{0,F},\mathcal{O}^{0,X},o_{1}^{0,F}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(F,F,\bm{X})
be the family
of basic open
F
-complemented subsets of
X
, where

\bm{o}_{F}(f):=\big(o_{0}^{1,F}(f),o_{0}^{0,F}(f)\big):=\big([f>0],[f\leq 0]\big),

[f>0]:=\{x\in X\mid f(x)>0\},\ \ \ \ [f\leq 0]:=\{x\in X\mid f(x)\leq 0\},

and, as
[f>0
,
[f\leq 0]
are extensional subsets of
X
, the dependent operations
\mathcal{O}^{1,X},\mathcal{O}^{0,X},o_{1}^{1,F}
, and
o_{1}^{0,F}
are defined by the identity map-rule. If
F
is clear from
the context, we may write
\bm{O}_{F}(X):=\big(o_{0}^{1},\mathcal{O}^{1,X},o_{1}^{1},o_{0}^{0},\mathcal{O}^{0,X},o_{1}^{0}\big)
.

Clearly,
f\colon o_{0}^{1}(f)\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}o_{0}^{0}(f)
, for every
f\in F
. Recall that a sequence of

F
-complemented subsets of
X
is a structure
\bm{B}(X):=\big(\beta_{0}^{1},\mathcal{B}^{1,X},\beta_{1}^{1},\beta_{0}^{0},\mathcal{B}^{0,X},\beta_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},F,\bm{X})
, where
\bm{\beta}(n):=\big(\beta_{0}^{1}(n),\beta_{0}^{0}(n)\big)\in\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)
, and
\beta^{1}_{nn}\colon\beta_{0}^{1}(n)\to\beta_{0}^{1}(n)
and

\beta^{0}_{nn}\colon\beta_{0}^{0}(n)\to\beta_{0}^{0}(n)
are given by
\beta^{1}_{nn}:=\mathrm{id}_{\beta_{0}^{1}(n)}
and

\beta^{0}_{nn}:=\mathrm{id}_{\beta_{0}^{0}(n)}
, respectively, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
. We also write

\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)
and
\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)
, instead of

\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)
and
\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)
, respectively. A family

\bm{A}(X):=\big(\alpha_{0}^{1},\mathcal{A}^{1,X},\alpha_{1}^{1},\alpha_{0}^{0},\mathcal{A}^{0,X},\alpha_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},F,\bm{X})

is defined similarly.

Definition 6.1.4
.

If
\bm{\Lambda}(X):=\big(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,F,\bm{X})
, the set
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))
of Borel sets generated by

\bm{\Lambda}(X)

is defined inductively by the following rules:

(\textnormal{{Borel}}_{1})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \frac{i\in I}{\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\textnormal{{Borel}}_{2})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \frac{\bm{\beta}_{0}(1)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X)),\ \bm{\beta}_{0}(2)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X)),\ldots}{\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))\ \ \&\ \ \bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))}{{\bm{B}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},F,\bm{X})}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\textnormal{{Borel}}_{3})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \frac{\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X)),\ \bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)}\bm{B}}{\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))}{{\bm{A}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},F,\bm{X})}}.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

The corresponding induction principle
\mathrm{Ind}_{\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))}
is the formula

\forall_{i\in I}\big(P(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i))\big)\ \&\ \forall_{\bm{B}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},F,\bm{X})}\bigg[\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\big(\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))\ \&\ P(\bm{\beta}_{0}(n))\big)\Rightarrow

P\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)\bigg)\ \&\ P\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\beta}_{0}(n)\bigg)\bigg]\ \&\

\forall_{\bm{A}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},F,\bm{X})}\forall_{\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))}\bigg([P(\bm{B})\ \&\ \bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)}\bm{B}]\Rightarrow P(\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0))\bigg)

\Rightarrow\forall_{\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))}\big(P(\bm{B})\big),

where
P
is any bounded formula.
Let

\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F}):=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{O}_{F}(X)),

and we call its elements the Borel sets of
\mathcal{F}
.

In
\mathrm{Ind}_{\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{\Lambda}(X))}
we quantify over the sets
\textnormal{{Fam}}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},F,\bm{X})
and
\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},F,\bm{X})
,
avoiding quantification over
\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)
in condition
(\textnormal{{Borel}}_{3})
and treating

\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}}(X)
as a set in
(\textnormal{{Baire}}_{2})
.

Proposition 6.1.5
.

(i)

\bm{O}_{F}(X)
is not in
\textnormal{{Set}}(F,F,\bm{X})
.

(ii)

If
f,g\in F
, then
\bm{o}(f)\cup\bm{o}(g)=\bm{o}(f\vee g)
.

(iii)

If
\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, then
-\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
.

(iv)

There are Bishop space
(X,F)
and
f\in F
such that
\neg[-\bm{o}(f)=\bm{o}(g)]
, for every

g\in F
.

(v)

\bm{o}(f)=\bm{o}([f\vee 0]\wedge 1)
.

Proof.

(i) If
f\in F
, then
\bm{o}(f)=\bm{o}(2f)
, but
\neg(f=2f)
.

(iii) This equality is implied from the following properties for reals

a\vee b>0\Leftrightarrow a>0\vee b>0
and
a\vee b\leq 0\Leftrightarrow a\leq 0\wedge b\leq 0
.

(iv) If
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, then
a\leq 0\Leftrightarrow\forall_{n\geq 1}\big(a<\frac{1}{n}\big)
and
a>0\Leftrightarrow\exists_{n\geq 1}\big(a\geq\frac{1}{n}\big)
, hence

\displaystyle-\bm{o}(f)

\displaystyle:=\big([f\leq 0],[f>0]\big)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\big[\big(\frac{1}{n}-f\big)>0\big],\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\big[\big(\frac{1}{n}-f\big)\leq 0\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{o}\big(\frac{1}{n}-f\big)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(F).

If
P(\bm{B}):\Leftrightarrow-\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, the above equality proves the first step of the
corresponding induction on
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
. The rest of the inductive proof is straightforward.

(v) Let the Bishop space
({\mathbb{R}},{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})})
. If we take
\bm{o}(\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}):=\big([x>0],[x\leq 0]\big)
, and
if we suppose that
-\bm{o}(\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}):=\big([x\leq 0],[x>0]\big)=\big([\phi>0],[\phi\leq 0]\big)=:\bm{o}(\phi)
, for some
\phi\in{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
, then
\phi(0)>0
and
\phi
is not continuous at
0
, which
contradicts the fact that
\phi
is uniformly continuous, hence pointwise continuous, on
[-1,1]
.

(vi) The proof is based on basic properties of
{\mathbb{R}}
, like
a\wedge 1=0\Rightarrow a=0
.
∎

Since
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
is closed under intersections and complements, if
\bm{A},\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
,
then
\bm{A}-\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
. Constructively, we cannot show, in general, that
\bm{o}(f)\cap\bm{o}(g)=\bm{o}(f\wedge g)
. If
f:=\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\in\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})
and
g:=-\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\in{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
,
then
\bm{o}(\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}})\cap\bm{o}(-\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}})=\big([x>0]\cap[x<0],[x\leq 0]\cup[-x\leq 0]\big)=\big(\emptyset,[x\leq 0]\cup[x\geq 0]\big)
Since
x\wedge(-x)=-|x|
, we get
\bm{o}(\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\wedge(-\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}))=\bm{o}(-|x|)=\big(\emptyset,[|x|\geq 0]\big)
. The supposed equality implies that
|x|\geq 0\Leftrightarrow x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0
. Since
|x|\geq 0
is always the case, we get
\forall_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}}\big(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0\big),
which implies LLPO (see
[
24
]
, p. 20). If one add the condition
|f|+|g|>0
, then
\bm{o}(f)\cap\bm{o}(g)=\bm{o}(f\wedge g)
follows constructively. The condition
(\mathrm{BS}_{4})
in the definition of a Bishop space is crucial to the next proof.

Proposition 6.1.6
.

If
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq F
, then
f:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(f_{n}\vee 0)\wedge 2^{-n}\in F
and

\bm{o}(f)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{o}(f_{n})=\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}>0],\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}\leq 0]\bigg).

Proof.

The function
f
is well-defined by the comparison test (see
[
19
]
, p. 32). If
g_{n}:=(f_{n}\vee 0)\wedge 2^{-n}
, for every
n\geq 1
, then

|
∑_n = 1^∞g_n - ∑_n = 1^N g_n
|
=
|
∑_n = N+1^∞g_n
|

≤∑_n = N+1^∞|g_n| ≤∑_n = N+1^∞

1

2

n

⟶

N

0,
the sequence of the partial sums
\sum_{n=1}^{N}g_{n}\in F
converges uniformly to
f
, hence by
\mathrm{BS}_{4}

we get
f\in F
. Next we show that
[f>0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}>0]
. If

x\in X
such that
f(x)>0
, there is
N\geq 1
such that
\sum_{n=1}^{N}g_{n}(x)>0
. By
Proposition (2.16) in
[
19
]
, p. 26, there is
n\geq 1
and
n\leq N
with
g_{n}(x)>0
,
hence
(f_{n}(x)\vee 0)\geq g_{n}(x)>0
, which implies
f_{n}(x)>0
. For the converse inclusion,
if
f_{n}(x)>0
, for some
n\geq 1
, then
g_{n}(x)>0
, hence
f(x)>0
. To show
[f\leq 0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}\leq 0]
, let
x\in X
such that
f(x)\leq 0
, and suppose
that
f_{n}(x)>0
, for some
n\geq 1
. By the previous argument we get
f(x)>0
, which
contradicts our hypothesis
f(x)\leq 0
. For the converse inclusion, let
f_{n}(x)\leq 0
, for
every
n\geq 1
, hence
f_{n}(x)\vee 0=0
and
g_{n}(x)=0
, for every
n\geq 1
. Consequently,
f(x)=0
.
∎

Proposition 6.1.7
.

If
h\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
and
\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{G})
, then
h^{-1}(\bm{B})\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

By the definition of
h^{-1}(\bm{B})
, if
g\in G
, then

\displaystyle h^{-1}(\bm{o}_{G}(g))

\displaystyle:=h^{-1}\big([g>0],[g\leq 0]\big)

\displaystyle:=\big(h^{-1}[g>0],h^{-1}[g\leq 0]\big)

\displaystyle=\big([(g\circ h)>0],[(g\circ h)\leq 0]\big)

\displaystyle:=\bm{o}_{F}(g\circ h)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F}).

If
P(\bm{B}):=h^{-1}(\bm{B})\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, the above equality is the first step of the
corresponding inductive proof on
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{G})
. The rest of the proof follows from the
properties
h^{-1}\big(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{B}_{n}\big)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}h^{-1}(\bm{B}_{n})

and
h^{-1}\big(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{B}_{n}\big)=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}h^{-1}(\bm{B}_{n})
of complemented subsets.
∎

Definition 6.1.8
.

If
\Phi
is an extensional subset of
F
and if
\mathrm{id}_{\Phi}^{F}\colon\Phi\hookrightarrow F
is defined by the
identity map-rule, let

\bm{O}_{\Phi}(X):=\bm{O}_{F}(X)\circ\mathrm{id}_{\Phi}^{F}
be the
\mathrm{id}_{\Phi}^{F}
-subfamily of
\bm{O}_{F}(X)
. We write

\bm{o}_{\Phi}(f):=\bm{o}_{F}(f)
, for every
f\in\Phi
, and let

\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi):=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{O}_{\Phi}(X)).

If
F_{0}
is a subbase of
F
, then,
\textnormal{{Borel}}(F_{0})\subseteq\textnormal{{Borel}}(F)
. More can be said on the relation
between
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
and
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, when
\Phi
is a base of
F
.

Proposition 6.1.9
.

Let
\Phi
be a base of
F
.

(i)

If for every
f\in F
,
\bm{o}_{F}(f)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
, then
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
.

(ii)

If for every
g\in\Phi
and
f\in F
,
f\wedge g\in\Phi
, then

\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
.

(iii)

If for every
g\in B
and every
n\geq 1
,
g-\frac{1}{n}\in\Phi
, then
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
.

Proof.

(i) It follows by a straightforward induction on
\textnormal{{Borel}}(F)
.

(ii) and (iii) Let
f\in F
and
(g_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\Phi

such that
\forall_{n\geq 1}\big(U(f,g_{n},\frac{1}{n})\big)
. Then we have tha

\bm{o}_{F}(f)\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{o}_{\Phi}(g_{n}):=\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}>0],\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}\leq 0]\bigg)

i.e.,
[f>0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}>0]
and
\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}\leq 0]\subseteq[f\leq 0]
; if
x\in X
with
f(x)>0
there is
n\geq 1
with
g_{n}(x)>0
, and if

\forall_{n\geq 1}\big(g_{n}(x)\leq 0\big)
, then for the same reason
\neg[f(x)>0
, hence
f(x)\leq 0
.

Because of (i), for (ii), it suffices to show that
\bm{o}_{F}(f)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
. We show that

\bm{o}_{F}(f)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{o}_{\Phi}(f\wedge g_{n}):=\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[(f\wedge g_{n})>0],\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[(f\wedge g_{n})\leq 0]\bigg)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi).

If
f(x)>0
, then we can find
n\geq 1
such that
g_{n}(x)>0
, hence
f(x)\wedge g_{n}(x)>0
. Hence
we showed that
[f>0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[(f\wedge g_{n})>0]
. For the converse inclusion,
let
x\in X
and
n\geq 1
such that
(f\wedge g_{n})(x)>0
. Then
f(x)>0
and
x\in[f>0]
.
If
f(x)\leq 0
, then
\forall_{n\geq 1}\big(f(x)\wedge g_{n}(x)\leq 0\big)
. Suppose next that

\forall_{n\geq 1}\big(f(x)\wedge g_{n}(x)\leq 0\big)
. If
f(x)>0
, there is
n\geq 1
with
g_{n}(x)>0
,
hence
f(x)\wedge g_{n}(x)>0
, which contradict the hypothesis
f(x)\wedge g_{n}(x)\leq 0
. Hence
f(x)\leq 0
.

Because of (i), for (iii), it suffices to show that
\bm{o}_{F}(f)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi)
. We show that

\bm{o}_{F}(f)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{o}_{\Phi}\big(g_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\big):=\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\big[\big(g_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\big)>0\big],\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\big[\big(g_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\big)\leq 0\big]\bigg)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\Phi).

First we show that
[f>0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\big[\big(g_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\big)>0\big]
.
If
f(x)>0
, there is
n\geq 1
with
f(x)>\frac{1}{n}
, hence, since
-\frac{1}{2n}\leq g_{2n}(x)-f(x)\leq\frac{1}{2n}
, we get

g_{2n}(x)-\frac{1}{2n}\geq\bigg(f(x)-\frac{1}{2n}\bigg)-\frac{1}{2n}=f(x)-\frac{1}{n}>0

i.e.,
x\in\big[\big(g_{2n}-\frac{1}{2n}\big)>0\big]
. For the converse inclusion, let
x\in X
and

n\geq 1
such that
g_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{n}>0
. Since
0<g_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{n}\leq f(x)
, we get
x\in[f>0]
.
Next we show that
[f\leq 0]\subseteq\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[\big(g_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\big)\leq 0]
.
Let
x\in X
with
f(x)\leq 0
, and suppose that
n\geq 1
with
g_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{n}>0
. Then

0\geq f(x)>0
. By this contradiction we get
g_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{n}\leq 0
. For the converse inclusion
let
x\in X
such that
g_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{n}\leq 0
, for every
n\geq 1
, and suppose that
f(x)>0
.
Since we have already shown that

[f>0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\big[\big(g_{n}-\frac{1}{n}\big)>0\big]
,
there is some
n\geq 1
with
g_{n}(x)-\frac{1}{n}>0
, which contradicts our hypothesis,
hence
f(x)\leq 0
.
∎

6.2
The Baire sets of a Bishop space

One of the definitions
1
1

1

A different definition is given in
[
57
]
. See
[
111
]

for the relations between these two definitions.
of the set of Baire sets in a topological space

(X,\mathcal{T})
, which was given by Hewitt in
[
60
]
, is that it is the least
\sigma
-algebra of
subsets of
X
that includes the zero sets of
X
i.e., the sets of the form
f^{-1}(\{0\})
, where

f\in C(X)
. Clearly, a Baire set in
(X,\mathcal{T})
is a Borel set in
(X,\mathcal{T})
, and for many topological spaces,
like the metrisable ones, the two classes coincide. In this section we adopt Hewitt’s notion in Bishop spaces
and the framework of
F
-complemented subsets.

Definition 6.2.1
.

Let
\bm{Z}_{F}(X):=\big(\zeta_{0}^{1,F},\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\zeta_{1}^{1,F},\zeta_{0}^{0,F},\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\zeta_{1}^{0,F}\big)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(F,F,\bm{X})
be the family of zero
F
-complemented subsets of
X
, where

\bm{\zeta}_{F}(f):=\big(\zeta_{0}^{1,F}(f),\zeta_{0}^{0,F}(f)\big):=\big([f=0],[f\neq 0]\big),

[f=0]:=\{x\in X\mid f(x)=0\},\ \ \ \ [f\leq 0]:=\{x\in X\mid f(x)\neq 0\},

and, as
[f=0
,
[f\neq 0]
are extensional subsets of
X
, the dependent operations
\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\zeta_{1}^{1,F}
, and
\zeta_{1}^{0,F}
are defined by the identity map-rule. If
F
is clear from the context,
we may write
\bm{Z}_{F}(X):=\big(\zeta_{0}^{1},\mathcal{Z}^{1,X},\zeta_{1}^{1},\zeta_{0}^{0},\mathcal{Z}^{0,X},\zeta_{1}^{0}\big)
.
Let

\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F}):=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\bm{Z}_{F}(X)),

and we call its elements the Baire sets of
\mathcal{F}
.

Since
a\neq 0:\Leftrightarrow|a|>0\Leftrightarrow a<0\vee a>0
, for every
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we get

\bm{\zeta}(f)=\big([f=0],[|f|>0]\big)=\big([f=0],[f>0]\cup[f<0]\big).

Proposition 6.2.2
.

(i)

\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
is not in
\textnormal{{Set}}(F,F,\bm{X})
.

(ii)

If
f,g\in F
, then
\bm{\zeta}(f)\cap\bm{\zeta}(g)=\bm{\zeta}(|f|\vee|g|)
.

(iii)

If
\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
, then
-\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
.

(iv)

There are Bishop space
(X,F)
and
f\in F
such that
\neg[-\bm{\zeta}(f)=\bm{\zeta}(g)
,
for every
g\in F
.

(vi)

\bm{\zeta}(f)=\bm{\zeta}(|f|\wedge 1)
.

Proof.

(i) If
f\in F
, then
\bm{\zeta}(f)=\bm{\zeta}(2f)
, but
\neg(f=2f)
.

(ii) This equality is implied from the following property for reals

|a|\vee|b|=0\Leftrightarrow|a|=0\wedge|b|=0
and
|a|\vee|b|\neq 0\Leftrightarrow|a|>0\vee|b|>0
.

(iii) If
f\in F
, then
-\bm{\zeta}_{0}(f):=\big([f\neq 0],[f=0]\big)
. If, for every
n\geq 1
,

g_{n}:=\bigg(|f|\wedge\frac{1}{n}\bigg)-\frac{1}{n}\in F,

\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\zeta}(g_{n}):=\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}=0],\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}\neq 0]\bigg)=-\bm{\zeta}(f)\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F}).

First we show that
[f\neq 0]=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}=0]
. If
|f(x)|>0
, there
is
n\geq 1
such that
|f(x)|>\frac{1}{n}
, hence
|f(x)|\wedge\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{n}
,
and
g_{n}(x)=0
. For the converse inclusion, let
x\in X
and
n\geq 1
such that
g_{n}(x)=0\Leftrightarrow|f(x)|\wedge\frac{1}{n}=\frac{1}{n}
, hence
|f(x)|\geq\frac{1}{n}>0
. Next we show that
[f=0]=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[g_{n}\neq 0]
. If
x\in X
such that
f(x)=0
, and
n\geq 1
, then
g_{n}(x)=-\frac{1}{n}<0
. For the converse inclusion, let
x\in X
such that for all
n\geq 1
we have that

g_{n}(x)\neq 0
. If
|f(x)|>0
, there is
n\geq 1
such that
|f(x)|>\frac{1}{n}
, hence
g_{n}(x)=0
,
which contradicts our hypothesis. Hence,
|f(x)|\leq 0
, which implies that
|f(x)|=0\Leftrightarrow f(x)=0
.
If
P(\bm{B}):=-\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
, the above equality proves the first step of the corresponding
induction on
\textnormal{{Baire}}(F)
. The rest of the inductive proof is straightforward
2
2

2

Hence, if we define the
set of Baire sets over an arbitrary family
\Theta
of functions from
X
to
{\mathbb{R}}
, a sufficient condition
so that
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\Theta)
is closed under complements is that
\Theta
is closed under
|.|
, under wedge with

\frac{1}{n}
and under subtraction with
\frac{1}{n}
, for every
n\geq 1
. If
\Theta:=\mathbb{F}(X,2)
, then

-\bm{o}_{\mathbb{F}(X,2)}(f)=\bm{o}_{\mathbb{F}(X,2)}(1-f)=\bm{\zeta}_{\mathbb{F}(X,2)}(f)
, hence by
Proposition
6.1.7
(ii) we get
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathbb{F}(X,2))=\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathbb{F}(X,2))
.
.

(v) Let the Bishop space
({\mathbb{R}},{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})})
. If we take
\bm{\zeta}(\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}):=\big([x=0],[x\neq 0]\big)
,
and if we suppose that
-\bm{\zeta}(\mathrm{id}_{{\mathbb{R}}}):=\big([x\neq 0],[x=0]\big)=\big([\phi=0],[\phi\neq 0]\big)=:\bm{\zeta}(\phi)
, for some
\phi\in{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
, then
\phi(0)>0\vee\phi(0)<0
and

\phi(x)=0
, if
x<0
or
x>0
. Hence
\phi
is not continuous at
0
, which contradicts the fact that

\phi
is uniformly continuous on
[-1,1]
.

(v) Using basic properties of
{\mathbb{R}}
, this proof is straightforward.
∎

As in the case of
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, we cannot show constructively that
\bm{\zeta}(f)\cup\bm{\zeta}(g)=\bm{\zeta}(|f|\wedge|g|)
. If we add the condition
|f|+|g|>0
though, this equality is constructively provable.

Proposition 6.2.3
.

If
(f_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}\subseteq F
, then
f:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|f_{n}|\wedge 2^{-n}\in F
and

\bm{\zeta}(f)=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{\zeta}(f_{n})=\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}=0],\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}\neq 0]\bigg).

Proof.

Proceeding as in the proof of Proposition
6.1.6
,
f
is well-defined, and if
g_{n}:=|f_{n}|\wedge 2^{-n}
,
for every
n\geq 1
,
the sequence of the partial sums
\sum_{n=1}^{N}g_{n}\in F
converges uniformly to
f
, and by
(\mathrm{BS}_{4})

we get
f\in F
. Since
f(x)=0\Leftrightarrow\forall_{n\geq 1}(g_{n}(x)=0)\Leftrightarrow\forall_{n\geq 1}(f_{n}(x)=0)
,
we get
