[f=0]=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}=0]
. To show
[f\neq 0]\subseteq\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}[f_{n}\neq 0]
,
if
|f(x)|>0
, there is
N\geq 1
such that
\sum_{n=1}^{N}g_{n}(x)>0
. By Proposition (2.16)
in
[
19
]
, p. 26, there is some
n\geq 1
and
n\geq N
such that
g_{n}(x)>0
, hence

|f_{n}(x)|\geq g_{n}(x)>0
. The converse inclusion follows trivially.
∎

Let
\mathcal{F}^{*}:=(X,F^{*})
be the Bishop space generated by the bounded functions
F^{*}
in
F
.

Theorem 6.2.4
.

(i)

If
\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
, then
\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
.

(ii)

If
\bm{o}(f)\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
, for every
f\in F
, then
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
.

(iii)

If
f\in F
, then
\bm{o}(f)=-\bm{\zeta}\big((-f)\wedge 0\big)
.

(iv)

\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F}^{*})=\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F}^{*})
.

Proof.

(i) By Proposition
6.1.5
(iv)
-\bm{o}(f)=\big([f\leq 0],[f>0]\big)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
,
for every
f\in F
, hence
-\bm{o}(-f)=\big([f\geq 0],[f<0]\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
too. Consequently

-\bm{o}(f)\cap-\bm{o}(-f)=\big([f\leq 0]\cap[f\geq 0],[f>0]\cup[f<0]\big)=\bm{\zeta}(f)\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F}).

If
P(\bm{B}):=\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, the above equality is the first step of the corresponding
inductive proof on
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
. The rest of the inductive proof is straightforward.

(ii) The hypothesis is the first step of the obvious inductive proof on
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
, which shows
that
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
. By (i) we get
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
.

(iii) We show that

\big([f>0],[f\leq 0]\big)=\big([(-f)\wedge 0\neq 0],[(-f)\wedge 0=0]\big).

First we show that
[f>0]\subseteq[(-f)\wedge 0\neq 0]
; if
f(x)>0
, then
-f(x)\wedge 0=-f(x)<0
.
For the converse inclusion, let
-f(x)\wedge 0\neq 0\Leftrightarrow-f(x)\wedge 0>0
or
-f(x)\wedge 0<0
.
Since
0\geq-f(x)\wedge 0
, the first option is impossible. If
-f(x)\wedge 0<0
, then
-f(x)<0
or

0<0
, hence
f(x)>0
.
Next we show that
[f\leq 0]=[(-f)\wedge 0=0]
; since
f(x)\leq 0\Leftrightarrow-f(x)\geq 0\Leftrightarrow-f(x)\wedge 0=0
(see
[
24
]
, p. 52), the equality follows.

(iv) Clearly,
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F}^{*})\subseteq\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
. By Proposition
6.2.2
(vi)
\bm{\zeta}(f)=\bm{\zeta}(|f|\wedge 1)
, where
|f|\wedge 1\in F^{*}
. Continuing with the obvious induction we
get
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F}^{*})
. By case (iii) and Proposition
6.2.2
(iv) we
get
\bm{o}(f)\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
, hence by case (ii) we conclude that
\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})=\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
.
Clearly,
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F}^{*})\subseteq\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})
. By Proposition
6.1.5
(vi)
\bm{o}(f)=\bm{o}((f\vee 0)\wedge 1)
, where
(f\vee 0)\wedge 1\in F^{*}
. Continuing with the obvious induction
we get
\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F})\subseteq\textnormal{{Borel}}(\mathcal{F}^{*})
.
∎

Either by definition, as in the proof of Proposition
6.1.7
, or by Theorem
6.2.4
(iii)
and Proposition
6.1.7
, if
h\in\mathrm{Mor}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
and
\bm{B}\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{G})
, then

h^{-1}(\bm{B})\in\textnormal{{Baire}}(\mathcal{F})
.
Suppose next that
\bm{A}
is strongly
F
-complemented i.e., there is
f\in F
such that

f\colon A^{1}\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{F}A^{0}
and
f(x)=1
, for every
x\in A^{1}
, and
f(y)=0
, for every
y\in A^{0}
.
If
g:=(f\vee 0)\wedge 1\in F
, then
0\leq g\leq 1
and
\forall_{x\in A^{1}}\forall_{y\in A^{0}}\big(g(x)=1\ \&\ g(y)=0\big)
. In
[
18
]
, p. 55, the following relation between complemented subsets is defined:

\bm{A}\leq\bm{B}:\Leftrightarrow A^{1}\subseteq B^{1}\ \ \&\ \ A^{0}\subseteq B^{0}.

If
\bm{A}
is strongly
F
-complemented, then
\bm{A}\leq\bm{o}(f)
. According to the classical
Urysohn lemma for
C(X)
-zero sets, the disjoint zero sets of a topological
space
X
are separated by some
f\in C(X)
(see
[
52
]
, p. 17). Next we show a constructive version
of this result, where disjointness is replaced by a stronger, but positively defined form of it.

Theorem 6.2.5

(Urysohn lemma for zero complemented subsets)
.

If
\bm{A}:=(A^{1},A^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{][_{F}}(X)
, then
\bm{A}
is strongly
F
-complemented if and only if

\exists_{f,g\in F}\exists_{c>0}\big(\bm{A}\leq\bm{\zeta}(f)\ \&\ -\bm{A}\leq\bm{\zeta}(g)\ \&\ |f|+|g|\geq c\big).

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Let
h\in F
such that
0\leq h\leq 1
,
A^{1}\subseteq[h=1]
and
A^{0}\subseteq[h=0]
.
We take
f:=1-h\in F,g:=h
and
c:=1
. First we show that
\bm{A}\leq\bm{\zeta}(f)
. If

x\in A^{1}
, then
h(x)=1
, and
f(x)=0
. If
y\in A^{0}
, then
h(y)=0
, hence
f(y)=1
and

y\in[f\neq 0]
. Next we show that
-\bm{A}\leq\bm{\zeta}(g)
. If
y\in A^{0}
, then
h(y)=0=g(y)
.
If
x\in A^{1}
, then
h(x)=1=g(y)
i.e.,
x\in[g\neq 0]
. If
x\in X
, then
1=|1-h(x)+h(x)|\leq|1-h(x)|+|h(x)|
.

(\Leftarrow)
Let
h:=1-\big(\frac{1}{c}|f|\wedge 1\big)\in F
. If
x\in A^{1}
, then
f(x)=0
, and
hence
h(x)=1
. If
y\in A^{0}
, then
g(y)=0
, hence
|f(y)|\geq c
, and consequently
h(y)=0
.
∎

The condition
(\mathrm{BS}_{3})
of a Bishop space
is crucial to the next proof.

Corollary 6.2.6
.

ILet
\bm{A}:=(A^{1},A^{0})\in\mathcal{P}^{][_{F}}(X)
and
f\in F
. If
f(\bm{A}):=\big(f(A^{1}),f(A^{0})\big)
is
strongly
{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
-complemented, then
\bm{A}
is strongly
F
-complemented.

Proof.

By the Urysohn lemma for zero complemented subsets there are
\phi,\theta\in{\mathrm{Bic}({\mathbb{R}})}
and
c>0
with

f(\bm{A})\leq\bm{\zeta}(\phi),-f(\bm{A})\leq\bm{\zeta}(\theta)
and
|\phi|+|\theta|\geq c
.
Consequently,
\bm{A}\leq\bm{\zeta}(\phi\circ f),-\bm{A}\leq\bm{\zeta}(\theta\circ f)
and
|\phi\circ f|+|\theta\circ f|\geq c
. Since by
(\mathrm{BS}_{3})
we have that
\phi\circ f\in F
and
\theta\circ f\in F
,
by the other implication of the Urysohn lemma for zero complemented subsets we conclude that
\bm{A}
is
strongly
F
-complemented.
∎

6.3
Measure and pre-measure spaces

There are two, quite different, notions of measure space in traditional Bishop-style constructive mathematics. The
first, which was introduced in
[
9
]
as part of
Bishop’s measure theory

(\mathrm{BMT})

(see Note
6.6.6
), is an abstraction of the measure function
A\mapsto\mu(A)
, where
A
is a
member of a family of complemented subsets of a locally compact metric space
X
. The use of complemented
subsets in order to overcome the difficulties
generated in measure theory by the use of negation and negatively defined concepts is one of Bishop’s great
conceptual achievements, while the use of the concept of a family of complemented subsets
is crucial to the predicative character of this notion of measure space
3
3

3

Myhill’s impredicative
interpretation in
[
80
]
of Bishop’s first definition is discussed in Note
6.6.8
.
. The
indexing required behind this first notion of measure space is evident in
[
9
]
, and sufficiently
stressed in
[
12
]
(see Note
6.6.7
).
The second notion of measure space, introduced in
[
18
]
and repeated in
[
19
]
as part of
the far more general
Bishop-Cheng measure theory

(\mathrm{BCMT})
, is highly impredicative, as the
necessary indexing for its predicative reformulation is missing. A lack of predicative concern is evident
also in the integration theory of
\mathrm{BCMT}
.
Next we define a predicative variation of the Bishop-Cheng notion of measure space using the predicative
conceptual ingredients of the initial Bishop notion of measure space. We also keep the operations of
complemented subsets introduced in
[
9
]
, and not the operations used in
[
18
]
and
[
19
]
.
Following Bishop’s views in
[
12
]
, we introduce the notion of pre-measure space, which is understood though,
in a way different from the classical term.

As in Definition
4.6.3
, if
\bm{\Lambda}(X):=(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1},\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
, the set
\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})
of complemented
subsets of
X
is the totality
I
, equipped with the equality

i=_{I}^{\Lambda(X)}j:\Leftrightarrow\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=_{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)
, for every

i,j\in I
. For simplicity we write
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i):=(\lambda_{0}^{1}(i),\lambda_{0}^{0}(i))
instead of
i

for an element of
\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})
.

Definition 6.3.1

(Measure space within
\mathrm{BST}
)
.

Let
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
be an inhabited set,
\bm{\Lambda}(X):=(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1};\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,\bm{X})
, and let
\mu\colon\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})\to[0,+\infty)
such that the following conditions hold:

(\mathrm{MS}_{1})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i,j\in I}\exists_{k,l\in I}\bigg(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\ \&\ \bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cap\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(l)\ \&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)+\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)\big)=\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\big)+\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(l)\big)\bigg).

(\mathrm{MS}_{2})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\bm{A}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},\bm{X})}\bigg[\exists_{k\in I}\bigg(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cap\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\bigg)\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \big(\exists_{l\in I}\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)-\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(l)\big)\ \&\ \mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)=\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\big)+\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(l)\big)\bigg].

(\mathrm{MS}_{3})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exists_{i\in I}\big(\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)>0.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{MS}_{4})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)}\bigg\{\forall_{\beta\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)}\bigg[\forall_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\bigg)\ \&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)>0\Rightarrow\exists_{x\in X}\bigg(x\in\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\lambda_{0}^{1}(\alpha(n))\bigg)\bigg]\bigg\}.

The triplet
\mathcal{M}:=(X,\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X}),\mu)
is called a measure space with
\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})
its set of integrable, or measurable sets, and
\mu
its measure.

With respect to condition
(\mathrm{MS}_{1})
, we do not say that the set
\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})
is closed
under the union or intersection of complemented subsets (as Bishop-Cheng do in their definition).
This amounts to the rather strong condition

\forall_{i,j\in I}\exists_{k,l\in I}\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j):=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\ \&\ \bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cap\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j):=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(l)\big)
.
The weaker condition
(\mathrm{MS}_{1})
states that the complemented subsets
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)
and
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cap\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)
“pseudo-belong” to
\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})
i.e.,
there are elements of it, which are equal to them in
\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
.
In contrast to the formulation of condition
(\mathrm{MS}_{2})
by Bishop and Cheng, we avoid quantification over
the class
\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}}(X)
, by quantifying over the set
\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},\bm{X})
. In our formulation of

(\mathrm{MS}_{4})
we quantify over
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)
, in order to avoid the use of some choice principle.
If we had written

\forall_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\exists_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=_{{\mathcal{P}^{\bm{\rrbracket}\bm{\llbracket}_{\neq}}}}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\bigg)

instead, we would need countable choice to express the limit to infinity of the terms

\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\big)
.
Next we define the notion of a pre-measure space, giving an explicit formulation of Bishop’s idea,
expressed in
[
12
]
, p. 67, and quoted in Note
6.6.7
, to formalise his
first definition of measure space, applied though, to Definition
6.3.1
.
The main idea is to define operations on
I
that correspond to the operations on complemented subsets,
and reformulate accordingly the clauses for the measure
\mu
. The fact that
\mu
is defined on
the index-set is already expressed in the definition of the set
\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X})
. The notion of a
pre-measure space provides us a method to generate measure spaces.

Definition 6.3.2

(Pre-measure space within
\mathrm{BST}
)
.

Let
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
be an inhabited set, and let
(I,=_{I})
be equipped with operations

\vee\colon I\times I\rightsquigarrow I,\wedge\colon I\times I\rightsquigarrow I
, and
\sim\colon I\rightsquigarrow I
.
If
i,j\in I
and
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\in I
, where
m\geq 1
, let
4
4

4

The operations
\bigvee_{n=1}^{m}i_{n}

and
\bigvee_{n=1}^{m}i_{n}
are actually recursively defined.

i\sim j:=i\wedge(\sim j)\ \ \&\ \ i\leq j:\Leftrightarrow i\wedge j=i,

\bigvee_{n=1}^{m}i_{n}:=i_{1}\vee\ldots\vee i_{m}\ \ \&\ \ \bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}i_{n}:=i_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge i_{m}.

Let
\bm{\Lambda}(X):=(\lambda_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\lambda_{1}^{1};\lambda_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\lambda_{1}^{0})\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,\bm{X})
, and
\mu\colon I\to[0,+\infty)
such that the following conditions hold:

(\mathrm{PMS}_{1})\ \ \forall_{i,j\in I}\bigg(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\vee j)\ \&\ \bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cap\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\wedge j)\ \&\ -\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\sim i)\ \&

\mu(i)+\mu(j)=\mu(i\vee j)+\mu(i\wedge j)\bigg).

(\mathrm{PMS}_{2})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\bm{A}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},\bm{X})}\bigg[\exists_{k\in I}\bigg(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\ \cap\ \bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\bigg)\ \Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)-\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\sim k)\ \&\ \mu(i)=\mu(k)+\mu(i\sim k)\bigg].\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PMS}_{3})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exists_{i\in I}\big(\mu(i)\big)>0.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PMS}_{4})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)}\bigg[\exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)\bigg)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)\bigg)>0\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Rightarrow\exists_{x\in X}\bigg(x\in\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\lambda_{0}^{1}(\alpha(n))\bigg)\bigg].

The triplet
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\Lambda}(X)):=(X,I,\mu)
is called
a pre-measure space, the function
\mu
a pre-measure,
and the index-set
I
a set of integrable, or measurable indices.

Corollary 6.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\Lambda}):=(X,I,\mu)
be a pre-measure space and
i,j\in I
.

(i)

The operations
\vee
,
\wedge
and
\sim
are functions.

(ii)

The triplet
(I,\vee,\wedge)
is a distributive lattice.

(iii)

\sim(\sim i)=_{I}i
.

(iv)

\sim(i\wedge j)=_{I}(\sim i)\vee(\sim j)
.

(v)

i\leq j\Leftrightarrow\ \sim j\leq\ \sim i
.

(vi)

i\leq j\Leftrightarrow\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\subseteq\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)
.

(vii)

\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)-\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\sim j)
.

Proof.

We show that
\vee
is a function, and for
\wedge
and
\sim
we proceed similarly.

\displaystyle i=i{{}^{\prime}}\ \&\ j=j{{}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\ \&\ \bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}})\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j{{}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\vee j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i{{}^{\prime}}\vee j{{}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow i\vee j=i{{}^{\prime}}\vee j{{}^{\prime}}.

(ii) The defining clauses of a distributive lattice
follow from the corresponding properties of complemented subsets for
\bm{A}\cap\bm{B}
and
\bm{A}\cup\bm{B}
,
from
(\mathrm{PMS}_{1})
, and from the fact that
\bm{\Lambda}(X)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,\bm{X})
. E.g., to show
i\vee j=j\vee i
,
we use the equalities
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\vee j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)\cup\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j\vee i)
. For the rest of the proof we proceed similarly.
∎

In the next example of a pre-measure space the index-set
I
is a Boolean algebra.

Proposition 6.3.4
.

Let
\big(X,=_{X},\neq_{X}^{{\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})}}\big)
be a set, and

\bm{\Delta}(X):=\big(\delta_{0}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{1,X},\delta_{1}^{1},\delta_{0}^{0},\mathcal{E}^{0,X},\delta_{1}^{0}\big)\in\textnormal{{Set}}(\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}),\bm{X})
the family of complemented detachable subsets of
X
, where
by Remark
4.6.9

\bm{\delta}_{0}(f):=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f),\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\big):=\big([f=0],[f=1]\big).

If
x_{0}\in X
and
\mu_{x_{0}}\colon\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})\rightsquigarrow[0,+\infty)

is defined by the rule

\mu_{x_{0}}(f):=f(x_{0});\ \ \ \ f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}),

then the triplet
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\Delta}(X)):=(X,\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}),\mu_{x_{0}})
is a pre-measure space.

Proof.

We define the maps
\vee,\wedge:\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})\times\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})\to\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
and
\sim\colon\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})\to\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
by

f\vee g:=f+g-fg,\ \ \ f\wedge g:=fg,\ \ \ \sim f:=1-f;\ \ \ \ f,g\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}),

where
1
also denotes the constant function on
X
with value
1
.
By definition of the union and intersection of complemented subsets we have that

\displaystyle\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)\cup\bm{\delta}_{0}(g)

\displaystyle:=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{1}(g),\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cap\delta_{0}^{0}(g)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{1}(g),\delta_{0}^{1}(1-f)\cap\delta_{0}^{1}(1-g)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f+g-fg),\delta_{0}^{1}((1-f)(1-g))\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f+g-fg),\delta_{0}^{1}(1-(f+g-fg))\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f+g-fg),\delta_{0}^{0}(f+g-fg)\big)

\displaystyle:=\bm{\delta}_{0}(f\vee g).

\displaystyle\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)\cap\bm{\delta}_{0}(g)

\displaystyle:=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap\delta_{0}^{1}(g),\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{0}(g)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap\delta_{0}^{1}(g),\delta_{0}^{1}(1-f)\cup\delta_{0}^{1}(1-g)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(fg),\delta_{0}^{1}((1-f)+(1-g)-(1-f)(1-g))\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(fg),\delta_{0}^{1}(1-fg)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(fg),\delta_{0}^{0}(fg)\big)

\displaystyle:=\bm{\delta}_{0}(f\wedge g).

Clearly,
\bm{\delta}_{0}(\sim f):=\bm{\delta}_{0}(1-f)=-\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)
.
Clearly, the operation
\mu_{x_{0}}
is a function. As

\mu_{x_{0}}(f)+\mu_{x_{0}}(g)=\mu_{x_{0}}(f+g-fg)+\mu_{x_{0}}(fg)\Leftrightarrow

f(x_{0})+g(x_{0})=f(x_{0})+g(x_{0})-f(x_{0})g(x_{0})+f(x_{0})g(x_{0}),

which is trivially the case,
(\mathrm{PMS}_{1})
follows.
Let
f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
and
\bm{B}:=(B^{1},B^{0})
a given complemented subset of
X
with

\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0):=\bm{B}
. If
g\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
such that

\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)\cap\bm{B}:=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{1},\delta_{0}^{1}(1-f)\cup B^{0}\big)=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(g),\delta_{0}^{1}(1-g)\big)\Leftrightarrow

\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{1}=\delta_{0}^{1}(g)\ \ \&\ \ \delta_{0}^{1}(1-f)\cup B^{0}=\delta_{0}^{1}(1-g),

\displaystyle\bm{\delta}_{0}(f(1-g))

\displaystyle:=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f(1-g)),\ \delta_{0}^{0}(f(1-g))\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap\delta_{0}^{1}(1-g),\ \delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{1}(1-g)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap[\delta_{0}^{1}(1-f)\cup B^{0}],\ \delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup[\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{1}]\big)

\displaystyle=\big([\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap\delta_{0}^{1}(1-f)]\cup[\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{0}],\ [\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap[\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup B^{1}]\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\emptyset\cup[\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{0}],\ X\cap[\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup B^{1}]\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{0},\ \delta_{0}^{0}(f)\cup B^{1}\big)

\displaystyle:=\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)-\bm{B}.

To complete the proof of
(\mathrm{PMS}_{2})
, we need to show

\mu_{x_{0}}(f)=\mu_{x_{0}}(g)+\mu_{x_{0}}(f(1-g))\Leftrightarrow f(x_{0})=g(x_{0})+f(x_{0})(1-g(x_{0})).

If
g(x_{0})=0
, the equality holds trivially. If
g(x_{0})=1
, and since
\delta_{0}^{1}(g)=\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cap B^{1}
,
we also have that
f(x_{0})=1
, and the required equality holds.
As
\mu_{x_{0}}(1)=1>0
,
(\mathrm{PMS}_{3})
follows. For the proof of
(\mathrm{PMS}_{4})
we fix
\alpha:{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
,
and we suppose that

\displaystyle\exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu_{x_{0}}\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}\bigg)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu_{x_{0}}\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}\bigg)>0\ \Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}\bigg)(x_{0})\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}\bigg)(x_{0})>0\ \Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\prod_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}(x_{0})\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\prod_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}(x_{0})>0.

Finally, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\prod_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}(x_{0})>0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\prod_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}(x_{0})=1

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\exists_{m_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\forall_{m\geq m_{0}}\bigg(\prod_{n=0}^{m}\alpha_{n}(x_{0})=1\bigg)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(\alpha_{n}(x_{0})=1\big)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow x_{0}\in\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\delta_{0}^{1}(\alpha_{n}).\qed

Proposition 6.3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\Lambda}(X)):=(X,I,\mu)
be a pre-measure space. If
\mu^{*}\colon\bm{\lambda}_{0}I\rightsquigarrow[0,+\infty)
,
where
\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)):=\mu(i),
for every
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\in\bm{\lambda}_{0}I
, then

\mathcal{M}:=(X,\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X}),\mu^{*})
is a measure space.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.6.4

\mu^{*}
is a function.
For the proof of
(\mathrm{MS}_{1})
we fix
i,j\in I
and we take
k:=i\vee j
and
l:=i\wedge j
. From

(\mathrm{PMS}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{PMS}_{4})
we get

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i))+\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j))

\displaystyle:=\mu(i)+\mu(j)

\displaystyle=\mu(i\vee j)+\mu(i\wedge j)

\displaystyle:=\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\vee j))+\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\wedge j)).

For the proof of
(\mathrm{MS}_{2})
we fix
i\in I
and
\bm{A}\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},\bm{X})
with
\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0):=\bm{B}
.
If
\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\ \cap\ \bm{B}=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)
, for some
k\in I
, we take
l:=i\sim k\in I
and
by
(\mathrm{PMS}_{2})

\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)):=\mu_{0}(i)=\mu(k)+\mu(i\sim k):=\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k))+\mu^{*}(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i\sim k))
. Condition
(\mathrm{MS}_{3})
follows immediately from
(\mathrm{PMS}_{3})
.
For the proof of
(\mathrm{MS}_{4})
, we fix
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)
, and we suppose that

\forall_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\bigg)\ \&\ \exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu^{*}\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu^{*}\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)>0\Leftrightarrow

\forall_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigg(\bigcap_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\bigg)\ \&\ \exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\beta(m)\big)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\beta(m)\big)>0.

If
m\geq 1
, by
(\mathrm{PMS}_{1})
we have that

\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))=\bigcap_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=\bm{\lambda}_{0}\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)\bigg),

hence, since
\bm{\lambda}_{0}
is a set of complemented subsets,
\beta(m)=\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)
,
and consequently

\mu\big(\beta(m)\big)=\mu\big(\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)\big)
. Hence

\exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)\bigg)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\bigg(\bigwedge_{n=1}^{m}\alpha(n)\bigg)>0.

By
(\mathrm{PMS}_{4})
we conclude that there is some
x\in X
such that

x\in\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\lambda_{0}^{1}(\alpha(n))
.
∎

Corollary 6.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\bm{\Delta}(X)):=(X,\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2}),\mu_{x_{0}})
be the pre-measure space of complemented
detachable subsets of
X
. If
\mu_{x_{0}}^{*}:\delta_{0}\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})(\bm{X})\rightsquigarrow[0,+\infty)
is
defined by
\mu_{x_{0}}^{*}\big(\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)\big):=\mu_{x_{0}}(f):=f(x_{0}),
for every

\delta_{0}(f)\in\delta_{0}\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})(\bm{X})
, then

\mathcal{M}(X):=(X,\delta_{0}\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})(\bm{X}),\mu^{*}_{x_{0}})
is a measure space.

Next we formulate in our framework the definition of a complete measure space given by Bishop and
Cheng
5
5

5

In the definition of Bishop and Cheng the symbol of definitional equality
l:=\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)
is used, but as this a
convergence condition, one can use the equality of
{\mathbb{R}}
for the same purpose
(see Note
6.6.5
).

Definition 6.3.7
.

A measure space
\mathcal{M}:=(X,\lambda_{0}I(\bm{X}),\mu)
is called complete,
if the following conditions hold:

(\mathrm{CM}_{1})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\bm{A}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},\bm{X})}\bigg(\lambda_{0}^{1}(i)\subseteq\alpha_{0}^{1}(0)\ \&\ \lambda_{0}^{0}(i)\subseteq\alpha_{0}^{0}(0)\Rightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\big(\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\big)\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{CM}_{2})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)}\bigg\{\forall_{\beta\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},I)}\forall_{l\in[0,+\infty]}\bigg[\forall_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\bigg)\ \&\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\beta(m))\big)=l\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Rightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\bigg(\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bm{\lambda}_{0}(\alpha(n))=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\ \&\ \mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\big)=l\bigg)\bigg]\bigg\}.

(\mathrm{CM}_{3})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i,j\in I}\forall_{\bm{A}(X)\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(\mathbb{1},\bm{X})}\bigg(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\subseteq\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)\subseteq\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)\ \&\ \mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(i)\big)=\mu\big(\bm{\lambda}_{0}(j)\big)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Rightarrow\exists_{k\in I}\big(\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0)=\bm{\lambda}_{0}(k)\big)\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

Regarding the completeness conditions and the space
\mathcal{M}(X)
, we show the following.

Proposition 6.3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(X):=(X,\delta_{0}\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})(\bm{X}),\mu^{*}_{x_{0}})
be the measure space of
complemented detachable subsets of
X
.

(i)

\mathcal{M}(X)
satisfies condition
(\mathrm{CM}_{1})
.

(ii)

The limited principle of omniscience
(\mathrm{LPO})
implies that
\mathcal{M}(X)
satisfies condition
(\mathrm{CM}_{2})
.

(iii)

In general,
\mathcal{M}(X)
does not satisfy condition
(\mathrm{CM}_{3})
.

Proof.

(i) Let
f\in\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
, let
\bm{B}:=(B^{1},B^{0})
be a given complemented subset of

X
with
\bm{\alpha}_{0}(0):=\bm{B}
, and let
\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\subseteq B^{1}
and
\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\subseteq B^{0}
. Since
X=\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\cup\delta_{0}^{0}(f)\subseteq(B^{1}\cup B^{0})\subseteq X
,
we get
B^{1}\cup B^{0}=X
, and hence
\bm{B}=\bm{\delta}_{0}(\chi_{\bm{B}})
.

(ii) Let
\alpha,\beta:{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{F}(X,\mathbb{2})
, and
l\in(0,+\infty)
such that

\forall_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigg(\bigcup_{n=1}^{m}\bm{\delta}_{0}(\alpha_{n})=\bm{\delta}_{0}(\beta_{m})\bigg)\ \&\ \exists\lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\beta_{m}(x_{0})\ \&\ \lim_{{m\to+\infty}}\beta_{m}(x_{0})=l.

The last conjunct is equivalent to
\exists_{m_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\forall_{m\geq m_{0}}\big(\beta_{m}(x_{0})=l\big)
, and
since
\beta_{m}(x_{0})\in\mathbb{2}
, we get
l\in\mathbb{2}
. For every
x\in X
the sequence
n\mapsto\alpha_{n}(x)
is in
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}},\mathbb{2})
, hence by
(\mathrm{LPO})
we define the function
f
from
X
to
\mathbb{2}
by the rule

f(x):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{, $\exists_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(\alpha_{n}(x)=1\big)$}\\
0&\mbox{, $\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(\alpha_{n}(x)=0\big)$.}\end{array}\right.

By the definition of interior union and intersection it is immediate to show that

\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bm{\delta}_{0}(\alpha_{n})=\bm{\delta}_{0}(f)\Leftrightarrow\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\delta_{0}^{1}(\alpha_{n})=\delta_{0}^{1}(f)\ \&\ \bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\delta_{0}^{0}(\alpha_{n})=\delta_{0}^{0}(f).

It remains to show that
f(x_{0})=l
. If
l=0
, then

\exists_{m_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\forall_{m\geq m_{0}}\big(\beta_{m}(x_{0})=0\big)
, which implies that

\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(\alpha_{n}(x_{0})=0\big):\Leftrightarrow f(x_{0}):=0
. If
l=1
, then

\exists_{m_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}}\forall_{m\geq m_{0}}\big(\beta_{m}(x_{0})=1\big)
, which implies that

\exists_{n\in\{1,\ldots,m_{0}\}}\big(\alpha_{n}(x_{0})=1\big)\Rightarrow f(x_{0}):=1
.

(iii) If
X:=\mathbb{3}
, let
f:\mathbb{3}\to\mathbb{2}
be defined by
f(0):=1,f(1):=0=:f(2)
and
let
g\colon\mathbb{3}\to\mathbb{2}
be the constant function with value
1
. If

\bm{B}:=\big(\{0\},\{1\}\big)
, then
\delta_{0}^{1}(f):=\{0\}=B^{1}\subseteq\delta_{0}^{1}(g)
and
\emptyset=\delta_{0}^{0}(g)\subseteq B^{0}\subseteq\delta_{0}^{0}(f)
. If
x_{0}:=0
,
then
\mu_{0}(f)=\mu_{0}(g)
, but
\bm{B}
cannot “pseudo-belong” to
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{3})
, since
B^{1}\cup B^{0}

is a proper subset of
\mathbb{3}
.
∎

Definition 6.4.1
.

If
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})

a

A
X
{\mathbb{R}}
.
i_{A}^{X}
f_{A}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\ \

Definition 6.4.3
.

Let
\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}}):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},\mathcal{P}^{{\mathbb{R}}})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X,{\mathbb{R}})

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\colon A\to{\mathbb{R}},\ \ \ \ \bigg(\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg)(a):=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg);\ \ \ \ a\in A,

under the assumption that the series on the right converge in

In the special case

\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n)),id_{{\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))}})\subseteq\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))

\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{{\bigcap}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\colon\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))\to{\mathbb{R}},\ \ \ \ \bigg(\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{{\bigcap}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg)(\Phi):=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\big(\Phi_{n}\big);\ \ \ \ \Phi\in\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n)),

under the assumption on the convergence of the corresponding series.

Proposition 6.4.4
.

If in Definition
6.4.3
the partial functions
f_{\kappa(n)}:=\big(\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n)),\mathcal{E}_{\kappa(n)}^{X},f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
are strongly extensional,
for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
, then the real-valued partial function
A
\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))
X
{\mathbb{R}}
i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}
e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\circ\kappa}
\ \ \ \ \ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}

f_{A}:=\bigg(A,\ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\circ\kappa}\circ i_{A}^{{\bigcap}},\ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg)

is strongly extensional.

Proof.

Let
a,a{{}^{\prime}}\in A
such that

\bigg(\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg)(a):=l\neq_{{\mathbb{R}}}l{{}^{\prime}}:=\bigg(\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg)(a{{}^{\prime}}).

There is
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
such that, if
\varepsilon:=|l-l{{}^{\prime}}|>0
, then

\displaystyle\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\bigg|l-\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg|+\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)-\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ +\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)-l{{}^{\prime}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)-\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg|+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle<\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)-\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigg[f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)-f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg]\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bigg|f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)-f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg|.

By the property of positive real numbers
x+y>0\Rightarrow[x>0\vee y>0
(see
[
20
]
, p. 13), then

0<\bigg|f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)-f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)\bigg|,

for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
with
1\leq n\leq N
. Since
f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}
is strongly extensional,
by Definition
6.4.1

\mathcal{E}_{\kappa(1)}^{X}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{1}\bigg)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{\kappa(n)}^{X}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a)\big]_{n}\bigg)\neq_{X}\mathcal{E}_{\kappa(n)}^{X}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{n}\bigg)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{\kappa(1)}^{X}\bigg(\big[i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}(a{{}^{\prime}})\big]_{1}\bigg),

which is the required canonical equality of
a,a{{}^{\prime}}
in
A
as a subset of
X
.
∎

Clearly, we have that (see Definition
2.7.1
)
A
\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))
X
\ {\mathbb{R}}
i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}
\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}
\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{{\bigcap}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\ \
\ {e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\circ\kappa}\circ i_{A}^{{\bigcap}}}
{e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\circ\kappa}}

\bigg(A,\ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\circ\kappa}\circ i_{A}^{{\bigcap}},\ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{A}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg)\leq\bigg(\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n)),\ e_{{\bigcap}}^{\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\circ\kappa},\ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{{\bigcap}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg).

6.5
Integration and pre-integration spaces

Next we reformulate predicatively the Bishop-Cheng definition of an integration space
(see Note
6.6.10
to compare it with the original definition).

Definition 6.5.1

(Integration space within
\mathrm{BST}
)
.

Let
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
be an inhabited set,
\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}}):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},\mathcal{P}^{{\mathbb{R}}})\in\textnormal{{Fam}}(I,X,{\mathbb{R}})
, such that
f_{i}:=\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X},f_{i}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is strongly extensional, for every
i\in I
,
let
\lambda_{0}I(X,{\mathbb{R}})
be the totality
I
, equipped with the equality
i=_{\lambda_{0}I(X,{\mathbb{R}})}j:\Leftrightarrow f_{i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}
, for every
i\in I
, and let a mapping

\int\colon\lambda_{0}I(X,{\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathbb{R}},\ \ \ \ f_{i}\mapsto\int f_{i};\ \ \ \ i\in I,

such that the following conditions hold:

(\mathrm{IS}_{1})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{a\in{\mathbb{R}}}\exists_{j\in I}\bigg(af_{i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\ \ \&\ \ \int f_{j}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}a\int f_{i}\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{IS}_{2})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i,j\in I}\exists_{k\in I}\bigg(f_{i}+f_{j}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{k}\ \ \&\ \ \int f_{k}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int f_{i}\ +\int f_{j}\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{IS}_{3})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\exists_{j\in I}\big(|f_{i}|=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\big).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{IS}_{4})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\exists_{j\in I}\big(f_{i}\wedge 1=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\big).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{IS}_{5})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\kappa\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)}\bigg\{\bigg[\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\int f_{\kappa(n)}\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\int f_{\kappa(n)}<\int f_{i}\bigg]\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\exists_{(\Phi,u)\ \in\ \big(\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))\big)\ \cap\ \lambda_{0}(i)}\bigg(\bigg(\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{{\bigcap}}f_{\kappa(n)}\bigg)(\Phi):=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n})<f_{i}^{{\mathbb{R}}}(u).\bigg)\bigg\}

(\mathrm{IS}_{6})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exists_{i\in I}\bigg(\int f_{i}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}1\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{IS}_{7})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)}\bigg(\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\bigg(n\bigg(\frac{1}{n}f_{i}\ \wedge\ 1\bigg)=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{\alpha(n)}\bigg)\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int f_{\alpha(n)}\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\ \lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int f_{\alpha(n)}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int f_{i}\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{IS}_{8})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)}\bigg(\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\bigg(\frac{1}{n}\big(n|f_{i}|\ \wedge\ 1\big)=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{\alpha(n)}\bigg)\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int f_{\alpha(n)}\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\ \lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int f_{\alpha(n)}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}0\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

We call the triplet
\mathcal{L}:=(X,\lambda_{0}I(X,{\mathbb{R}}),\int)
an integration space.

In the formulation of
(\mathrm{IS}_{5})
we have that

\bigg(\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))\bigg)\cap\lambda_{0}(i):=\bigg\{(\Phi,u)\in\bigg(\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))\bigg)\times\lambda_{0}(i)\ {{\mid}}\ \mathcal{E}^{X}_{\kappa(1)}(\Phi)=_{X}\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}(u)\bigg\},

\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))
X
\lambda_{0}(i)
{\mathbb{R}}
.
\mathcal{E}_{\kappa(n)}^{X}
\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X}
f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\ \
\ f_{i}^{{\mathbb{R}}}

If, for every
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, such that
a>0
, and every
i\in I
, we define

f_{i}\ \wedge\ a:=a\bigg(\frac{1}{a}f_{i}\ \wedge\ 1\bigg),

the formulation of
(\mathrm{IS}_{7})
and
(\mathrm{IS}_{8})
becomes, respectively,

\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)}\bigg(\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\big(f_{i}\ \wedge\ n=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{\alpha(n)}\big)\Rightarrow\lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int f_{\alpha(n)}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int f_{i}\bigg),

\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)}\bigg(\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\bigg(|f_{i}|\ \wedge\ \frac{1}{n}\bigg)=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{\alpha(n)}\bigg)\Rightarrow\lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int f_{\alpha(n)}=_{{\mathbb{R}}}0\bigg),

where, for simplicity, we skip to mention he existence of the corresponding limits in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
We also quantify over
\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)
, in order to avoid the use of countable choice. If we had written
in its premise the formula
\forall_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\exists_{j\in I}\big(f_{i}\ \wedge\ n=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\big)
,
we would need countable choice (
{\mathbb{N}}{-}I
) to generate a sequence in
I
to describe the limit of the
corresponding integrals. Moreover, by
(\mathrm{IS}_{1})
,
(\mathrm{IS}_{3})
and
(\mathrm{IS}_{4})
, and the definition of
f\wedge a

above we get
\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\exists_{j\in I}\big(f_{i}\wedge a=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\big)
.

Definition 6.5.2

(Pre-integration space within
\mathrm{BST}
)
.

Let
(X,=_{X},\neq_{X})
be an inhabited set, and let the set
(I,=_{I})
be equipped with
operations
\cdot_{a}\colon I\rightsquigarrow I
, for every
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
+\colon I\times I\rightsquigarrow I
,
|.|\colon I\rightsquigarrow I
,
and
\wedge_{1}\colon I\rightsquigarrow I
, where

\cdot_{a}(i):=a\cdot i,\ \ \ \ +(i,j):=i+j,\ \ \ \ |.|(i):=|i|;\ \ \ \ i\in I,\ a\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Let also the operation
\wedge_{a}\colon I\rightsquigarrow I
, defined by the previous operations with the rule

\wedge_{a}:=\cdot_{a}\circ\wedge_{1}\circ\cdot_{a^{-1}};\ \ \ \ a\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \&\ a>0.

Let
\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}}):=(\lambda_{0},\mathcal{E}^{X},\lambda_{1},\mathcal{P}^{{\mathbb{R}}})\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X,{\mathbb{R}})
i.e.,

f_{i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\Rightarrow i=_{I}j
, for every
i,j\in I
, and
f_{i}:=\big(\lambda_{0}(i),\mathcal{E}_{i}^{X},f_{i}^{{\mathbb{R}}}\big)
is strongly extensional, for every
i\in I
. Let also a mapping

\int\colon I\to{\mathbb{R}},\ \ \ \ i\mapsto\int i;\ \ \ \ i\in I,

such that the following conditions hold:

(\mathrm{PIS}_{1})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{a\in{\mathbb{R}}}\bigg(af_{i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{a\cdot i}\ \ \&\ \ \int a\cdot i=_{{\mathbb{R}}}a\int i\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PIS}_{2})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i,j\in I}\bigg(f_{i}+f_{j}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{i+j}\ \ \&\ \ \int(i+j)=_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int i\ +\int j\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PIS}_{3})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\big(|f_{i}|=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{|i|}\big).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PIS}_{4})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\big(f_{i}\wedge 1=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{\wedge_{1}(i)}\big).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PIS}_{5})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\forall_{\kappa\in\mathbb{F}({\mathbb{N}}^{+},I)}\bigg\{\bigg[\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\int\kappa(n)\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\int\kappa(n)<\int i\bigg]\Rightarrow\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\exists_{(\Phi,u)\ \in\ \big(\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}\lambda_{0}(\kappa(n))\big)\ \cap\ \lambda_{0}(i)}\bigg(\bigg(\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}^{{\bigcap}}f_{\kappa(n)}\bigg)(\Phi):=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\

\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}f_{\kappa(n)}^{{\mathbb{R}}}(\Phi_{n})<f_{i}^{{\mathbb{R}}}(u).\bigg)\bigg\}

(\mathrm{PIS}_{6})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \exists_{i\in I}\bigg(\int i=_{{\mathbb{R}}}1\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PIS}_{7})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\bigg(\lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int\wedge_{n}(i)\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\ \lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int\wedge_{n}(i)=_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int i\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(\mathrm{PIS}_{8})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall_{i\in I}\bigg(\lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int\wedge_{\frac{1}{n}}(|i|)\in{\mathbb{R}}\ \ \&\ \lim_{{n\longrightarrow+\infty}}\int\wedge_{\frac{1}{n}}(|i|)=_{{\mathbb{R}}}0\bigg).\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

We call the triplet
\mathcal{L}_{0}:=(X,I,\int)
a pre-integration space.

All the operations on
I
defined above are functions. E.g., since
\Lambda(X,{\mathbb{R}})\in\textnormal{{Set}}(I,X,{\mathbb{R}})
,

i=_{I}i{{}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow f_{i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{i{{}^{\prime}}}\Rightarrow af_{i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}af_{i{{}^{\prime}}}\Rightarrow f_{a\cdot i}=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{a{{}^{\prime}}\cdot i}\Rightarrow a\cdot i=_{I}a{{}^{\prime}}\cdot i.

It is immediate to see that a pre-integration space induces an integration space, if

\forall_{i\in I}\forall_{a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}}\big(f_{i}\ \wedge\ a=_{\mathcal{F}(X)}f_{j}\Rightarrow\wedge_{a}(i)=_{I}j\big),

and hence
(\mathrm{PIS}_{7})
and
(\mathrm{PIS}_{8})
imply
(\mathrm{IS}_{7})
and
(\mathrm{IS}_{8})
, respectively, with the integral

\int^{*}f_{i}:=\int i;\ \ \ \ i\in I.

The notion of a pre-integration space is simpler than that of an integration space, and also closer
to the Bishop-Cheng notion of an integration space. One could say that a pre-integration space is the
“right” notion of integration space within
\mathrm{BST}
. In
[
18
]
, p. 52 Bishop and Cheng formulate
the non-trivial theorem that a measure space induces the integration space of the corresponding simple
functions (see also
[
19
]
, p. 285). In
[
131
]
Zeuner interpreting the various constructions
of Bishop and Cheng into the framework of pre-measure and pre-integration
spaces

If

\int_{\mu}(n,u):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\mu(i_{k});\ \ \ \ u:=\big((a_{1},i_{1}),\ldots,(a_{n},i_{n})\big).
